# Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ (Chapter 28) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Narrative of the Oppressed and the Mirage of Tyranny**

Al-Qaṣaṣ, "The Stories," is the twenty-eighth chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 88 verses of late Makkan revelation. It is the final and most detailed of the three *Ṭā-Sīn-Mīm* chapters, taking its title from verse 25, where Moses arrives exhausted at the wells of Midian and recounts *al-qaṣaṣ* (the narrative of his life) to the venerable elder (traditionally identified with Shuʿayb or a righteous Midianite patriarch), who consoles him with the words: *“Do not fear; you have escaped from the wrongdoing people.”* Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ is particularly renowned in Islamic history for verse 85, traditionally revealed at the station of Juḥfah during the Prophet's dangerous, sorrowful emigration (*Hijrah*) from Makkah to Madinah: *“Indeed, He who has mandated upon you the Qur'an will surely return you to an appointment / place of return (*maʿād*)”*—a divine guarantee that he would return victorious to the city from which he was being driven. The sūrah provides the most emotionally intimate, biographical account of the infancy, youth, exile, and prophetic commissioning of Moses found in the entire sacred text, framed as an explicit, living mirror for the persecuted Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ and his companions.

**The Arrogance of Oligarchs and the Consolation of the Exiled**

The historical canvas of al-Qaṣaṣ is defined by the absolute asymmetrical confrontation between imperial tyranny and vulnerable faith. In Makkah, a tiny band of disenfranchised believers was surrounded by the arrogant Qurayshi oligarchy, who boasted of their wealth, mercantile networks, and military power, threatening the Muslims with extermination. Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ opens with a revolutionary divine charter in verses 4–5: Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and divided his people into factions, slaughtering their male infants and keeping their women alive; yet the divine decree states: *“And We wished to confer favor upon those who were oppressed in the land, and make them leaders, and make them the inheritors, and establish them in the land, and show Pharaoh and Hāmān and their soldiers through them that which they feared.”* This was a thunderous reassurance to the early Muslims: the God who brought down the greatest superpower of the ancient world through a helpless infant floating in a wooden basket upon the Nile was the very same God watching over the believers in the rocky alleys of Makkah.

**The Epic Biography of Moses and the Fall of Qārūn**

The architecture of Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ is built around two monumental historical portraits: the political tyranny of Pharaoh versus the divine empowerment of Moses, and the financial hubris of Korah (*Qārūn*) versus the spiritual wealth of the righteous. The first movement (1–43) delivers the gripping, cinematic narrative of Moses: Pharaoh's murderous decree, the desperate mother of Moses inspired to cast her infant into the Nile, the baby fetched by the family of Pharaoh, the intervention of Moses' sister watching from afar, Moses refusing all wet-nurses until his own mother's breast is restored to him *“so that her eye might be comforted and she might not grieve and that she might know that the promise of Allah is true”* (28:13). It traces his youth: the accidental manslaughter of the Egyptian while defending an Israelite, the secret warning from a man running from the furthest end of the city that the council was plotting his execution, Moses fleeing in terror across the desert to Midian, watering the flocks for the two modest daughters of the elderly sage, being hired for eight or ten years of service, traveling with his family in the cold, seeing the divine fire at the side of Mount Ṭūr, receiving the staff and radiant hand, returning to confront Pharaoh, and Pharaoh's minister Hāmān ordered to build a lofty brick tower to view the God of Moses, culminating in the drowning of the tyrant and his armies cast into the sea like refuse. The second movement (44–75) turns to the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ, confirming that he was not present on the western slope of Sinai when the decree was given, nor among the people of Midian, but this revelation is a direct mercy from his Lord. It rebukes the pagan excuses that following guidance would cause them to be snatched from their land, and presents the tragic narrative of Qārūn (76–84): an Israelite possessed of such vast treasures that the mere keys to his vaults weighed down a band of strong men, who boasted that his riches were acquired through his own scientific intellect (*“I was only given it because of knowledge I have,”* 28:78). The sūrah describes the catastrophic climax: the earth splitting open and swallowing Qārūn, his palatial estate, and his gold into the subterranean void, leaving the materialistic onlookers who had previously envied him to reflect with horror on their folly. The epilogue (85–88) delivers the promise of return to Makkah, commands the Prophet never to be an assistant to the disbelievers, and concludes with the immortal metaphysical declaration of verse 88: *“Everything will perish except His Face. To Him belongs the judgment, and to Him you will be returned.”*

**The Ultimate Law of Historical Inversion**

The foundational thesis of Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ is the divine principle of historical inversion: that worldly power divorced from moral righteousness contains the seeds of its own destruction, while vulnerable faith nurtured in patience is destined to inherit the earth. Pharaoh tried to murder all male infants to prevent a savior from rising, yet God made Pharaoh pay for that savior's milk and raise him inside his own royal bedchambers. Qārūn imagined his financial genius made him immortal, yet the very earth from which he extracted his gold opened up to swallow him whole. Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ teaches that the only reality that survives the wreck of time and empires is that which is attached to the Eternal Face of God (*kullu shay'in hālikun illā wajhah*).

---

## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:1
> Ṭâ-Sĩn-Mĩm

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters That Halt the Reading**

The chapter opens with three letters pronounced one by one: Ṭā, Sīn, Mīm. Twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an begin with such letter combinations — the ḥurūf muqaṭṭaʿāt, the "disjointed" or separated letters, so called because each letter is recited in isolation, name by name, rather than blended into a word. The reciter who reaches this verse does not read a word; he spells. The pause built into that spelling is part of the text's rhetoric, because every human book a seventh-century Arab knew began by introducing its author or its subject, and this Book begins by asking to be sounded letter by letter before it says anything at all. Whatever the letters mean, their effect is immediate: the listener is told at the threshold that what follows is not arranged the way human speech is arranged, and that its authority will not rest on the conventions of oratory. Fourteen letters of the Arabic alphabet appear across these openings, and the commentators observed that a Book revealed in plain Arabic (26:195) should begin with bare letters of that same Arabic — a sign, several of them argued, that the challenge of the Qur'an was issued from inside the language, not from outside it.

**The Same Three Letters Open Two Moses Chapters**

Ṭā-Sīn-Mīm opens this sūrah and also Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ (26:1), and the pairing deserves attention because the two chapters are a matched pair by theme. Chapter 26 carries the longest sustained account of Moses' confrontation with Pharaoh before the assembled court, complete with the contest with the sorcerers and the exodus; chapter 28 carries the arc that chapter 26 leaves off — the birth in a genocidal Egypt, the killing in the street, the flight to Madyan, and the commission at the burning bush. The shared letters function like a series title. Sūrah al-Naml, which falls between them, opens with Ṭā-Sīn alone and the words “Ṭâ-Sĩn. These are the verses of the Quran; the clear Book.” (27:1). The letters therefore do not float free of context: they recur where the Qur'an is about to unfold its grandest continuous narratives, and the earliest listeners could have learned to hear them as a signal that a prophetic story was about to begin.

**What the Commentators Proposed, and Why None Settled It**

Three families of explanation stand in the tradition. The first holds that the meaning belongs to God alone, and that the earliest generations left no agreed reading — their disagreement was preserved rather than resolved, which is itself information. The second treats the letters as names pointing to the sūrah that follows: to God, to Moses, or to the chapter's own themes. The third reads them as an oath, on the pattern of the Qur'an's frequent swearing by created things. Each position has a ground. The first rests on the companions' own caution; a reading invented later would carry less weight than their silence. The second rests on the pattern that the letters are immediately followed by a sentence about the Book or the revelation, as in the sūrah of the Ant, so the letters may belong to that announcement. The third rests on Arabic rhetorical habit, in which an oath sharpens attention before a grave statement. The first remains the strongest, because it alone refuses to add to the text; the other two explain the letters by supply, and their supplies differ from one another, which is why the disagreement survived fourteen centuries.

**Letters Calibrated for a Weak Audience**

Al-Qaṣaṣ is the twenty-eighth chapter of the Qur'an, Makkan in origin, and it runs to eighty-eight verses — facts that place this opening among the earliest revelations, delivered when the believers were a persecuted handful without power, sanctuary, or numbers. An audience in that position heard an opening no human could mimic: letters of their own alphabet, arranged by no grammar they knew, issued as the preface to a promise that their oppressors would fall. The disjointed letters made the Qur'an's foreignness and its familiarity hold together in one breath. The claim was not that the Qur'an spoke an alien tongue needing translation, but that the Arabs' own letters, separated from their service to ordinary words, could still defeat the masters of eloquence. For a community whose whole case rested on the claim that this Book came from the One who made the alphabet itself, the opening was the claim set to music before the argument began.

**Form, Credential, Content: The Order of the First Three Verses**

The sequence of the sūrah's first three verses is itself an argument. Verse 1 gives form without content — letters, and nothing else. Verse 2 gives credential — these are the verses of the clear Book. Verse 3 gives content — the story of Moses and Pharaoh, announced before it is told. The order teaches the audience how to receive what follows: first the sign that this speech is unlike other speech, then the claim about its clarity, then the narrative that will demonstrate both. No human composer opens a romance, a chronicle, or a sermon this way, because no human author needs to certify himself before his own audience — an Arab poet named his tribe and his lineage; a historian named his informants. This Book opens with letters no grammar owns, then with a sentence that dares the reader to find it unclear, and only then begins to speak. The arrangement also front-loads the two assurances the third verse's audience needed most: that what they hear is unique in form, and that what it tells is true. A congregation of the weak, listening to a promise that the strongest court of the age would fall, required exactly those two certainties before the story started, and the sūrah spends its first two verses on nothing else. The letters, the credential, and the narrative are one instrument, and the chapter never lets them separate — its story will read, from the first scene, as the demonstration of the clarity announced here.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:2
> These are the verses of the clear Book

**Expanded Commentary**

**Verses That Are Also Signs**

The word translated "verses" is āyāt, and the Qur'an deliberately stretches that word across two registers. An āyah is a sign — the alternation of night and day is called an āyah, so is the creation of the heavens, so is a miracle worked through a prophet — and the recited units of the Book itself carry the same name. By calling its own verses āyāt, the Qur'an puts the words a believer recites in the same category as the cosmos: both are evidence, both demand a verdict, and neither can be treated as decoration. A reader who accepts the rising of the sun as meaningful but the recited verse as literature has already failed the category. This chapter will go on to use āyah in the narrow sense as well, for the proofs Moses will carry into Egypt; the word's range is the argument. Revelation and creation issue from one Author, so their tokens bear one name.

**What the Clarity Claims**

"Clear" translates mubīn, an active word: the Book makes things plain, and it does so in more than one direction. The classical commentators enumerated the senses in which it is clear — in its message, in the blessings it carries, in the goodness it contains — and the enumeration matters because it blocks a lazy reading. Clarity here is not simplicity of style; the Qur'an nowhere promises that its every sentence will be equally transparent to every reader. The claim is that the Book's core — what God requires, what follows after death, how He has dealt with those before — is stated without riddle, and that anyone who misses it misses it against the evidence. A book that is clear puts the burden of confusion on the reader's will. That is why the Qur'an can describe itself as clear and still report that people turn away from it: the turning is explained as refusal, never as obscurity.

**A Formula That Opens the Narrative Chapters**

The sentence is a formula, and its placements are the point. Sūrah Yūsuf opens: “Alif-Lãm-Ra. These are the verses of the clear Book.” (12:1). Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ opens its second verse with the same words (26:2). Both chapters are narrative giants — the first tells Joseph from the well to the throne of Egypt, the second unfurls prophet after prophet before ending in the longest account of Moses before Pharaoh. The formula does for these chapters what a seal does for a decree: it authenticates the narrator before the narration starts. Stories are exactly the genre where a community expects embellishment, where the listener assumes the teller is shaping events for effect. The Qur'an plants its credential at the gate — this telling is not folklore polished by retelling but revelation accountable to truth — and then permits itself a storyteller's art with a clear conscience.

**Clear, Yet Addressed to Faith**

Clarity of statement is not compulsion of assent, and the next verse will name the intended audience as people who believe. The distinction is precise. The Book is clear; the heart still chooses. Nothing in "clear Book" means that reading it mechanically produces submission, and the Qur'an's own history — recited in the hearing of eloquent Makkans who called it sorcery — is the standing proof. The clarity is in the message and its claims; the acceptance is a separate act, one that faith performs and unbelief declines on the same evidence. This verse, standing between the disjointed letters and the announcement of Moses' story, sets the terms for everything that follows: a narrative will now be told, it will be told plainly, and it will be told so that believing hearts can read their own situation inside it. A story told by a clear Book to believers is not entertainment; it is a mirror with the fog wiped off.

**The Book That Files Its Own Claims as Evidence**

The Qur'an is the only book in this tradition that argues about itself inside its own text, and this verse is one of the places the argument is joined. It does not merely contain verses; it names them āyāt, folding the recitation into the same category of evidence as the cosmos, and then it adds a property no comet or mountain can carry: clarity of intent. The commentators enumerated that clarity — in what it commands, in the good it brings, in its message — and the enumeration converts the Book from an object to be admired into an address to be answered. A sign in the sky can be ignored without insult; a clear address cannot. Other chapters open with the same kind of announcement — the tenth, thirteenth, and fifteenth sūrahs carry parallel formulas — as though the Qur'an kept stamping its consignments so that no listener could later claim not to know what he was holding. The claim of clarity also fixes the terms of every dispute the Book records about itself. When its hearers call it poetry, sorcery, or fables of the ancients, the Qur'an reports the charges without softening them, and its answers never run to self-excuse; they run to the standard this verse established — the message is plain, so the disagreement is not about meaning but about will. For the believers who first heard Moses' story under persecution, that standard was shelter. If the Book is clear, then the truth of their cause did not depend on clever reading, learned debate, or the good fortune of a sympathetic audience. It rested on the plain words of a plain Book, delivered by a Prophet who repeated them in the marketplace exactly as they were revealed, and it invited every listener to name the obscurity he was objecting to — knowing he could not.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:3
> We narrate to you ˹O Prophet˺ part of the story of Moses and Pharaoh in truth for people who believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**Narration Asserted as Truth**

The verse makes three claims at once, and the first concerns the act of telling: We narrate means that God Himself conveys the account to His Prophet — the classical commentators specify through the Angel Gabriel — so the Prophet stands in this verse as recipient, not compiler. "In truth" then governs the telling itself: the narrative is not arranged for effect, its details are not softened, and nothing is included because it makes a better scene. This is a strong claim to set beside the genre it enters, because the ancient Near East told the Moses story constantly, and every retelling adjusted it. The Qur'an enters that crowded field announcing that its version is measured against reality by the One who was present. The assertion of truth is not a stylistic flourish; it is the criterion by which the audience is asked to judge the selection that follows.

**A Part Deliberately Told**

"Part of the story" is the second claim, and it is the more striking one. The verse concedes selection openly: not the whole account, but a portion chosen for this audience at this moment. The Qur'an returns to Moses in more than twenty chapters, and the episodes shift — his infancy in this sūrah, his mission speeches in chapter 26, the wonders in chapter 20, his prayer for expansion of his chest in chapter 20 as well. Selection of this kind is a form of argument. The portion chosen here runs from a mother's despair to a fugitive's flight, and that arc is precisely the shape of the Makkan believers' own circumstances: hemmed in by a power that kills their sons, awaiting a deliverance they cannot see. A comprehensive chronicle would have buried the application; a selected episode aims it. The concession of partiality in the verse is what guards its truthfulness — no one claims the part is the whole, and the part is true as told.

**For Believers, Not for Spectators**

The verse closes by naming its audience: people who believe. The story is not offered to the curious as antiquity or to scholars as comparative literature. Belief functions here as the instrument of reception — the believer is the one positioned to hear what the account is doing. Others can register the events; only the believer can register the pattern, because the pattern's premise is that God acts in history toward those who hold to Him. The Makkan congregation, small and pressed, was being handed a document of identification: Egypt's Israelites were them, Egypt's Pharaoh was their own opposition, and the narration was delivered "in truth" so that the identification could not be dismissed as moralizing. The stories of the Qur'an are consistently audience-addressed in this way — told to a particular congregation at a particular pressure point, with the conviction that God's dealings do not change with the century.

**What Belief Reads in the Same Text**

Two readers can hold the same chapter and leave with different possessions. One leaves with an episode of ancient politics — an infant adrift, a palace intrigue, an extradition flight. The believer leaves with the machinery laid bare: that God's will ran beneath every scene, choosing the oppressed, turning the river into a corridor, feeding His enemy's household with the child who would end it. The Qur'an itself marks the difference later in this very sūrah, in the confession that most people do not know (28:13) — knowledge of the pattern is granted, not automatic. This verse therefore functions as a set of instructions appended to the story: read as a believer, which means read for the handling of God inside events, and the "part" told will turn out to be the part that matters. The truth of the telling and the faith of the hearing are the two handles by which the whole sūrah is meant to be carried.

**An Unlettered Prophet in a Storied City**

The claim "in truth" carries a weight that is easy to miss from a distance of centuries: Moses was not the Prophet's ancestor, his story was not Makkah's local heritage, and the man announcing it was known to his own city as unlettered. The Makkans said so themselves, and the Qur'an records the charge that he was merely receiving tales handed down — the accusation is on the record precisely because the audience knew other versions of Egypt's stories existed on the trade routes and among the People of the Book. Against that suspicion the verse stakes a precise position: the narration is God's own act, conveyed as recitation, and its truth is guaranteed by the One who witnessed it. The guarantee explains the telling's peculiarities. Where inherited story runs to embellishment, this telling withholds — no name for Pharaoh, no name for the mother, the sister unnamed, the running man unnamed. Where inherited story elaborates motive, this telling leaves the killing of the boys unexplained and lets the policy speak. Restraint of that kind is not how borrowed material behaves; borrowing adds. The verse therefore puts its audience on notice about the standards of the recitation to come: part of the story, selected by God, delivered in truth — and the selection's very silences are part of the truth being told. Nothing in the recitation defends itself against the charge of borrowing; it simply refuses the borrower's habits. The stories arrive with names subtracted and motives left open, and the subtraction is the signature: a man assembling tales would have padded them, because padding is what makes tales satisfying, while this text cuts even its own hero's name from the scene of his worst hour and lets the confession carry it.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:4
> Indeed, Pharaoh ˹arrogantly˺ elevated himself in the land and divided its people into ˹subservient˺ groups, one of which he persecuted, slaughtering their sons and keeping their women. He was truly one of the corruptors

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tyranny Organized Like a Census**

The verse sketches a system, not a mood. Pharaoh "elevated himself in the land" — the classical commentators read this as rule exercised through tyranny and oppression within his own kingdom, and some connect it to his claim, recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an, to be the highest lord of his people. Then comes the machinery: the population is divided into factions, one faction is marked down for persecution, and the persecution has a scheduled procedure — sons slain, women kept alive. Elevation, classification, and culling form one sequence, and the sequence is the essence of the verse. Tyranny in the Qur'an's portrait of Egypt is not the caprice of a hot-tempered king; it is administration. A despot who merely raged would be dangerous and survivable. A despot who counts his subjects in order to decide which households may raise a son has converted the state itself into an instrument of extermination, and that is what the verse documents in one breath.

**Why the Sons and Not the Daughters**

The text gives no reason for the asymmetry, and one classical commentator drew the right conclusion from that silence: the Qur'an records the practice without explaining it because the practice requires no defense to be recognizable — it is simply what domination does. The commentators did preserve explanations from the wider tradition, the commonest being that soothsayers or a dream warned Pharaoh of an Israelite boy who would end his kingdom. Two observations weigh against that as the driving motive. First, the same decree is reissued long after Moses stands before Pharaoh: his chiefs protest the threat Moses poses and Pharaoh answers, “We will kill their sons and keep their women. We will completely dominate them.” (7:127), and again later, “Kill the sons of those who believe with him and keep their women.” (40:25). Second, the Qur'an earlier summarized the whole Egyptian period as torment that included “slaughtering your sons and keeping your women” (2:49). The repetition across decades points to collective punishment as standing policy — a tool for keeping a subject people weak — rather than a single prophecy-panic. The Bible's own account of Pharaoh's order to the midwives (Exodus 1) records the same policy shape.

**The Claim Behind the Slaughter**

"Elevated himself" carries the ideology that made the rest possible. A ruler who names himself the highest lord owes account to no one, and the verse's next clauses show what freedom from account produces: a population sorted into instruments and obstacles. The commentators' two readings — oppressive rule on one side, the pretension to deity on the other — are not rivals so much as stages of one descent. The pretension authorizes the oppression, because a merely human magistrate can be reminded of limits, while a self-declared god must never be contradicted. Egypt in this verse is therefore the Qur'an's complete model of tyranny: a theology of the ruler, an economy of the enslaved, and a demography of the knife. Every element will be answered in the sūrah's course, and each answer will be taken from the hand of the faction Pharaoh tried to erase.

**The Verdict: One of the Corruptors**

The sentence ends on a legal word: Pharaoh "was truly one of the corruptors." Corruption, in the Qur'an's usage, names the wrecking of a land's right order — blood spilled without due cause, servitude imposed on the free, wealth and power circulated among a faction. The charge is deliberately placed last, after the facts that justify it, so that it lands as a verdict rather than an insult. It also announces the sentence the reader will watch executed across the sūrah: the corruptor's corruption will return through his own instruments, since the boy he spared in his wife's arms will be the means of his drowning. The verse thus performs in miniature what the whole sūrah performs at length — evidence gathered, motive exposed, verdict pronounced — and it does so before Moses is even born into the narrative.

**Factions as a Technology of Rule**

The division of the population deserves its own inspection, because the verse presents it as Pharaoh's method rather than his mood. To sort a people into groups, mark one for persecution, and alternate reward and terror among the rest is the standing technology of every settled tyranny: it prevents the birth of a united front, prices the loyalty of the favored, and makes the oppressed quarrel over shrinking exemptions instead of common rights. Egypt under this verse is administered on that file system — and the system explains facts that mere cruelty cannot. It explains why the persecution of the Israelites could be intensified or relaxed by decree, as policy required across the generations the Qur'an covers. It explains the presence of chiefs and councilors at every stage of the sūrah, since a divided land is governed through its notables. And it explains the deepest fact in the chapter: that a population so managed could still produce, from inside its own weakness, the leadership verse 5 announces. Division was designed to make that impossible. The verse's cold observation that Pharaoh "divided" the land's people is therefore not a background detail; it is the strategic problem the entire deliverance will solve, and the first reader — a persecuted minority being sorted and pressured in Makkah — recognized the file system from the inside, because it was the one they were living in. The verse's own progress mirrors the method: elevation, division, persecution, slaughter, and finally the label — corruptors — that the system earned. A state that sorts human beings into instruments and obstacles has already answered the question of what it is; the rest of the sūrah merely executes the verdict.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:5
> But it was Our Will to favour those who were oppressed in the land, making them models ˹of faith˺ as well as successors

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Initiative Runs Downward from Heaven**

The verse pivots on a single verb of willing: it was Our Will. Nothing in the clause grounds the coming rescue in Israelite merit, preparation, or strength, and the omission is the theology. The oppressed are not described as crying out at this point, nor as repenting, nor as organizing; they are described as being crushed. The deliverance begins on God's side of the ledger. This ordering guards a doctrine the Qur'an maintains across its stories: favor is prior to fitness, and fitness is produced by favor rather than purchased before it. A community that read its own weakness as disqualification would have the verse's grammar to answer — the sentence makes God's will the engine and Israel's condition the occasion. For the verse's first audience, believers who could point to no earthly strength at all, that grammar was the whole message: the same Will that lifted the enslaved of Egypt had already turned toward them.

**Leadership Conferred on the Enslaved**

Two offices are bestowed, and both are startling in context. They are to be made "models ˹of faith˺" — leaders, in the classical understanding: people set foremost in faith and in commanding what is right — and they are to be made successors, heirs who inherit standing in the land. The Qur'an applies this leadership language elsewhere to prophets and to the righteous: it speaks of making guides who call to God by His permission, and of Abraham being raised to leadership for mankind. The pattern underneath the applications is consistent. Leadership in this Book is conferred on the lowly as a trust, never seized by the mighty as a prize, and the men and women who emerge from servitude carrying it testify by their origin to where authority comes from. Egypt's Israelites had spent a generation being told their lives were negotiable; the verse opens by handing them the one title Pharaoh's court could neither grant nor revoke.

**Heirs of a Land They Never Owned**

"Successors" names the inheritance, and the sūrah will not here specify its terms — but the Qur'an settles the question elsewhere, at the sea's far shore: “And ˹so˺ We made the oppressed people successors of the eastern and western lands, which We had showered with blessings.” (7:137). The inheritance was Egypt itself, its cultivated abundance and its standing — not seized as plunder by conquest's own logic, but transferred as an heir's portion after the previous holder forfeited it. The legal imagination of the verse is worth marking: an heir succeeds because the predecessor has died, and the verse has already confined Pharaoh's regime to corruption. What follows in the sūrah is the reading of a will. The oppressed who could not own a hut in the delta are named, while the decree is still being carried out against their sons, as inheritors of the palace, its river, and its fields.

**The Direction of the Promise**

Set beside the preceding verse, this one completes a contrast that will organize the whole chapter. There, a ruler elevated himself, divided people, and culled households; here, God wills favor for the divided, and the instrument of their elevation is stated without armies or alliances. The promise's direction of travel is unmistakably upward from below: the trampled are made leaders, the landless are made heirs. That direction is not a one-time policy for one people. The verse is narrated to a congregation under pressure precisely so they can locate themselves inside it, and the Qur'an's own later promise to such congregations runs on the same rails — the earth belongs to God, and He causes it to be inherited by whom He wills of His servants. The next verse will state the second half of the decree: establishment in the very land of their humiliation, and a showing to Pharaoh of the thing he most feared.

**Made Models Before Made Heirs**

The order of the two promises is part of their meaning. The verse makes the oppressed "models ˹of faith˺ as well as successors" — leadership in faith is conferred before inheritance is granted, and the sequence is the lesson: character is formed before territory is transferred, because an inheritance given to the unformed destroys both the heir and the inheritance. Egypt's Israelites had been ground down for generations; the first gift would have to be the restoration of their own standing before God, the rebuilding of a people capable of holding what was coming to them. The commentators' definition of the model-leaders here — those foremost in faith and in calling others to it — makes the same point from the other side: the office described is spiritual before it is political. Only after the clause of character does the verse reach possession, first of themselves, then of the land. The reading matters for every community that has ever read this verse under oppression, because it forbids the shortcut. The promise does not say the crushed will be handed power while remaining as the crushing made them; it says they will be remade into people who can stand, and then established. The sequence also answers a temptation the oppressed know well — to seek the inheritance first and the formation later, taking the land while keeping the wounds. The verse's grammar closes that road: in God's administration of this deliverance, the making always precedes the giving. The sūrah will show the sequence in motion: before Israel inherits a stone of Egypt, it receives Moses — formed, forgiven, commissioned — and before Moses speaks to Pharaoh, he is given words, signs, and a brother at his side. Possession in this Book is always the last installment, paid after the people it is paid to have been rebuilt into those who can hold it without becoming what it replaced.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:6
> and to establish them in the land; and through them show Pharaoh, Hamân, and their soldiers ˹the fulfilment of˺ what they feared

**Expanded Commentary**

**Established Where They Had Been Property**

The decree now names its second term: establishment in the land. The verb behind "establish" carries the sense of being given firm footing, empowerment, and settled authority — the very things the Israelites had been denied under a system that kept them landless and expendable. The geography sharpens the point: the land in question is Egypt itself, and the Qur'an elsewhere states the outcome without disguise, that the oppressed were made successors in its eastern and western parts (7:137). A people who could not lawfully keep their own sons are promised secure standing in the capital of the man destroying them. The verse therefore completes what the previous verse began: favor decided, leadership conferred, and now permanence granted. Nothing in the sequence depends on Israelite power, because at this stage of the story they possess none — which is exactly the point, since the promise is issued as pure decree while the knives are still working.

**Hāmān Beside the Throne**

The verse names Pharaoh's chief officer, and the naming is worth pausing over because Hāmān appears six times across the Qur'an — here and in verses 8 and 38 of this chapter, then in chapter 29, and twice in chapter 40, where his most famous commission is issued: “O Hamân! Build me a high tower so I may reach the pathways” (40:36) — the tower by which Pharaoh proposed to climb toward the God of Moses. The figure who can be ordered to mobilize the state's builders and materials for such a project is plainly the second man in the kingdom. That functional picture — deputy in authority, director of great public works — is what Muslim scholars who examined the identification question closely concluded the name should carry, treating Hāmān as a title of office on the pattern of "Pharaoh" itself, associated with the high priesthood of Amun and the supervision of construction. The office explains the texts; the texts are what the officeholder would have controlled.

**A Title or a Borrowed Name?**

The alternative on the table came from outside the tradition: some revisionist scholars proposed that the Qur'an's Hāmān was a confused borrowing from the Bible — specifically from the villain of the book of Esther, the Persian courtier who plotted against the Jews. That derivation has to explain how a Persian grandee of a much later empire wandered into an Egyptian court, and the explanations offered — mixing of sources, garbled transmission — treat the Qur'an as a document whose errors could be traced genealogically. Against it stands the title reading, which requires no borrowing at all: an Egyptian court title, plausibly vocalized from the name of the god Amun, fits every Qur'anic appearance of the man — his proximity to the throne, his management of building works, his presence at the politics of persecution. The title reading is the stronger account because it explains the data with the least strain, and it dissolves the anachronism that the borrowing theory exists to exploit. The disagreement itself is instructive: Muslim scholars and their critics were arguing about a name, and the argument turned on whether the Qur'an was quoting its neighbors or describing its own scene.

**What They Dreaded, and How It Arrived**

The verse's last clause promises that the fear Pharaoh, Hāmān, and their soldiers entertained would be shown to them — fulfilled before their eyes — through the very people they were crushing. What was that fear? The commentaries transmit one answer drawn from the soothsayer tradition: a prophecy that an Israelite male child would rise and destroy the kingdom, which is why the sons were being killed. But the stronger answer reads the fear out of the verse's own policy: a regime that rules a subject population only by periodically culling its males lives in permanent dread of that population, and the dread grows with every act of suppression. On this reading, what they feared was precisely the loss of control their violence was designed to prevent — and the fulfilment shown to them through the oppressed would be the complete unraveling of that control, witnessed at the sea. The irony is structural: the fear was not irrational, only misdirected. It should have warned them away from the injustice that was manufacturing their enemy.

**Six Mentions, One Assistant**

Trace Hāmān through the Qur'an and a single portrait emerges. He is named here at the politics of persecution (28:6), again when the basket reaches the palace (28:8), and at the tower project, where Pharaoh orders him to mobilize the builders (28:38). Chapter 29 lists him among the punished councils of the past, and chapter 40 names him twice in the last act — at the court's vote to kill Moses (40:24) and at the tower's commission (40:36). Six mentions, and in every one he stands at Pharaoh's shoulder. The pattern is the office: the Qur'an never shows him acting alone, because his function in the story is to be the machinery of another man's will — the executor without whom the tyrant's decrees would remain rhetoric. That is why his presence at the persecution in this verse matters as much as his presence at the tower. Persecution on the Egyptian scale required administration, and administration required a Hāmān; the verse includes "their soldiers" in the same clause for the same reason. Guilt in this Book is not confined to the man on the throne, and the sūrah's later scenes of judgment will name the assistant among the ruined precisely because the record kept him beside his principal at every stage of the crime.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:7
> We inspired the mother of Moses: “Nurse him, but when you fear for him, put him then into the river, and do not fear or grieve. We will certainly return him to you, and make him one of the messengers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Mother Told to Do the Unthinkable**

The command sequence is ruthless on its face: nurse him, and at the moment fear arrives, place him in the river. Every maternal instinct runs the other way, and the verse asks its first reader to feel the contradiction before resolving it. A mother's arms are the one place Egypt's decree cannot reach, and the instruction schedules their surrender for the very hour the danger peaks. Obedience here precedes explanation — she is not told how the river will help, only that she must not fear and must not grieve, and that the outcome is guaranteed twice over: he will be returned, and he will be made one of the messengers. The second promise is larger than anything she could have imagined asking for, and its size is the point. The command takes her to the edge of what she can bear, and the promise carries her past it, because a mother who knows her castaway is a future prophet is not the same person as a mother watching a basket sink.

**Inspiration Without Prophethood**

The verb is the Qur'an's revelation verb — We inspired — yet the recipient is a woman the tradition insists was not a prophet. The word's range in the Qur'an makes room for her: the same verb describes what God cast into the hearts of Jesus' disciples, and what He communicated to the bee, teaching it where to build its home (16:68) and how to draw its provision (5:111). Classical theology sorted such matters into prophetic revelation on one side and inspiration — a casting into the heart without office — on the other, and one commentator argued pointedly that even had Gabriel conveyed this message, its arrival would not have made her a prophet, since the tradition holds by consensus that she was not one. The sorting matters for the verse's tenderness. God spoke to this mother directly enough that she could act on it, without conferring rank on her, because the situation required guidance and she was the one who needed it. Revelation's economy in the Qur'an is never wasteful of rank and never stingy with help.

**Two Prohibitions, One Peace**

"Do not fear or grieve" names two different wounds, and the commentators drew the line between them: fear suffers over what may come, grief over what has gone. She is forbidden both halves of maternal agony — the anticipated loss of the child she is about to release and the mourning for him once he is gone. The pairing belongs to a refrain the Qur'an reserves for the people of the Garden, who are told of themselves that “There will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve.” (3:170). Placing that grammar in a mother's ear, on the riverbank, at the hour of surrender, amounts to handing her the peace of the saved in advance of the world to come. The promise is constructed like the covenant it guarantees — return, then prophethood — and it is sealed with the strongest divine idiom of certainty: We will certainly.

**The River as a Road**

The river that carries death decrees for her people becomes, by command, the corridor of rescue. The same waters that Pharaoh's order has turned into a graveyard are instructed to deliver her son to the one house in Egypt from which no Israelite child returned alive. Chapter 20 tells the same moment with one more piece of equipment — “Put him into a chest, then put it into the river.” (20:39) — and the commentaries treat the two accounts as one narrative told at different resolutions, the chest named there and the river here. The divergence is worth stating plainly rather than smoothing over: the accounts differ in detail because each selects for its own emphasis, and neither claims to be exhaustive. What both insist on is the inversion. Water, the instrument of the genocide, is conscripted as its defeat, and the household that decreed drowning receives the drowned child intact — a rescue accomplished with the tyrant's own river.

**No Calendar Was Given**

One silence in the verse is deliberate: she is given no date. The classical commentator who collected the reports on this story noted that various suggestions circulated for how long the mother could hide her son before casting him away, and he ruled that no sound evidence fixes any such period. The silence keeps the command where it belongs. Had a term been attached — hide him a month, a season — her task would have been arithmetic, and arithmetic asks nothing of the heart. As told, the command requires her to read her own fear as the appointed trigger, to act at the moment the danger becomes unbearable, and to trust the promise rather than a schedule. Trust without a timetable is the hardest kind, which is why the verse spends its grammar on certainty — We will certainly return him — instead of on dates she could have counted down.

**Three Accounts of How the Message Reached Her**

How, mechanically, did the instruction arrive? The commentators preserve three answers, and the disagreement is instructive. One reading holds that the word was cast directly into her heart — the plainest sense of inspiring a non-prophet. A second holds that she saw the whole command in a dream, which fits the poem-like certainty she carried afterward. A third says Gabriel appeared and spoke to her; one commentator argued against this on solid grounds, since an angelic visitation was the vehicle of prophethood, and the tradition holds by consensus that she was never a prophet. Each reading has its ground: direct casting honors the verb's breadth in the Qur'an, where God inspires the bee and the disciples; the dream reading explains how a mother could trust so fantastic a promise; the angel reading takes the magnitude of the commission seriously. The first is the strongest, because it requires the least machinery and fits the word's use across the Book. But the deeper point survives every reading: the same God who addresses prophets addressed this mother at the riverbank, and the address carried a promise He then kept to the letter — which is why the tradition never needed to make her a prophet to explain the certainty in her obedience.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:8
> And ˹it so happened that˺ Pharaoh’s people picked him up, only to become their enemy and source of grief. Surely Pharaoh, Hamân, and their soldiers were sinful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rescue Delivered to the Enemy's Door**

Of all the houses on the river, the basket drifts to the one under whose seal the killing decree went out. The verse does not flinch from the fact that the rescue was carried out by the enemy's own household — the people who pulled him up were the people whose policy had condemned him. Providence in this sūrah works by capture: the hunters are made the couriers, the wolf den is made the nursery, and the closer the child is brought to the center of Egyptian power, the more completely that power is penetrated. Any other deliverance — a fisherman's family, a village upstream — would have saved the boy and accomplished nothing more. This deliverance saves the boy and installs him, at state expense, at the exact point where Egypt's decisions are made. The verse's opening phrase, rendered "˹it so happened that˺", concedes the human view: from the riverbank it looked like chance, a floating box, a palace servant's idle curiosity. The verse then states the other view in the grammar of purpose.

**An Enemy and a Sorrow, by Design**

"Only to become their enemy and source of grief" — the clause is built to describe an outcome, not an intention. The classical commentators insisted on the distinction: the House of Pharaoh took the child in with genuine hope, yet what God had appointed for that act of taking was enmity and grief. Both sides of the clause deserve their weight. The affection was real — the next verse will show a queen pleading for the baby's life — and God accomplished His plan through it rather than against it. The sorrow was also real, and the sūrah will count it out: grief at the plagues, grief at the pursuit, the final grief at the water's edge. A modern reader might call this dramatic irony and stop; the verse calls it appointment. The household raised its own adversary the way a garrison raises the siege engine that will breach it, and the clause's grammar — so that he would become — puts the appointment in heaven's sentence even while the adoption was in the Egyptians' hands.

**Lovability as Strategy**

How does a condemned Israelite infant survive intake into the household that kills his kind? Chapter 20 supplies the mechanism, spoken by God to Moses long afterward: “And I blessed you with lovability from Me ˹O Moses˺ so that you would be brought up under My ˹watchful˺ Eye.” (20:39). The charm that disarmed the palace was not the baby's luck; it was a deployed attribute, a sweetness assigned for the purpose. The detail repays attention because it shows the plan operating at the level of feelings. Pharaoh's household was not overpowered into mercy — no plague had yet fallen, no sign had been shown. They were moved, and the moving was managed. The tyrant's own affections were turned into the weak point through which his opponent entered his house. Power's greatest fortifications have never been walls; they have always been the attachments of the people inside them, and this verse reports the capture of exactly that.

**Sinners Certain of Their Mercy**

The closing verdict names the household sinners, and a minority of commentators softened the word to mean those who merely mistook the situation — they did not know what the child would become, so they erred rather than sinned. The harder reading is the stronger one, for two reasons. The word's primary register in the Qur'an is criminal guilt, and the context is a standing policy of murdered children; ignorance of the baby's future does not launder the policy. And the verse's structure — sin named immediately after the adoption — prevents the reader from romanticizing the rescue scene into a story about kind-hearted Egyptians. The men who pulled the basket up were the same men who enforced the culling; their tenderness toward one child coexisted with the machinery against all the others. The Qur'an keeps both facts in one verse: mercy received through sinners, verdict pronounced on sinners, and the whole arrangement carried by a God who needs neither to excuse the one nor to pause the other.

**The Education of a Prince of Egypt**

Look at the adoption from the side of what it gave the child, and the plan's reach becomes visible. The hunted Israelite infant grew up inside the court of Egypt — speaking its language as a native, schooled in its administration, moving through its hierarchy with the fluency of a household son. Years later, when he stands before that same court to deliver God's message, he will need exactly what the palace gave him: the idiom the mighty understand, the working knowledge of the state he is confronting, the standing of a man arguing from inside rather than pleading from outside. The commentators noted that by adulthood he was identified with Pharaoh's own retinue; that identification was training. Providence here behaves like a general who feeds his future adversary's fortress from the inside: the household that decreed the destruction of Israel's sons financed the formation of the man who would break it. The sūrah's doctrine of formation — gifts given at maturity (28:14) — rests on this foundation, since wisdom and knowledge do not descend into a vacuum but into a mind already stocked by an extraordinary education. Nothing in the arrangement was wasted: the boy's years at the tables of his people's destroyer were, in the plan's economy, the first act of his preparation against it.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:9
> Pharaoh’s wife said ˹to him˺, “˹This baby is˺ a source of joy for me and you. Do not kill him. Perhaps he may be useful to us or we may adopt him as a son.” They were unaware ˹of what was to come˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Speech Against a Royal Mood**

The verse catches the moment of decision, and it is a woman who turns it. The household's default procedure for an Israelite boy was the knife; into that procedure the wife inserts a sentence that reframes the entire object: “˹This baby is˺ a source of joy for me and you” (28:9). Her grammar does the work — the pronouns fold Pharaoh into the blessing, making the child's survival a shared interest rather than her private sentiment, and the closing plea is a command to the king himself: do not kill him. One classical report imagines his reply — a comfort for you, but for me, no — and whether or not he said it, the scene it imagines is exactly the one the verse shows: the family's men see a threat, the wife sees a son. [UNVERIFIED: the exchange and the report that Pharaoh would have been guided had he echoed her hope are transmitted in the commentary tradition without a checkable collection number.] The political fact underneath the dialogue is that extermination policies run on unanimity, and one voice inside the palace, placed to speak at the moment of intake, was enough to break it.

**Āsiyah in the Company of Four**

The Qur'an does not name Pharaoh's wife, but the tradition does, and it ranks her without embarrassment. The Prophet himself named her among the four women who stand at the head of the women of the worlds — Maryam, Khadījah, Fāṭimah, and Āsiyah — in a narration carried by Muslim (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2430). The Qur'an elsewhere records her own prayer, made under torture in her husband's house: “My Lord! Build me a house in Paradise near You, deliver me from Pharaoh and his ˹evil˺ doing, and save me from the wrongdoing people.” (66:11). Read together, the two texts give her a biography in outline: a believer living inside the tyranny, dissenting at the decisive moment in this verse, and finally asking for delivery from her own household. Her sainthood was not a withdrawal from that household into safety; it was exercised precisely at the point of intake, when her word was the last wall between one Israelite child and the state's blade.

**The Hope the King Refused**

Her reasoning is stated in the verse in two clauses that read like an investor's brief: he may be useful to us, or we may adopt him as a son. The commentary tradition preserves the report that Pharaoh answered her point by point — benefit for you, none for me — and the report that had he accepted her hopeful framing, God would have granted him the guidance He granted her. Both reports circle the same teaching. Two people held the same child and the same facts; one read them through hope and was led by it to faith, the other read them through threat and was led by it to the sea. The Qur'an's portrait of Pharaoh is not of a man never offered mercy, but of a man who declined it in the vocabulary of statecraft, and his wife's sentence is the earliest offer on record.

**Unaware of the Fruit They Were Planting**

The verse closes on the household's ignorance — “They were unaware ˹of what was to come˺” (28:9) — and the irony it plants is doubled. They hoped the child would benefit them; he would, but not as they calculated, since the benefit of Egypt's prophets was never profit but warning, and its refusal was ruin. They hoped to adopt him as a son; the adoption succeeded so thoroughly that he grew up in the palace's confidence, moved through its streets unrecognized as an Israelite, and learned its administration from inside — training no oppressed outsider could have bought. The prayer of the tyrant's wife, spoken to save a baby from her husband, turned out to be the instrument by which her husband's kingdom received its final preacher. Nothing in the scene was wasted: the mercy was real, the unawareness was real, and both were carried by a plan that needed exactly this household, exactly this woman, exactly this hour.

**Persuasion Inside a Tyranny**

Study what the queen actually argues, and a complete theory of dissent under dictatorship sits inside her sentence. She does not preach to Pharaoh, does not invoke the God of the oppressed, does not appeal to law or mercy — the court has no language for any of these. She argues interest: the child may be useful to us; we may adopt him as a son. The two grounds are the only two a tyrant's house can hear — profit and possession — and she frames the baby's survival in exactly those terms. The commentators' report of Pharaoh's chilling reply, that the comfort was hers and none of it his, shows the limit of such persuasion: interest can suspend a killing for an afternoon; it cannot convert a killer. Her deeper faith waited for another register entirely — the prayer the Qur'an records in chapter 66, asked openly of God for a house in Paradise and deliverance from her own husband (66:11). Two registers, one believer: the prudential speech inside the court, which saved a life, and the confessional prayer before God, which saved her soul. The Qur'an honors both, and the pairing is its quiet instruction to every believer living inside a system they cannot yet confront — work the register the room can hear, and keep the other register for the One who hears everything.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:10
> And the heart of Moses’ mother ached so much that she almost gave away his identity, had We not reassured her heart in order for her to have faith ˹in Allah’s promise˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Heart Emptied to Its Walls**

The Arabic says the mother's heart became empty, and the translators reach for "ached" to carry the weight of a word that pictures a vessel scraped bare. The classical commentators read the emptiness precisely: a heart emptied of everything except the remembrance of her son and the grief of separation — no room left for patience, strategy, or self-command. Some noted that the verb can carry a morning sense, so that the verse would say morning found her heart already emptied, the sleepless night compressed into two words. The state described is the far edge of maternal endurance, and the verse does not treat it as failure. A mother who felt nothing at the river would have been the failure; the text's honesty about the emptying is what makes the next clause — the almost-disclosure — comprehensible. She was not weak. She was a woman holding the two heaviest things a heart can hold, the command of God and the body of her child, and the verse reports what that weighed.

**Two Words for One Organ**

The verse uses two different words for heart in a single sentence, and the commentators attended to the shift. The first is fuʾād, the word for the heart as the seat of feeling and perception — the organ that burns; the second is qalb, the heart as kernel and turning core. Lexicographers sometimes distinguish them as container and essence, and the spiritual literature of Islam built entire ladders on such terms, ranking fuʾād, qalb, sirr, and rūḥ as depths of the inner life. The more disciplined reading is also available: the two words may simply alternate as scripture's prose does, the variety itself a mark of oral power. What holds in either case is the meaning of the sequence — her feeling (fuʾād) was scraped empty, and what held her together was fastened at the deeper core (qalb). The vocabulary of the verse thus maps the anatomy of endurance: the surface of the heart may be emptied while something at its center is knotted fast by a hand from outside.

**"We Tied Her Heart"**

The verb rendered "reassured" is rabatnā — We tied it. The root is the ordinary Arabic of binding: a rope tied, a knot fastened, a thing secured against falling. The word-by-word commentators give it as strengthening, and the image it carries is a restraint applied from outside, like a hand closing over a trembling mouth from behind. What was about to escape her was the one secret Egypt could not be allowed to hear — that this child belonged to the woman being searched for — and the verse says plainly that she almost gave it away. The tie held. And the verse assigns the tie a purpose clause that reads strangely at first: it was applied "in order for her to have faith." Faith here is not the absence of turmoil but the act performed inside it — believing the promise that the drowning river was a delivery road. God held her tongue so that her heart could hold His word, and the knot is the verse's own icon of how divine help usually arrives: not by removing the weight, but by fastening the person to a promise underneath it.

**The Disclosure That Never Happened**

The danger the verse averted was total. One cry in the street — my son, they have my son — would have returned the child to the decree and the mother to the executioner's ledger, because a palace that counts households knows how to interrogate a grieving woman. What she would have disclosed, the commentators say, is exactly that: his identity, his origin, the river. The countermeasure was applied at the single point of failure, her own tongue, at the single decisive hour. The verse thereby teaches its doctrine of providence at maximum resolution: God's help is not always the removal of the danger; it is often the management of one syllable at the moment the danger turns on it. The mother who walked home having said nothing had performed, without knowing it, the most consequential silence recorded in the sūrah — and the faith it secured in her is the faith the verse names as its purpose, granted at the edge of speech.

**Help That Arrives as Restraint**

Notice what kind of help the verse records, because it is not the kind anyone would have prayed for. The mother would have asked for her child back, or for the decree abolished, or for the palace to fall. What she received instead was the management of a single impulse — the withheld cry, the swallowed name — at the one moment when everything else depended on it. God's intervention did not remove the danger; it removed the one syllable that would have made the danger fatal. This is help as restraint, and the Qur'an documents it elsewhere in the same register: the young men in the cave hid by their persecutors without a plan (18), and Hagar's water was found by the dig of a desperate heel rather than a miracle in the sky. The mode is consistent enough to be called characteristic. Divine help usually reaches a person at the precise point of their failing, not at the scene of their catastrophe, because the catastrophe is not the thing being managed — the person is. For this mother, the deliverance she begged for was weeks away, on the other side of an adoption she could not yet see; the help she needed that morning was to keep silent, and the verse records that the tie held.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:11
> And she said to his sister, “Keep track of him!” So she watched him from a distance, while they were unaware

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah of the Tracking**

This chapter bears the name al-Qaṣaṣ — the twenty-eighth sūrah, Makkan, eighty-eight verses, titled in English "The Story." The Arabic root behind the name carries a double sense that this verse happens to stage: qaṣṣ is to follow a thing closely, to trace it, and it is also to narrate what one has traced. The sūrah is both a following and a telling, and its eleventh verse hands the following to a little girl. The mother who cannot walk to the river herself sends the next best thing, a daughter unremarkable enough to move through a slave society's streets unexamined, and the girl's assignment — keep track of him — is the first act of narration's raw material in the chapter. Before the story can be told, someone must be able to see it. The Qur'an's title thus points back to a child on a surveillance errand, which is as good a statement as any of the book's habit of loading its greatest weights onto the figures the world's stories use for scenery.

**A Girl on the Palace's Blind Side**

The watcher's effectiveness depended on the watchers not existing, and the verse states the asymmetry in its last clause: they were unaware. The House of Pharaoh — the best-policed court of its world, a state that counted Israelite households so precisely it could schedule the murder of their sons — could not see a girl at the water's edge. The blindness was not accidental but structural. Tyranny of the Egyptian kind trains itself to see populations as categories, and categories have no children, no sisters, no grief; a state that has decided a class of infants is not worth registering has also blinded itself to the class's mothers and daughters. The sister therefore operated, unknowingly, inside the dictatorship's largest vulnerability: it could not imagine being observed by the people it had stopped regarding as persons. The verse's calm record of her watching is a study in the limits of power — the palace's gaze was total in one direction and absent in the other, and heaven's plan traveled precisely through the absent direction.

**Distance That Was Also Obedience**

The instruction the sister received extends the chain of commands this family is living under. The mother was told to nurse, to release at the peak of fear, and to trust a promise with no date attached; now she adds a further discipline on her own initiative, sending her daughter to watch what cannot be helped. The watching is faith's active form here — not resignation at the riverbank, and not a rescue attempt that would have exposed the child, but sustained, disciplined attention at a safe remove. The family of Moses is thus portrayed running entirely on instruction and initiative under God, with no map beyond the last command received. The mother does not know where the basket will land; the daughter does not know what she will find; both act anyway, because acting on what has been given is the whole of what has been asked. In a chapter that will repeatedly show God commanding before explaining, this verse supplies the civilian version: obedience that looks like a mother's errand and a child's patience.

**What the Watching Made Possible**

The verse ends where its usefulness begins. The girl watches, the household remains oblivious, and the information she gathers — where the basket went, whose hands pulled it up, whether he lived — becomes the family's entire knowledge of the situation. On that knowledge the next move of the story will turn, when this same sister walks up to Pharaoh's own people and proposes a nurse for a baby who refuses to nurse. Nothing in this verse announces that turn, and nothing in it needs to; the text is content to record the patrol and the blindness it exploited. But the reader is being trained to see how the sūrah's providence works at ground level — through a discarded basket, an oblivious court, and a sister's feet — before it works through burning bushes and seas standing on either side. The small machinery and the great machinery are the same machinery, and this verse shows the small one running first.

**The Root That Names the Chapter and Runs Through Its Story**

The verb on the girl's assignment belongs to the root that gives this sūrah its name. Qaṣṣ in Arabic is to follow a thing closely, to pursue its trail — and, from that, to relate what the pursuit found. The chapter is called al-Qaṣaṣ, "the following, the stories," and this verse is where the root's two senses meet in one command: the sister is told to keep track of him, and what she tracks becomes the story. The root returns at the other end of the episode, when the fugitive stands at a well in Madyan and "told him his whole story" (28:25) — the trail followed in Egypt becomes the account given in a stranger's house, and the chapter's title brackets the whole arc. Names of this kind are the Qur'an's habit: a keyword from the text is lifted to name the whole, and the lifting is interpretive. Here it is exact. The sūrah is a story about being followed — a baby followed by a decree, followed by a sister, followed by an enemy's suspicion, followed by a runner with a warning — and about the One who follows every trail His servants run, arriving ahead of them each time. The girl who walked to the river carrying that verb was, without knowing it, carrying the chapter's name.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:12
> And We had caused him to refuse all wet-nurses at first, so his sister suggested, “Shall I direct you to a family who will bring him up for you and take good care of him?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hunger That Refused Everyone**

Before the sister speaks, the verse discloses what had been happening offstage: We had caused him to refuse all wet-nurses. The classical commentators are unanimous that this was no ordinary fastidiousness — God made the infant reject the breast of every woman other than his mother, so that no solution the palace tried could quiet him. From the Egyptians' side, the scene is a frustrating mystery: the child they spared will not eat. Nurses are procured, tried, dismissed; the baby's refusal persists; the household that saved him from the decree now watches him starve by choice. The verse names the cause plainly and locates it in heaven's prior act — We had caused — so that the reader holds both pictures at once: a sickly, unfed foundling as the problem appears below, and a locked door as it appears above. The refusal is the story's most concentrated display of providence as inconvenience: God does not need to strike anyone or split anything; He needs only to arrange that an infant will not latch.

**A Problem Manufactured for a Purpose**

The design inside the difficulty is the verse's theology. The refusal does not merely save Moses from an unknown nurse; it manufactures the one vacancy in Egypt that only his mother can fill, and it manufactures it inside Pharaoh's own household, on Pharaoh's own initiative. Had the baby nursed contentedly from the first woman offered, he would have been raised to weaning by strangers, and the promise made to his mother — We will certainly return him to you (28:7) — would have needed another path. The refusal is that path. The pattern deserves naming because the sūrah will repeat it: providence in this chapter usually advances by arranging a problem, not by removing one. The mother's emptied heart, the sister's watching, and now the baby's hunger are all weights, and each weight is a hinge. The Qur'an's God does not wait for convenient openings; He schedules the inconvenience and then stands in it.

**The Sister's Proposal**

Into the manufactured vacancy walks the only person who knows the solution: a girl the court has never seen, suggesting a family — hers — in words that promise competence and discretion. Her brother's own account in chapter 20 compresses the moment: “when your sister came along and proposed” (20:40) is how the Qur'an later describes her entrance to Moses himself. Her phrasing in this verse is a masterpiece of controlled information. She does not say his name, his origin, or the one fact that would kill the arrangement — that the family is his family. She offers a service: a household that will bring him up and treat him with care. The court hears a practical suggestion from an alert child; the reader hears a sister testifying, without saying so, that the baby's refusal has a cure and the cure is his mother. Everything depends on the palace never asking the obvious question, and the verse's opening clause — the divinely arranged refusal — has already guaranteed that the question is one they cannot afford to leave unanswered.

**The Enemy Pays to Reunite Them**

The transaction's final shape is the verse's quietest irony. The household that decreed the drowning of Israelite sons now hires an Israelite woman — the mother of the very child — to nurse him at their expense. The decree's intended orphan is restored to his mother under a contract signed by the decree's own authors, and his survival is funded from the palace. Nothing in the arrangement required a miracle visible to the naked eye: no staff turned serpent, no hand whitened. The whole rescue ran through a baby's appetite, a sister's nerve, and an employer's eagerness. This is the sūrah's doctrine of deliverance stated in miniature — God's promises to the oppressed are kept, but the keeping is dressed as ordinary commerce, ordinary child-rearing, ordinary luck. The mother receives her son in the open, under the tyrant's seal, and the tyrant funds the violation of his own law, certain that his household has merely acquired a healthy child.

**The First Sign Was a Refusal**

Moses' life will fill with signs — a staff that becomes a serpent, a hand that shines, a sea that opens — but the first of his signs is recorded here, and it is entirely negative: a baby who will not eat. God's power is displayed as an inability, placed in the smallest body in the story, and the palace feels it as frustration without ever learning its name. The pattern is one the sūrah will keep: the instrument Egypt despises carries the blow Egypt cannot answer. A slave nation's son will judge its king; a stammering tongue (20:27) will out-argue its priesthood; a shepherd's staff will swallow its sorcery. Strength and significance run on different tracks in this Book, and the verse marks the crossing point early, in a hunger that no wealth could relieve. The wet-nurses of Pharaoh's capital — the finest milk Egypt could buy — were defeated by an infant's turned face, and the defeat was total: not one succeeded, not once. What the court read as illness was obedience, and what the mother read as God's doing (28:13) was already, in the plan's own order, the opening sign of a prophetic career. The refusal also protected the secret at both ends. Had any nurse succeeded, the palace would have raised a child with no tie to Israel; because none succeeded, the one woman who could feed him was sent for, and the search for a nurse became the search that ended in his mother's arms. The sign and the rescue were the same act, read from two sides.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:13
> This is how We returned him to his mother so that her heart would be put at ease, and not grieve, and that she would know that Allah’s promise is ˹always˺ true. But most people do not know

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise Kept in a Feeding**

"Thus We returned him" — the verse announces its own key: the return promised at the riverbank (28:7) has been carried out, and the verse's opening word, thus, invites the reader to study the mechanism. The promise was kept not by an angel striding into the palace with a verdict, but by a nursing arrangement that every participant understood as ordinary commerce. The mother is reunited with her son lawfully, publicly, and at Pharaoh's expense; the tyrant who could not tolerate one Israelite child now cannot function without him. The classical commentators identify God's Promise here as precisely this return, foretold when she was told to release him. The shape of the fulfillment is the lesson: no part of the promise was scaled down to fit the method. She received the child whole, alive, and restored — but the delivery route ran through the enemy's kitchen, which is where heaven's postal service apparently keeps its doors.

**Comfort as a Stated Purpose**

The verse does something unusual with its grammar: it lists the mother's healing as an outcome in its own terms. Her heart is to be put at ease, and she is not to grieve — the same two prohibitions issued at the riverbank, now discharged. The commentators noted the tenderness of this: the narrative of a world-historical prophet pauses to heal one woman, because her suffering was not raw material for his story but a matter God tracked separately. The sūrah had opened with genocide; it now spends a verse on a mother's peace of mind, and the juxtaposition is deliberate. The same divine attention that counts the murdered sons of a nation registers the sleeplessness of one of their mothers. Later in the Qur'an, the mother of Jesus receives the same solicitude under the palm tree, told to eat, drink, and be comforted when her labor is upon her (19:23–26). The pattern is consistent enough to be called a doctrine: in God's administration, the consolation of the afflicted is never a by-product of the plan. It is in the plan.

**Knowing After Living**

The verse's third clause carries an epistemology: she would know that Allah's promise is always true. At the riverbank she was asked to believe the promise; here, holding her son under the tyrant's roof, she knows it — and the difference between those two states is the whole distance this verse has traveled. Knowledge of God's truthfulness, in the Qur'an's usage of this scene, is not the conclusion of an argument but the residue of an experience: a promise made, endured, and kept leaves behind a settled certainty that no argument alone could have installed. The order matters, and the verse preserves it — first the promise, then the obedience inside fear, then the knowledge. Had the sequence been reversed, faith would have been a calculation. As told, it is a testimony she earned with a night beside a river and a morning of silence in a hostile city.

**"But Most People Do Not Know"**

The verse ends by closing its own circle: most people do not know. The clause is a verdict on the spectators of this whole episode — the palace that never learned the nurse was the mother, the servants who handled the deliverance as routine, the neighbors who saw only a foundling's luck. Every one of them stood inside the working of God's promise and read it as circumstances. The commentators took the sentence as a general statement about mankind's inattention to what God is doing in ordinary events, and the placement proves the point rather than merely asserting it: the reader has just watched a promise kept through a baby's appetite, a girl's nerve, and a tyrant's payroll, and would have missed it in real time. Only the verse's narration makes the machinery visible. That is why the Qur'an tells stories to believers — not because the events are hidden, but because the pattern is, and the pattern is the knowledge that most people, including most beneficiaries, never collect.

**The Line of Knowledge Between Two Houses**

The verse splits its own cast into two kinds of knowers. On one side stands the mother, whose knowledge was purchased — a promise believed in terror, kept in silence, and then confirmed in her arms until she knew it was true.
