On the other side stands the palace, which handled the same events and remained exactly where it started: unaware (28:9), unseeing, certain that its household had acquired a foundling. Nothing about the events themselves distinguished the two houses; the basket, the hunger, and the nursing contract were visible to both. What differed was the capacity to read them — the mother read because she had been given the promise, and the palace could not read because its whole way of seeing had no category for God's action in the world. The closing verdict — most people do not know — therefore draws a line through the story that no observer standing inside it could see. The sūrah itself is the instrument that moves its reader across that line: by telling what the palace could not see, it trains the believing audience in the reading skill the verse says most people lack. Education, in this chapter, is not the delivery of facts but the transfer of sight, and the mother of Moses is its first graduate. The same asymmetry will govern the whole chapter: Pharaoh's court will watch the plagues without learning, while a believing man inside his own council will see enough to stake his life on what he saw. Sight, in this sūrah, is distributed according to what the heart will do with it — and the mother's heart, tied by God, had already proved what it would do with everything it was given.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:14
> And when he reached full strength and maturity, We gave him wisdom and knowledge. This is how We reward the good-doers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Arrival Measured Twice**

The verse marks Moses' crossing into adulthood with two verbs, and the distinction between them is the classical commentary's first concern. He reached full strength — the word for the prime of bodily life — and he became settled, a verb whose range runs from mounting a throne to being on an even keel. The commentators gave the second verb an inward reading that deserves its weight: the stage at which a person stands balanced between hope and fear, between humility and dignity, between dependence and autonomy — the equilibrium in which judgment becomes possible at all. On that reading the verse describes a double maturity: the body's, and the soul's footing inside it. A strong man without settledness is only armed; the verse insists on both together. The genealogy of the verse's own syntax makes the same point — strength comes first, but the gifts come after the settling, as if the text were teaching that readiness for weight is a state, not an age.

**Forty, the Prophets' Hour**

The tradition fixed the numbers anyway. The commentators report thirty-three years for the first term and forty for the second, and forty is the age the Qur'an itself ties elsewhere to spiritual adulthood, in the verse commanding gratitude to parents: “when the child reaches their prime at the age of forty” (46:15). Forty is also, in Muslim tradition, the age at which Muhammad received the first revelation, and the pattern was read backward into the prophets generally — the age at which a man's judgment can carry a message. The precision should be held honestly: the commentators themselves disagreed on the split between the two terms, and the verse does not state a number. What the number-40 tradition preserves is the pattern — a long, unremarkable maturation before commission, prophethood arriving not to teenagers but to settled men. Moses at the burning bush will be a grown man with decades of formation behind him, and this verse is where the Qur'an notes the formation's completion.

**Gifts Given Before the Mission**

What he receives is wisdom and knowledge, and the commentators split the pair exactly as the sentence invites. Wisdom here is the sound judgment possessed by a prophet even before investiture; the knowledge is understanding in religious matters — some said the tradition of his Israelite forefathers, others the capacity to judge between people. Two features of the gift deserve marking. It is given, not achieved — We gave him — so the verse refuses the romance of the self-made sage; and it is given before the mission, as equipment rather than wages. One commentator pressed the point to its limit: this verse cannot refer to prophethood itself, because prophethood in Islamic theology is never conferred as a reward for merit — it is an appointment, made by God's choice, not a prize earned. The gifts of verse 14 are the human excellence that appointment finds and uses, and their placement in the story is the argument: first the man, then the message.

**The Same Payment Joseph Received**

The closing formula — This is how We reward the good-doers — is one of the Qur'an's refrain-verbs, sounded at the close of several prophetic stories, and its most striking repetition is in the previous sūrah's account of Joseph: “And when he reached maturity, We gave him wisdom and knowledge. This is how We reward the good-doers.” (12:22). The near-identity is deliberate. Joseph — sold, enslaved, imprisoned — and Moses — hunted at birth, exiled by a killing — arrive at the same station by the same road: trials borne without corruption, followed by wisdom and knowledge as the recompense. The refrain also folds the mother back into the reward, since the commentators heard in the good-doers her endurance by the river; her son's formation is the verse's payment to her. The template the two verses share is the Qur'an's standing answer to the question of what virtue gets: not immunity from the trial, but a self equal to what comes after it.

**Thirty Years in Half a Verse**

Watch the compression. Between the nursing infant of verse 12 and the mature man of verse 14 lie three decades — the whole of Moses' childhood, schooling, and rise inside the Egyptian court — and the Qur'an spends half a verse on them. The same compression will govern the rest of his story: years of shepherding in Madyan will pass in a clause, and the eight or ten years of his contract with his father-in-law will be given as terms of employment, not as narrative. The omission is deliberate and it carries a doctrine. Formation matters to this Book, but formation is not its subject; the hour of appointment is, and everything before it is summarized as the arrival at readiness. The believer is being taught how to understand the unreported middle of every life of faith — the decades that no verse will mention, the growth that happens without signs and without applause. God's giving in this verse comes at the appointed hour and not before: the gifts of wisdom and knowledge arrive when the man is settled enough to hold them, and the story's rhythm implies the promise behind the rhythm — what God is building is never late. A community waiting under persecution, whose own deliverance had not come, heard in that compressed verse the license for their own long middle: the silence of the years is not the silence of abandonment. Read the verse beside its neighbors and the rhythm stands out — a feeding in one line, three decades in the next, a killing the line after. The Book moves at the speed of meaning, not of clocks, and its reader learns to do the same: measuring a life by its crossings, not its calendar.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:15
> ˹One day˺ he entered the city unnoticed by its people. There he found two men fighting: one of his own people, and the other of his enemies. The man from his people called to him for help against his foe. So Moses punched him, causing his death. Moses cried, “This is from Satan’s handiwork. He is certainly a sworn, misleading enemy.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hour the Streets Emptied**

The verse times its scene with care: he entered the city at a moment of heedlessness, which the commentators most often read as midday, when the streets sleep in hot countries. The hour mattered because of who Moses was by then — a man raised inside Pharaoh's own house, identified with the court that owned the city, yet walking into its heart unrecognized. The commentators divided over how the non-recognition worked: some held that he was still understood to belong to Pharaoh's retinue, even called the son of Pharaoh, and the heedlessness lay in the city's stupor at that hour; others held that he had already broken with the court and the people no longer remembered him. Either reading lands on the same fact: the most watched man in Egypt moved through its capital unwatched, because God had arranged the gap in the surveillance. The scene that will change his life happens inside an interval of inattention — the same substance, heedlessness, that this sūrah keeps assigning to the powerful when their plans come apart.

**Two Fighters, Two Nations**

The two men are described in political vocabulary: one from his own faction, one from his enemies. Verse 4 has already shown Egypt sorted into groups by Pharaoh's administration, with one group marked for persecution; here the sorting shows up at street level as a fistfight. The fight recruits Moses by birth — no one asked him, no doctrine compelled him, and the man from his people simply called for help. The moment is universally recognizable: a member of the comfortable caste is summoned by blood into the quarrel of the oppressed, before he has decided anything. Moses answers the call. The commentators note the Quranic wording keeps the enemy's identity generic — "his enemies" as a class — and that the Bible's parallel scene names an Egyptian outright (Exodus 2). The generic word does more work: it makes the fight a photograph of the whole national conflict in miniature, with Moses standing exactly on the line his birth had drawn for him.

**One Blow, an Unintended Death**

What he intended was rescue; what he accomplished was a death. Moses struck the Egyptian with his fist, and the man died — the Quranic phrase says he was finished, brought to his end, and the commentators read the wording with care: Moses did not set out to kill; the strike carried further than he meant. The classical tradition was unanimous in guarding the prophets from the charge of murder, but the more honest reading they preserved is the one the verse itself performs: the death was excess, not intention — help delivered with a force that outran its purpose. Some of the commentators located the wrong precisely there: he could have pulled the oppressor off without the blow that killed. The verse does not hide the death behind the good intention, and Moses' own cry in the next breath confirms that he counted the death to his own account. The scene is the Qur'an's study in how a just cause, handled in anger, produces a corpse — and the rest of the episode will price that corpse exactly.

**"This Is from Satan's Handiwork"**

His verdict on the event names a third combatant: “This is from Satan's handiwork. He is certainly a sworn, misleading enemy.” (28:15). Moses does not blame the Egyptian's oppression, his own anger, or Israel's misery — he locates the fight's true author, the one who had engineered a quarrel so that the palace's favorite would become a killer in an ungoverned hour. The diagnosis carries the lesson the scene was built to teach. Partisanship — the raw summons of my man against their man — is the door Satan uses, because it recruits good people into escalated violence without ever presenting them a principle. Moses, the most powerful young man in Egypt, was managed in one afternoon into blood on his hands by his own side's emergency. He saw it instantly, and named it instantly, and the accuracy of his self-indictment is the first evidence in the sūrah of the wisdom verse 14 had just reported given to him.

**Where the Bible Agrees and Parts**

The parallel in Exodus runs close and then separates. There, too, Moses intervenes for a Hebrew against an Egyptian, kills and hides him, and the next day meets two Hebrews fighting; the Qur'an's second quarrel keeps its combatant unnamed as "an enemy to them both." The Muslim commentators split over that second man — Egyptian again, or an Israelite so corrupt as to count as an enemy — and the Quranic word choice leaves the question open while making a different point than the Bible's. Scripture here is tracing lines of allegiance that are not ethnic only: Satan's party and God's party cut across Egypt and Israel, which is why the same sūrah will produce a believing man inside Pharaoh's court. The verse's function is fixed either way: one death, unjust though its victim was, now hangs over Moses' head in a state that does not weigh an Egyptian's blood against an Israelite's intention. Flight will follow, and the sūrah will insist on recording whose handiwork started it.

**What He Carried Out of the Square**

Inventory what one afternoon cost him, and the scene's weight appears. He carried out of that square a death on his conscience, a fear he would wear through the next day's streets, a debt to the man he rescued — a man who would prove to be a liability rather than a friend — and, once the accusation flew, a name in public circulation attached to killing and to lust for power. Nothing about the good intention discounted any of it. The verse's sequence teaches the proportion the hard way: the cause was just, the intervention was needed, and the excess was still a corpse, because causes do not launder means. For a man who would one day deliver a law to a nation — a law rich with rules of exact retribution, of wounds graded and compensated — the first lesson in his formation was administered here, in the school of his own hand: that helping the wronged requires a measure, and that the measure is owed to God before it is owed to the enemy. He learned it in the worst classroom available, and the sūrah preserved the lesson with the scar showing, because the lesson was the point.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:16
> He pleaded, “My Lord! I have definitely wronged my soul, so forgive me.” So He forgave him, ˹for˺ He is indeed the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Complete Confession**

The plea runs one sentence, and every part of a full repentance is inside it. Address — my Lord. Admission, in the first person and without a witness to blame — I have definitely wronged my soul. Request — so forgive me. No circumstance is entered in mitigation: not the oppression that produced the quarrel, not the man's death being unjust in itself, not the ungoverned hour or the summons of his own people. Moses files the act where it belongs, against his own soul, and asks for what only the wronged party's Lord can give. The commentators observed that the word for wrong here is the Qur'an's word for injustice — he describes himself, the future herald of God's oneness, as having been unjust, and the object of the injustice is himself. A man who was defending his own kinsman confesses the matter as self-harm. The confession's precision is what makes it complete: he does not say he made a mistake; he says he wronged, which is a claim about guilt, not about accuracy.

**Answered Before the Plea Cooled**

The verse's hinge is its smallest clause: So He forgave him. No interval is recorded, no second request, no penance scheduled — the answer is carried in the same breath as the plea, and the closing names carry the reason: He is indeed the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The Qur'an states the principle behind the scene as a standing rule: “Whoever commits evil or wrongs themselves then seeks Allah's forgiveness will certainly find Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.” (4:110). The Prophet's own words carry the same rule into the details of a life: the one who repents from sin is like one who has no sin (Sunan Ibn Mājah 4250, graded ḥasan by al-Albānī). What the verse adds to the principle is the speed. Forgiveness, in this scene, is not a bureaucratic outcome that follows processed remorse; it is the immediate posture of the One whose name the plea invoked. The future prophet learned at the scene of his worst act that his Lord's mercy did not need to be persuaded.

**What Exactly Was Wronged**

The tradition worked at the question the confession raises: in what did the wrong consist, if the man who died was an oppressor and the man who struck him acted to defend a victim? One commentator's answer is the most exact: the wrong lay in the measure of the help — Moses could have freed the victim without the force that killed, and the force was his choice. On this reading the confession prices an excess, not an atrocity, and the pricing still requires the full vocabulary of guilt. The commentators also noted the larger instruction in the scene: it shows the harm of unintended consequences — that a just intervention can carry a death inside it, and that the agent of a just cause owes his own soul an account of proportion. The prophets' scrupulousness is a theme the tradition defends elsewhere; here it is dramatized. A lesser man would have carried the death as the enemy's expense. Moses carried it as his own sin, and the carrying is what the verse rewards.

**The Same Confession Decades Later**

The Qur'an has Moses retell this event once, face to face with Pharaoh, and the retelling is faithful to the confession. Asked about the killing, the older Moses answers: “I did it then, lacking guidance.” (26:20) — an admission that names the same event, claims the act without excuse, and locates the failing where the original confession located it. He adds in the next breath that he fled from them in fear and that his Lord then gave him wisdom and made him a messenger (26:21), which is this sūrah's sequence told in the first person. The consistency across the two tellings is itself evidence for the unity of the account: a fabricated tradition would have tidied the hero's worst hour. Instead, the Qur'an lets the man who will carry God's message to the greatest court on earth stand forever on the record as a man who killed, confessed in one sentence, and was forgiven in half of one.

**A Record Made for Everyone After Him**

Why does the Qur'an preserve a prophet's worst hour in such detail? The answer lies in whom the record serves. A report scrubbed clean would have taught the believers nothing except that they could never measure up; the record as it stands teaches the opposite — that the gate of return is wide enough for the man whose single strike ended a life, and therefore wide enough for anyone. The scene works as a catechism in three lines: the sin named plainly, the pardon granted instantly, and both sealed with two of God's names that belong to the family of mercy. The Qur'an's standing invitation to the despairing runs on exactly this logic — that no one should give up on God's mercy because of what they have done, since He forgives all wrongdoing — and this verse is that invitation dramatized before prophethood, at the lowest point of the story's hero. The commentators' caution about prophets kept them from calling the act murder; nothing in that caution softens the scene's instruction for ordinary believers, which is the speed of the answer. A person who has wronged his own soul stands one sentence away from "So He forgave him" — and the sentence is short on purpose. The record's completeness is part of the mercy: the wrong is not minimized, the confession is not edited, and the pardon is not smuggled in as an afterthought. Every element a sinner will need is present in working order — the naming of the act against one's own soul, the request with no bargaining attached, and the answer that arrives before the plea has finished echoing.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:17
> Moses pledged, “My Lord! For all Your favours upon me, I will never side with the wicked.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Vow Tied to Favor**

The verse records a pledge, and its logic runs through gratitude: for all Your favours upon me. The commentators tied the favors to the gifts just listed — the wisdom and knowledge given at maturity — and the inventory easily extends: a life spared by the river, an upbringing inside the enemy's house, a mother restored to him under the tyrant's seal. The vow's structure is the theology of thanksgiving in one line: received mercy is not concluded but converted, transformed into a standing policy of conduct. Moses does not promise God a ritual or an offering; he promises a direction for his allegiance — never again a supporter of the wicked. The pledge therefore has an object, and the object is other people. Gratitude in the Qur'an is measured less in what flows up toward God — who needs nothing — than in what the grateful refuse to do to the creatures the favors came through. A man saved from the mighty vows never to become the mighty's instrument.

**Gratitude Before Any Certainty**

One commentator made a chronology that changes how the verse reads: Moses made this pledge before prophethood, and so before he could have certain knowledge that his plea had been answered with God's own forgiveness. His gratitude was not payment for a settled verdict; it rose from the favors he could count — life, formation, restoration — while his guilt still stood unanswered between him and heaven. That ordering gives the vow its moral height. People pledge fidelity after they know they are forgiven, when gratitude is safe; Moses pledged while the ledger was still open. The verse thus presents thanksgiving as the correct response to favor received, independent of assurance about favor pending — a discipline of counting what one has been given rather than negotiating over what one owes. The man who had just learned, in the same episode, that his Lord forgives at once, answered that learning the only way mercy can be answered: with a change of allegiance, not a receipt.

**The Narrow Vow and the Wide Vow**

The commentators divided over the vow's scope, and both sides deserve statement. Narrowly, the pledge refers to Moses' own position: a man raised in Pharaoh's household, sharing its tables and its power, vows that he will no longer lend that household his strength — the vow as resignation from the machinery of persecution. Widely, the pledge is a principle for life: never to support wrongdoing, whatever house it lives in. The wider reading is the stronger, for a reason the story supplies: Moses' danger was never that he would end up marching in Pharaoh's army — his break with the court was already in motion — but that he would keep acting as his own anger's ally, as the killing had just shown. Support of the wicked begins at the level of one's own hands. The vow covers the palace he is leaving and the impulse he is correcting, and the Qur'an's portrait of him afterward — a fugitive who shepherds for years before he speaks to Pharaoh — reads like the vow being kept in both directions at once.

**The Vow's Echo at the Summit**

Gratitude returns at the highest point of Moses' story, and God Himself raises it. At the commissioning, when Moses is elevated above all others by messages and direct speech, the command that seals the honor is: “So hold firmly to what I have given you and be grateful.” (7:144). The circle closes on the same duty this verse records him volunteering before he was ever commissioned. The beginning of his prophetic career and its exalted middle are bracketed by one instruction — be grateful — first self-imposed, then divinely commanded. The arrangement teaches what the Qur'an consistently holds about the relationship of the two: the gratitude God commands at the summit is not a new demand but the ratification of what the grateful do unprompted at the bottom. Moses' pledge in this verse, made by a fugitive with blood on his conscience, is the same act God will later command as an honor — which is as plain a statement as the Qur'an gives of where obedience and thankfulness meet.

**Allegiance Declared Before Any Office**

The pledge is remarkable for its timing: Moses declares his side before he holds any commission, before a single miracle, before God has addressed him even once. Identity in this Book precedes office — the two parties, the people of God and the party of the wicked, are joined by choice long before the choice carries authority or rank. Moses enlists in the first while holding the second's citizenship, and the enlistment costs him everything the palace offered. The vow also prefigures, with almost schematic precision, the role he will grow into: the man who promises never to support the wrongdoers will spend his prophetic career as the wrongdoers' named opponent, confronting the most complete wrongdoer his age produced. And the next morning shows the vow's fragility in his own hands — summoned again into the same quarrel, he moves to strike, and stops only because the enemy's tongue stops him (28:19). Allegiance declared once does not run on its own; it must be renewed against anger, habit, and the pull of old alliances, which is why the Qur'an anchors the pledge not in Moses' resolve but in the favors it answers. Gratitude, not willpower, is the covenant's engine — a debt that can be remembered when resolve fails. That is also why the Qur'an records the vow at all. A resolution that lasts a day teaches nothing; a resolution anchored in the memory of favors — life, formation, forgiveness in progress — has a foundation that outlasts the morning's failure, because the favors remain true no matter how the man who owes them behaves.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:18
> And so Moses became fearful, watching out in the city, when suddenly the one who sought his help the day before cried out to him again for help. Moses rebuked him, “Indeed, you are clearly a trouble-maker.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Morning After a Killing**

The verse opens on the state the killing left behind: fear, and vigilance — he watched out in the city. The commentators read the vigilance precisely: he was looking to see what consequences the death would produce, whether it had been seen, what the state would do. The pairing of the two words is the psychology of a man with a secret: fear supplies the dread, vigilance the scanning. Nothing in the verse suggests his fear was cowardice; a man afraid of nothing after killing someone in Pharaoh's capital would be a fool, since the state's response to an Egyptian's death at an Israelite's hands would fall on Israel's whole quarter. The verse also lets a day pass without narration — a day in which the death must have been discovered — and the silence prices the interval: Moses spent it inside the city he could no longer trust, at the center of the power he had now injured, waiting for the knock that had not yet come.

**The Same Hand, Extended Again**

Into the vigilance erupts the voice from yesterday: the man he had saved cries out to him for help again. The repetition is the verse's social observation. Yesterday's rescue created an entitlement — to the rescued man, Moses is now the resource that worked, the patron who moves when called — and partisanship does not audit its patron's circumstances before drawing on him again. The man has learned nothing about cost: the fight he initiated yesterday cost a life and put his people's most protected son under a capital threat, and the first thing he does on the second day is reopen the account. The commentators noted the rebuke's grounds lie exactly there — the same man, the same quarrel, the day after the catastrophe. The scene exposes the engine that drove the whole disaster: a pattern of alliances in the oppressed quarter that consumes its own defenders, one emergency at a time, until the defender is consumed.

**A Rebuke Aimed at the Wrong Target**

Moses' answer is a sentence of blame: “Indeed, you are clearly a trouble-maker.” (28:18). The accuracy of the rebuke deserves to be tested, because the verse quietly complicates it. The man was a trouble in the sense that his quarrels kept summoning Moses into the open; but the death that made yesterday dangerous was delivered by Moses' own fist, and the verse has already — in Moses' own mouth — assigned that act to Satan's handiwork (28:15). The rebuke therefore tells only half the truth, and the half it omits is the one about the speaker. The Bible's parallel scene puts a gentler question in the same encounter — why would you strike your fellow? — aimed at the fighter, with Moses as bystander; the Qur'an makes Moses the accused rescuer and lets his irritation land on the client instead. The commentators were careful to note what the verse does not say: that the rebuke settles the guilt. Its function in the story is to show the friendship between the two men fraying under pressure, and pressure, in this sūrah, is the appointed instrument of revelation.

**Fear That Has Not Yet Fled**

What the verse documents, in the end, is a man still present. Moses is afraid, watched, implicated in a death, rebuking his own client in the street — and he is still in the city, still walking in it, still reachable by the next cry for help. The flight that will end this phase has not begun; the sūrah will move him only when the machinery of exposure completes its work. The commentators read this interval as the passage between the private sin and its public discovery, and the verse holds it open for one purpose: to show fear doing its proper work. Fear that scans, fear that rebukes, fear that stays at its post — these are the motions of a conscience still operating inside danger. The pressure has one more turn to make. The next breath of the story brings the threat from the other side of the fight, and the words spoken there will finish what the fist started.

**The Street as Witness**

Everything in this verse happens in public, and the publicity is part of the danger. The quarrel erupts where it can be heard; the rebuke is delivered in the open; the cry for help draws whatever audience the hour allows. For a man whose secret is a day old, every set of eyes is a threat and every raised voice a report waiting to be filed. The verse's economy hides nothing of the quarter's sociology: the rescued man expects service because he was served once, the patron resents the expectation because the first installment nearly killed him, and their exchange is conducted as street theater for whoever is watching. The commentators were careful to note that the man's cries, repeated aloud, were exactly the channel through which yesterday's killing would spread — and this verse is where that channel opens. Patronage in an oppressed community is a public bond with public accounting, and Moses is discovering that the role he accepted cannot be privately held. The protector of one Israelite belongs, in the street's arithmetic, to all of them, and the state's arithmetic will soon agree — for different reasons. Set against the verse before it, the scene also measures what the killing had changed in him. The man of the previous verse entered the city unnoticed and left it indebted; this verse finds him walking it fearful, watched, and publicly bound to a quarrel he no longer controls. The city that never saw him arrive will not let him leave unremarked.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:19
> Then when Moses was about to lay his hands on their foe, the enemy said, “O Moses! Do you intend to kill me as you killed a man yesterday? You only want to be a tyrant in the land. You do not intend to make peace!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Yesterday Spoken Aloud**

The sentence that breaks Moses' cover is a question: “Do you intend to kill me as you killed a man yesterday?” (28:19). Its power lies in its tense. The killing had been private, unwitnessed as far as Moses knew, buried in an hour of urban sleep — and here it stands in the open street, packaged as an accusation, knowledge moving faster than any report Moses could imagine. The commentators traced the leak to the quarrel itself: the argument in verse 18 was loud enough to be heard, and suspicion did the rest. The scene teaches the physics of secrets: a deed done in heedlessness does not stay in the hour of heedlessness; it ripens in daylight until it speaks. The man's phrasing also does something crueler than exposure — it frames the killing as Moses' established character, "as you killed," a precedent rather than an incident. An accidental death, once spoken of this way, becomes a biography, and in a state like Pharaoh's Egypt, biography is enough to sentence.

**Who Spoke These Words**

The verse attributes the speech to their foe — the enemy of the two men — and the commentators' majority read him as the Egyptian or enemy party of the second quarrel. But a minority reading preserved in the tradition deserves its weight: some held the speaker was the Israelite whom Moses had helped, the client of the previous day, turning on his rescuer with the exposure that would drive Moses from the city. That reading explains what the majority reading leaves loose — how the incident traveled citywide overnight, and why both rebukes in the story misfire. It also fits the sūrah's unsparing portrait of the faction Moses was born into: verse 18 showed the client drawing on his patron without shame; this reading shows the same man selling him under pressure. The Quranic wording keeps the man's identity deliberately general — the enemy of them both — and that generality lets the scene make its point without depending on his tribe: the party of Satan's handiwork, named two verses ago, is present on both sides of the fight.

**Jabbār, the Tyrant's Trade**

The accusation's second sentence names the charge: “You only want to be a tyrant in the land.” (28:19). The word is jabbār, and one commentator fixed its anatomy precisely: a jabbār is a human being who commits violence and oppression without regard for consequences and without seeking the right way of doing things — force as method, appetite as policy. The word is elsewhere in the Qur'an a title of God alone, the One who compels creation into its course, and its human use is always an encroachment: man reaching for a prerogative not his. That is the exact anatomy of Pharaoh's Egypt — verse 4's sorted population, culled sons, enslaved labor — and now the accusation is spoken, of all people, against the man running to pull an oppressed Israelite out of a beating. The vocabulary of tyranny thus circulates in the story like stolen goods: the party that practices it deploys it as an accusation, and the accusation lands, because Moses did kill a man. A true fact, stolen from its context, is the oldest weapon of the guilty.

**Accusation as Confession**

The verse's irony completes itself: the faction that enslaved a nation, murdered its sons and kept its women now instructs its rescuer on the difference between tyranny and peace. The charge describes the accusers with such precision that the sentence functions as their confession — they know what wanting to be a tyrant in the land looks like, because they are its civil service. And Moses does not answer. The silence is itself the evidence of the charge's failure: a man building tyranny argues, negotiates, consolidates; a man caught in an intolerable position has nothing to say that the street will hear. The verse leaves him mid-crisis, his cover gone, his name attached in public speech to a killing and a lust for power, his client's faction a witness against him. What remains is exit. The next verse of the story will send a runner from the city's edge — and the reader who has followed the irony will understand that the man who saved Israel from Egypt is about to be saved from Egypt by a stranger's warning.

**Peace as the Tyrant's Property**

The accusation ends on a word that deserves its own hearing: you do not intend to make peace. In Egypt's public vocabulary, peace is defined by the people who run the country — it means the system as it stands, the quotas of forced labor, the culling of sons, the settled order that verse 4 documented. Whoever threatens that arrangement is, by definition, against peace, whatever his actual object; and Moses, who had just endangered the arrangement's quiet by defending one of its victims, is duly indicted. The Qur'an owns an exact vocabulary for this swap: it calls the setting of things right iṣlāḥ — reform, making good — and their opposite fasād, corruption, the charge verse 4 already entered against Pharaoh himself. The scene shows the two charges trading places in the street: the land's actual corrupters indict the reformer as a corrupter, and the peace of the graveyard is cited against the man disturbing it. Every reformer the Qur'an narrates stands in this dock — Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, all told they were breaking the people's settled way — and Moses' turn at the bar comes years early, for the crime of stopping a beating. The accusation misfires on the facts but succeeds on the street, and the sūrah's honesty about that success is part of its teaching: under tyranny, the truthful man is processed as the troublemaker long before the truth arrives to overturn the court.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:20
> And there came a man, rushing from the farthest end of the city. He said, “O Moses! The chiefs are actually conspiring against you to put you to death, so leave ˹the city˺. I really advise you ˹to do so˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Runner Outpaces the Council**

The verse opens with motion: a man comes rushing from the city's farthest end. The distance is the point — the warning travels faster than the process it warns about, because councils move at the speed of procedure while urgency moves at the speed of legs. The men the runner names are the malaʾ, the notables — the word the Qur'an uses for the leadership class that convenes against its prophets with grim regularity: the council around the Queen of Sheba, the chiefs of Moses' own later opponents, the assembly around Hūd and Ṣāliḥ, the notables of Shuʿayb's people, the courtiers plotting around Joseph's king. The class is consistent across the Book: not the mob, which can be moved, but the committee, which decides — landholders, priests, courtiers whose position depends on the arrangement a prophet has come to disturb. In this verse the committee has reached its verdict — they are conspiring to kill Moses — and the only counter-institution in the story is one anonymous man moving at speed through the city that wants him dead.

**Who Was the Running Man**

The verse leaves him unnamed, and the commentators filled the silence in two directions: some said a believer from Pharaoh's own people, others an Israelite. The first suggestion recalls a later figure — the believing man of Pharaoh's household who, when the court votes to kill Moses at the height of his mission, stands up to argue the case (40:28), introduced there as “A believing man from Pharaoh's people, who was hiding his faith” (40:28). Most commentators kept the two apart, and rightly: the believer of chapter 40 speaks openly in council at the crisis of the plagues, years after this scene, while this runner's act is covert and urgent. What the two figures share is the lesson of the verse: God's protection of His servants travels through people whose names history never asked for. The man is defined entirely by function — he runs, he warns, he advises — and the sūrah grants him four clauses of direct speech and no identity, which is its own commentary on how deliverance is staffed.

**Advice That Costs Something**

The runner closes with the word that defines his act: I really advise you. Sincerity in this scene is measured by exposure — the speaker moves against his own city's council, at obvious personal risk, for a man the state has decided to kill, and asks nothing. The Qur'an treats this quality, naṣīḥah — the will for another's good — as the working currency of its unnamed righteous: the sister on the riverbank, the wife inside Pharaoh's house, and now the runner from the outskirts, each performing a decisive act of loyalty with no institution behind them. The contrast with the malaʾ is structural. The notables have permanence, numbers, and procedure, and they use them to kill; the adviser has legs and nerve, and he uses them to save. The verse thereby completes a pattern this chapter has been assembling since verse 4: every institution of Egyptian power fails Israel, and every rescue of Israel arrives through persons — a mother, a daughter, a queen, and now a stranger running for his life to save another's.

**Flight Before Commission**

Moses receives the warning and the verse ends on the edge of obedience. Nothing supernatural has entered the story yet — no staff, no white hand, no burning bush, no revelation addressed to him. God's care has reached him through a baby's appetite, a sister's watching, a queen's plea, and now a stranger's sprint, and the sum of these ordinary deliveries is the fulfillment of promises made to his mother before he could walk. The flight the runner advises is not disgrace; read within the sūrah's architecture, it is the scheduled next stage of a plan — the same plan that will return him to Egypt years later carrying what this escape cannot provide, the commission and the signs. The verse's placement at the chapter's turn is exact: the city that raised him has voted to kill him, and the man who will defeat that city must first survive it by leaving. What he carries out of Egypt is himself, formed and forgiven — and the road out runs toward the one thing the palace could never have given him.

**One Man Against a Unanimous Court**

A conspiracy advertises itself as unanimity — the notables have resolved, the machinery has decided, the case is closed. The runner's existence falsifies the advertisement. Inside the same city, inside the reach of the same council, lived at least one man who refused to let the resolution stand, and who acted on the refusal at real risk: the news he carried could only have cost him if discovered, and he delivered it anyway. The Qur'an keeps returning to this figure — the dissenter inside the machine. Pharaoh's Egypt will produce a second, bolder one later in the mission, when a believing man of the court argues openly against the vote to kill Moses and is answered with threats (40:28). The trajectory from covert warning to open argument is the story of conscience inside a tyranny: it begins with one anonymous act that costs little, and grows into public testimony that costs everything. The sūrah's realism deserves the credit — it never pretends that whole institutions turned against injustice. Egypt fell with its notables intact; what pierced the system were individuals, almost never named, moving a little faster than the resolution they were disobeying. The deliverance of verse 5 begins here, with one such man's sprint from the city's farthest end.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:21
> So Moses left the city in a state of fear and caution, praying, “My Lord! Deliver me from the wrongdoing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Exile From the City That Raised Him**

The man who owned no city leaves the only city he has ever known. Moses had been Egypt's adopted prince — housed, schooled, and identified with the court — and the verse records him walking out of it in fear, watching over his shoulder, a fugitive from the administration that considered him one of its own. The classical commentators connected the two descriptions — fearful here and fearful in the street scene before — as the same condition carried to its conclusion: he is concerned that Pharaoh's forces will catch and kill him. The completeness of the loss is easy to pass over. In one week Moses lost his standing, his cover, his client, and his country, and the sūrah spends no words mourning any of it. Scripture's economy here is a teaching: what the world took from him was everything except the one thing he would need, and the one thing was not in the city's inventory. He walks out owning a prayer, and the prayer turns out to be the deed to everything that follows.

**A Prayer Shaped Like a Diagnosis**

The plea is narrow and exact: deliver me from the wrongdoing people. Moses does not ask for victory, vindication, or revenge; he asks for distance from a specific category — the people who do wrong. The commentators read the prayer as the language of pure reliance: he had realized that God was his only refuge, and the words carry that realization. The diagnosis is also a confession of his own position — a man who killed one of the wrongdoers' victims' oppressors has learned that he is now, by the state's ledger, one of the pursued. The prayer's narrowness is its strength. It names the danger as people rather than as circumstance, and it hands the entire problem of rescue to the only party who can move him out of a police state intact. What God will give in answer is not a battle but a road, and the prayer does not even imagine the road — it asks only for deliverance, leaving the method to the Deliverer.

**The Same Two Words, Now Earned**

Fearful and vigilant are the exact words the sūrah used for Moses the morning after the killing, and the repetition is deliberate. There the words described a man scanning for consequences; here they describe a man carrying those consequences out of the country. The pair has matured into its full meaning: fear with nowhere to hide it, vigilance with nothing left to guard. The Qur'an's honesty about its hero's condition is one of its unanswerable signatures — the man chosen to speak with God left Egypt terrified, and the text records the terror without embarrassment because the terror is part of the truth being told. A rescue that required no fear would have been a rescue into fable. This rescue is delivered to a man whose fear is real, whose danger is real, and whose prayer from inside both is the only asset he carries across the border.

**The Wrongdoing People, Named Twice**

The phrase "the wrongdoing people" closes the prayer, and the sūrah has taught the reader precisely who fills it: the court that legislated infanticide in verse 4 and the street machinery that exposed a rescue as a crime in verse 19. The label is not rhetoric; it is the Qur'an's legal category — those who place things out of their right station — and it has been demonstrated in policy before it is prayed against. The prayer therefore functions as the fugitive's own verdict on the system he is fleeing: he does not call it the strong people, the foreign people, or the majority; he calls it the wrongdoing people, and the wrong is a matter of record. One chapter later, an old man in Madyan will use the same phrase to welcome him — you have been saved from the wrongdoing people — and the echo will confirm that the fugitive's diagnosis was not trauma speaking. It was the honest name of the thing, and it held true at the other end of the desert.

**Leaving as the First Act of Faith**

Nothing supernatural marks the departure. No vision instructs him, no angel escorts him, and the sūrah does not even say God answered before Moses moved. He left, and he prayed — and the ordering of the sentence lets the leaving carry the faith. Later in his story the command will run the other way: God will send him back into Egypt deliberately, staff in hand, to confront the court he is escaping. The two departures frame his prophetic career: one flight out in fear, one return in authority, and between them the decade that turns a fugitive into a messenger. The verse's placement teaches the pattern before the pattern is visible: with God, movement toward safety is not cowardice when He is the destination of the prayer. The fugitive who prays while walking has already made his exit an act of worship. The prayer's wording also does the traveler's own work: by naming the danger as people rather than as distance, it refuses to treat the desert as the enemy. The wilderness is not what is wrong with his life; the wrongdoing people are — and the verse keeps that distinction clean so that the reader's own flights are rightly aimed. A man who blames his geography never leaves; a man who prays against injustice can cross any wilderness, because the prayer keeps the real adversary in view and the real Deliverer addressed. Nothing in the scene promises the road will shorten; the promise is only that the road is being watched.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:22
> And as he made his way towards Midian, he said, “I trust my Lord will guide me to the right way.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Fugitive With No Map**

The verse exposes the traveler's condition in one clause: he turned his face toward Midian — and nothing else. The commentators filled in the blank precisely: some said he did not know the way to Midian at all; others added that he had no provisions and no money; one reading strips him even of a settled destination, leaving trust as his only luggage. The prayer he speaks matches the inventory — perhaps my Lord will guide me — a plea for direction, not for arrival. The scene is the Qur'an's portrait of the hijrah taken to its barest terms: a man in motion with no compass, whose only fixed point is upward. Egypt's crown prince had crossed into statelessness in a single week, and the verse refuses to soften the arithmetic. What he lost is gone; what he has is the habit of addressing his Lord mid-stride, and the habit is about to be revealed as worth more than the palace.

**Midian, East of the Gulf**

The destination was real geography. Midian — Madyan in the Arabic — lay well beyond Egypt's administrative reach, on the far side of the Gulf of ʿAqabā, in the region classical and historical sources place along the northwest coast of the Arabian peninsula: wells, oases, and pasture lands whose remains travelers can still visit between the gulf and the lava plains. For a fugitive from Pharaoh's police, the logic of the choice is exact — the one direction Egypt's writ ran thinnest was east, across water and mountain. The Qur'an does not lecture on geography; it lets the man's turned face carry the decision. He walks out of the empire's center of gravity toward the zone between empires, where a stranger with no papers could disappear into a pastoral economy that asked no questions. The commentator tradition adds a detail the Bible shares: this was the country of Shuʿayb's people, which made the fugitive's road run toward a prophet's household before either man knew it.

**Guidance as a Relationship, Not a Route**

The word for the request is guidance, and the prayer asks for something larger than directions. In the Qur'an's vocabulary, being guided is the standing name for the whole business of being led by God — through doctrine, through decisions, through history — and Moses uses it here for the most literal possible need: which way is the way. The double duty of the word is the verse's quiet achievement. A reader hears a refugee asking for the road to Midian; the Qur'an hears a servant asking to be kept on the straight path, and the two requests are one in the text because they were one in the man. This is how the Qur'an consistently portrays faith — not as a compartment for the sacred, but as the atmosphere in which a fugitive checks his bearings. The crossing of a desert and the keeping of a covenant are both guidance, and the man who prays for the small thing has already asked for the large thing, whether he measures it that way or not.

**The Hopeful Grammar of the Plea**

The particle of hope — perhaps, or I trust — marks the prayer's tense, and it deserves attention. Moses does not say my Lord will guide me as a man reciting a certainty; he says perhaps, as a man walking into a blank horizon with the only confidence he owns. The commentators noted that this hesitation is not doubt of God but honesty about the servant's sight: from inside the desert, the outcome is unseen, and the verse keeps the future in its proper place — God's hands. Hope, in the Qur'an's architecture, is the virtue proper to exactly this position: knowledge that God guides, joined to ignorance of how. The fugitive who could see the whole plan would need no hope; the fugitive who saw no plan at all would have none. Moses stands between — the promise made to his mother long ago is still running its course — and his perhaps is the sound of a man trusting the Promise-keeper without a peek at the ledger.

**The Right Way Running Through Strange Country**

The prayer's last phrase — the right way — will be answered within verses by a well, two women, an old man, and a marriage contract, which is the verse's own commentary: God's answer to prayers for guidance is usually a scene, not a syllable. Moses asked to be guided, and guidance arrived as a sequence of ordinary events that happened to be perfectly aimed — each one visible only as it occurred, none of them announced. The pattern is the sūrah's standing doctrine of providence, already displayed at the river and the palace, now operating in open desert. For the believers hearing this sūrah under their own pressure, the verse drew a circle around their own unplanned journeys: the road that feels like flight may be formation, and the man who keeps his face turned toward God while everything else is stripped away is not lost — he is being led by the only method that produces the right way: walking, asking, and being answered one mile at a time. The verse's closing position in the day's sequence is also its comfort: the prayer is spoken while the legs are still moving. Moses does not wait for guidance before walking; he walks because he trusts, and the trust is what turns a random track across Sinai into a guided road. Faith, in the Qur'an's picture of it here, is momentum with a compass of hope — and the compass is checked out loud.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:23
> When he arrived at the well of Midian, he found a group of people watering ˹their herds˺. Apart from them, he noticed two women holding back ˹their herd˺. He asked ˹them˺, “What is the problem?” They replied, “We cannot water ˹our animals˺ until the ˹other˺ shepherds are done, for our father is a very old man.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Well as a Crossroads of Society**

The scene at the well is drawn from life as every desert society knew it: a water point, a queue of shepherds, animals pressing forward, and the day's whole economy arranged around the hours when the water is drawn. Wells in this world were more than infrastructure — they were meeting places, marketplaces, and the setting where strangers became guests, which is exactly what this one is about to become. The Qur'an stages the moment with documentary economy: a community of people watering, and apart from them, two women standing back with their flock. The camera angle is the fugitive's own — a man entering a strange country registers the water first, the crowd second, and the anomaly third. The anomaly is the two women waiting, and the verse's whole drama will grow out of that single observed exception. Providence, in this sūrah's method, rarely interrupts a scene; it hides inside one until the right question is asked.

**Two Women Holding Their Ground**

Why were they waiting apart? The commentators offered three grounds and preserved them all: the modesty of avoiding the crush of men at the trough, the weakness of being unable to push a herd forward against the press, and the discipline of not mingling their animals with strangers'. One dissenting reading even reverses the image — that they were holding the crowds back from their own flock. The multiplicity is worth keeping rather than resolving, because all the readings share one floor: the women are managing a difficult public situation with visible self-command, standing their ground at the edge of the crowd rather than abandoning the day's work. Whatever the exact motive, the posture tells the observer what he needs to know. These are not laborers on someone else's errand; they are running their household's essential work themselves, under constraint, with patience — and the household's situation is stated in their own reply: the man who would normally be here is too old to come.

**A Question That Opens a Life**

Moses initiates — he asked them — and the asking is the most consequential manners in the sūrah. A stranger in a strange land has every reason to keep moving; wells are watched, and a foreigner loitering near two unaccompanied women invites suspicion. But Moses sees a problem he might be positioned to solve, and he does the natural, courteous thing: he asks what is going on. The commentators noted the phrasing can be rendered "what is your situation," an inquiry after their state rather than an interrogation. Nothing in the exchange is charming or staged; it is practical — and that is precisely its register in this Book. God's guidance for Moses, prayed for on the road in the previous verse, is being delivered through small talk at a trough. The man who asked to be shown the right way is shown it by noticing who was left out of the queue, and by opening his mouth.

**The Reply That Explains a Household**

The women's answer is a compact sociology: we cannot water until the shepherds drive off, and our father is a very old man. The first clause reports the local custom — the queue belongs to the strong, and the weak wait until it empties. The second explains the exception: there is no man of working age to press their claim, because the head of the house is past the labor. The commentators heard in the words both respect and plight — the old man's age is mentioned without complaint, but the arithmetic underneath is plain: two daughters are carrying a ranch's watering on the hours the men leave them. The Qur'an lets the reply stand as the women spoke it, and its dignity is part of the characterization: no plea for help, no performance of hardship — information, offered to a stranger who asked. Moses will answer the information with work before anyone requests it, which is why the next verse's charity reads as character rather than courtship.

**The Last Household a Stranger Meets**

The scene completes a sequence the sūrah has been assembling since the riverbank: every turning of Moses' life has arrived through an unguarded moment of ordinary life — a basket on the water, a street fight, a runner at the city's edge, and now a queue at a well. The pattern has a name in this chapter's theology: God's appointments wear work clothes. The fugitive looking for the right way has been guided, with documentary precision, to the one trough in Midian where an old prophet's daughters need exactly what a strong stranger's hands can give — and the meeting that will produce his marriage, his vocation, and his decade of formation begins as an observation about a queue. The verse's final placement in the sequence also restores what the flight from Egypt took: Moses is again among people, again useful, again addressed and answering — a man re-entering the human economy at its lowest, most ordinary door. The well's geography completes the teaching. Every station of the flight has been a social threshold — a palace door, a city gate, a desert track — and each threshold has sorted the mighty from the servants. Moses passed the palace door as its son and left it its enemy; he passes the well's edge as nobody at all, and is received as its future. The thresholds of God's plan have never once been located where the powerful keep their gates.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:24
> So he watered ˹their herd˺ for them, then withdrew to the shade and prayed, “My Lord! I am truly in ˹desperate˺ need of whatever provision You may have in store for me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**His Strength's First Lawful Work**

Read the clause against the sūrah's last sight of Moses' strength in action: a fist that killed. Here the same body, the same power, works for the first time in the story on the side of mercy — he watered their flock for them, unasked, uncompensated, and finished. The commentators noted the plain sense: a strong man in that social setting could do what the two women could not, move animals to water past the press of shepherds. The popular expansions — a rock ten men could not lift — were circulated in some commentaries and dismissed in others as embellishment; the Qur'an itself needs no enlargement, because the act's meaning is already complete. One blow in Egypt made him a fugitive; one hour of work in Midian makes him a son-in-law. The contrast is the sūrah's quiet instruction on strength: the same force that ruins when aimed by anger blesses when placed at the service of the weak, and the distance between a killing and a kindness is exactly one redirection of the arm.

**The Shade and the Turn**

The work done, he turned toward the shade — the tree's shadow, at what the commentators took to be the heat of midday. The motion is worth pausing on because the verse has already used it: he turned his face toward Midian on the road, and now he turns toward the shade at the well. The man's whole condition is written in his turnings — out of Egypt, toward the unknown, and now, at the end of his strength, toward the nearest shelter. Into that shade goes the prayer, and its wording is the poorest and richest in the sūrah: my Lord, I am truly in need of whatever good You send down to me. The commentators identified the immediate referent without hesitation — Moses was hungry, days out from Egypt with no provisions. The pharaoh's adopted son, who ate at the tables of the wealthiest court on earth, ends his second week of freedom starving under a tree, and the verse does not blink.

**FaQīr: The Grammar of Need**

The key word is faqīr, the state of dependence and poverty — used in the Qur'an both for material want and for the soul's standing before God, and Moses applies it to himself in the first person. The prayer's structure repays attention: he does not name what he needs. No bread, no shelter, no protection, no destination — need of whatever good You send down, addressed to a God who knows the inventory better than the beggar does. The commentators heard in the open clause the full condition of the creature: the man is not reporting a shortage; he is declaring a relationship — dependent, on watch, dependent on the Sender for the definition of "good" as much as for its delivery. The prayer is therefore the exact opposite of Egypt's theology, where Pharaoh proclaimed himself the source of his people's goods. At the well of Midian, the true source is named by a starving prince in one clause, and the naming is itself the provision — because the next verse's invitation is already walking toward the shade.

**The Shepherd's Curriculum**

The Prophet said: Allah did not send any prophet but that he herded sheep — and when his companions asked whether it applied to him too, he answered that he had tended the sheep of the people of Makkah for a few qīrāṭs, a small wage (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2262). Moses is the hadith's standing illustration: the man who will shepherd a nation out of an empire learns the trade on an old man's flock in Madyan, for wages of room and bread. The curriculum is not accidental in this Book. Shepherding trains precisely the muscles a prophet needs — patience with the slow, protection of the weak from predators, endurance of solitude, and authority exercised without wealth or force. David and Solomon receive the same training in the Qur'an's summary of their story. Moses' decade with the flocks of Shuʿayb's household — the tradition identifying his host with that prophet — is the long middle of his formation, and it begins with one hour of unpaid watering at a trough he was never required to visit.

**The Prayer Before the Answer Walks Up**

The verse ends with the prayer still on the air, and the next verse's first clause — one of the two came to him — is the answer in motion. The structure is the sūrah's doctrine of provision rendered as timing: the request for whatever good is followed, within a breath of the telling, by an invitation that carries food, shelter, family, and vocation in a single sentence. Nothing in the interval is miraculous. The woman walks, speaks, and Moses follows her — and the whole apparatus of his future arrives through the ordinary mechanics of a household paying its debt. The believers who first heard this verse, poor and pursued in Makkah, were being shown the ledger they lived under: the God who feeds prophets through old men's flocks had not changed his methods, and a prayer said honestly under a tree had never once gone unanswered in kind. The verse also completes the day's moral arc in a single frame: labor, then shade, then prayer. The sequence is the Qur'an's whole ethic of provision compressed into an afternoon — the hands work first, the trust speaks second, and neither replaces the other. A prayer without the watering would be begging; a watering without the prayer would be forgetting. Moses does both, and the shade between them holds a man who has just discovered that being empty-handed before God is a full-time dignity.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:25
> Then one of the two women came to him, walking bashfully. She said, “My father is inviting you so he may reward you for watering ˹our animals˺ for us.” When Moses came to him and told him his whole story, the old man said, “Have no fear! You are ˹now˺ safe from the wrongdoing people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Invitation That Arrives Walking Bashfully**

The verse follows the messenger before it delivers the message: one of the two women came to him, walking bashfully. The adverb is doing real work — it describes a gait that keeps its distance from every impropriety, and the tradition read it as the walk of a free woman managing a public approach to a strange man with modesty intact. The wording joins a hadith the Prophet himself spoke about this very scene: he described Moses as an extremely shy man who covered himself completely, and told how God once cleared him of slander when a stone ran off with his clothes and he chased it unclad before his people (reported in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim from Abū Hurayrah). The Prophet's Moses is the same man this verse shows — shyness as a constitution, not a mood — and the daughter who approaches him carries the matching quality. The invitation is issued by the shy to the shy, at arm's length, in daylight, for a stated and lawful purpose: wages owed for work done.

**A Debt Paid in Person**

The stated reason for the summons is payment: so he may reward you for watering for us. The old man does not send for a beggar; he sends for a creditor — his daughters' work was done by a stranger, and the household's honor requires that the debt be settled face to face. The economics are small and the ethics are exact, and the Qur'an frames the whole next stage of Moses' life inside this transaction: a man who asked for no wages is summoned to receive them. The structure repeats the sūrah's established method — Moses' interventions are never solicited by the beneficiaries in advance, and the reward always arrives from a hand he did not foresee. He watered because the women were stuck, and the watering will return to him as shelter, employment, a wife, and a decade of formation. The verse's first clause is therefore a miniature of the doctrine the whole story teaches: give without demanding, because the Ledger above is never out of balance for long.

**The Whole Story, Told to a Stranger**

When he came, the verse says, he recounted his whole story — and the verb is the root that names this sūrah: he qaṣṣa, followed his own trail aloud, from Egypt to the trough. The act deserves marking. A fugitive's safest policy is silence — no names, no king, no killing — and nothing in the scene obliges Moses to unload his history on a household he met an hour ago. He tells it whole. The commentary tradition connected the disclosure to its effect: the old man's welcome is calibrated to the story — fear not, you are safe from the wrongdoing people — a response that requires knowing what there is to fear. Honesty, in this scene, is not a liability but the key that unlocks the door; the household that heard the whole story could judge the fugitive's case rightly, and judged it as refuge deserved. For a sūrah titled The Stories, the detail is almost self-referential: the telling of a true story is what converts a vagrant into a son-in-law.

**Fear Not: The Borders of Pharaoh's World**

The old man's answer — have no fear, you are now safe from the wrongdoing people — is the first asylum pronounced in the sūrah, and its ground is geographical: Pharaoh has no power in this land. The commentators took the words as the plain fact of the refuge — the reach of the man who killed sons stopped at a coastline and a desert — and the sentence quietly measures tyranny's true size. In Egypt, Pharaoh was a god's peer; one border away, he was a name. Moses' fear, real at the well, is answered not by reassurance about his innocence but by a fact about jurisdiction: the wrongdoing people's writ, vast as it seemed, ran out. The same lesson is the sūrah's gift to its persecuted first hearers — the system that owns your city does not own the earth, and deliverance is a matter of miles as well as miracles. The welcome also comes from a prophet's household, unnamed in this text but identified by the tradition with Shuʿayb, whose own people were being called to God in this same Book: the fugitive has walked out of one prophetic story and into another.

**The Household That Read Him Rightly**

What the old man saw, when the whole story was done, deserves the verse's closing attention: a fugitive who killed under oppression's pressure, confessed it to his Lord, fled rather than feud, and watered strangers' flocks within an hour of crossing the border — the whole record, told in person, without an advocate. The verdict is instant: fear not. Egypt's court and this household processed the same man in opposite ways — the court as a killer with ambitions of tyranny, the household as a guest owed shelter — and the difference was not the evidence but the institution receiving it. A court built on corruption processes truth as threat; a righteous household processes truth as truth. The scene thus sets a compass reading for the decade to come: Moses has landed among people who weigh character by what a man does when no one owes him anything, and in that currency he arrived wealthy.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:26
> One of the two daughters suggested, “O my dear father! Hire him. The best man for employment is definitely the strong and trustworthy ˹one˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Daughter Who Makes the Case**

For the second time in the scene, one of the two women takes the initiative — and this time the initiative is a hiring recommendation made to her own father. The verse records no embarrassment in the structure: the household's employment decision is argued, out loud, by a daughter, and the father proceeds to act on her analysis. In the sūrah's ledger of who acts and who is acted upon, the pattern is now unmistakable: the women of this story move its plot — the mother who released her son to the river, the sister who tracked him and spoke up in the palace, the queen who stayed the first decree, and now the daughter who turns a guest into an employee. The men, by contrast, are mostly institutions — a court, a council, a decree. The Qur'an's casting here was countercultural enough to stay startling, and it is framed not as exception but as competence: her argument is simply better than anyone else's in the scene.

**Strong and Trustworthy: The Complete Employee**

Her evidence is stated in two words that the tradition treated as a full character reference. Strong — because she watched him clear the trough that her household could not; trustworthy — because of his conduct in the hours between the watering and the invitation. The commentators preserved the accounts of that conduct: he walked ahead of her on the path rather than behind, addressing the wall rather than the woman, keeping the interview at the exact distance modesty required — though the same commentators flagged such details as later adornment rather than the Qur'an's own text. The two words she chose became, in Islamic commercial ethics, the permanent specification for anyone entrusted with authority: capacity without honesty is a weapon, and honesty without capacity is a sentiment — employers, judges, and spouses are all to be weighed on both scales at once. The daughter of Madyan stated the specification in a single breath, and the Qur'an recorded her formulation as the standard.

**What the Strangers Saw**

The verse's quiet force lies in its source of information: everything she knows about Moses, she learned in an afternoon, from what he did when nothing was owed. He had no reason to water their flock — he was a stranger, unfed, unpayable; and he had every reason, as a fugitive, to be careless with a woman's dignity on a lonely path — no one in Midian knew his name. Character, in this scene, is exactly what a man does at the point where no record is being kept — and the Qur'an's irony is that a record was being kept: the daughter was watching, and heaven's camera was running. Within days the man Egypt processed as a killer is processed by a righteous household as strong and trustworthy, and both verdicts cite evidence. The sūrah thus teaches its readers how reputation actually travels — not through testimony about intentions but through observed conduct in unguarded hours.

**From Guest to Wage-Earner**

The recommendation is not for charity but for hire, and the distinction preserves something essential about the household's kindness. Moses is not to be kept as an object of pity; he is to be given work — standing, wages, a place in the economy of the family. The suggestion thereby completes the rescue that began at the border: safety was delivered by the old man's welcome, and dignity is delivered by the daughter's proposal. A man fed for free remains a dependent; a man employed becomes a member, and within the chapter's arc a son-in-law. The Qur'an's social doctrine runs through the scene: the community of faith absorbs the stranger through useful work, not through indefinite hospitality — the Prophet's own community yoked its Makkans and Madinans in exactly this pattern of paired support, each emigrant joined to a helper with work and means. Moses' case, in Madyan, is the pattern in its earliest scriptural form: the refugee is hired before he is housed at anyone's expense, and the hiring is what turns exile into residence.

**The Scales That Caught a Prophet**

Her judgment was sounder than she could have known. The man she recommends for a shepherd's wage is the man God has already marked for the greatest mission of the age — and her two-word evaluation turns out to describe exactly the qualifications the mission will use: strength that can carry a nation, trustworthiness that can carry revelation. The commentators' larger point stands behind the scene: God's chosen are recognized among the righteous before they are recognized from heaven, because the qualities of prophethood cast visible shadows — the staff that will swallow sorcery is in the hand of the man a girl just recommended for honest wages. Her father will negotiate the marriage contract within the chapter, the staff will fall to him in the house, and the call will come on a mountain — but the first human being in Midian to identify Moses correctly is this daughter, weighing strength and truthfulness at a well, and finding the fugitive fully solvent. Her recommendation also closes the loop the scene opened at the trough: the strangers who met as inquirer and answerer end the day as advocate and beneficiary, and the household that absorbed neither of them into charity now holds both inside its economy. The Qur'an's preference for competent mercy over sentimental charity could not be staged more plainly — the girl did not ask her father to feed the stranger; she asked him to hire him, because employment is the respect that lasts past supper.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:27
> The old man proposed, “I wish to marry one of these two daughters of mine to you, provided that you stay in my service for eight years. If you complete ten, it will be ˹a favour˺ from you, but I do not wish to make it difficult for you. Allah willing, you will find me an agreeable man.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Dowry Paid in Years**

The old man moves from hiring to marriage in a single speech, and the contract's architecture is one of the Qur'an's most instructive legal sketches: a daughter's hand offered against eight years of service, extendable to ten at the groom's own free choice, with the extra two expressly labeled a favor rather than an obligation. The structure answers a practical problem — Moses owns nothing with which to pay a dower — and solves it in the currency he does own: labor. The father converts his own wages claim into a marriage gift, taking what Moses owes him as an employee and gifting its worth to his daughter as her settlement. Islamic law would later name such arrangements precisely, and the Prophet himself ratified the principle in a reported case when a penniless man asked to marry: he asked what Qur'an the man knew, and married the couple for the sake of what he had memorized (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5087) — the dower measured in what the groom truly possesses. Moses possesses strength and trustworthiness; the old man prices the first and banks the second.

**"I Do Not Wish to Be Hard Upon You"**

The father's speech carries its own fairness clause: the extra term is voluntary, the burden is disclaimed, and the eight years are described as the baseline, not the bargain. The commentators read the disclaimer directly against the length of service — he was saying that whatever the term, his demands within it would be reasonable, the request of a man who knows he holds every card and refuses to play them. The scene thereby models negotiation under unequal power, the situation every contract in every century actually lives in. A fugitive with nothing cannot bargain with a rich elder; the elder knows it, and so builds the protections into his own offer — the ceiling of eight, the favor of ten, the open door of refusal. The Qur'an's contract culture starts here: the strong party's restraint is not charity but law, and the man who holds the power is the one responsible for making the terms bearable. Shuʿayb's household writes the template a generation of jurists would follow.

**The Terms of a Prophetic Decade**

The length of the term deserves its own reading. Eight years — or ten, as Moses' choice will make it — is not a sentence but a formation, and the verse's placement in the sūrah shows the design: between the fugitive at the well and the messenger on the mountain stand these years, compressed here into a contract and unfolded across the sūrah's silence. Within them, Moses gains what neither the palace nor Egypt's streets could give him: a wife from a prophet's household, children, the daily discipline of shepherding, and long acquaintance with the very desert he will cross with a nation. The commentators noted the sūrah's arithmetic elsewhere — God's appointed terms are exact — and the marriage term is Moses' appointed term, the space his Lord uses to finish what the river and the palace began. He enters the contract starving and leaves it, two verses later, a husband and father heading for a burning bush.

**"You Will Find Me, If God Wills, Among the Righteous"**

The speech closes on two phrases that carry the old man's theology in miniature. If God wills — the inshāʾ Allāh that binds every promise to the divine permission, spoken by a man whose household lives on that reliance; and among the righteous — his own self-description offered as testable fact: stay with me, and check.
