Both idioms belong to the same trust the daughters showed at the well — this is a house where God's will is the working assumption and righteousness is a family trade. The commentaries also noted what the offer implies about the offerer: only a father who has watched the candidate's character can make it, and the marriage of a prophet's daughter to a prophet in formation is transacted between two parties who weigh the same currency — strength and trustworthiness, priced at a well. The daughters' report, the father's observation, and Moses' record have converged, and the contract is the receipt.

**The Silence of the Daughter's Consent**

One legal fact rests quietly under the scene: the marriage is proposed to Moses, but it is a marriage to one of the daughters, and the Qur'an records no consultation because it does not need to — the same daughter who diagnosed the hire has been the household's voice throughout, and the tradition took her as the natural party to the offer. The sūrah's narrative economy keeps its camera on contract and character rather than courtship; the romance is conducted entirely in the grammar of work, wages, and worth. What the scene teaches about marriage, ahead of its law, is its order of operations: the match is made on observable character, the terms are stated aloud, the burden is capped by the stronger party, and God's will is invoked over the whole. Marriage, in this first full Qur'anic portrait of its making, is a covenant between households verified by evidence — and the evidence in this case was gathered at a trough, by a woman whose judgment her father trusted enough to build a decade on. The offer's scale, measured against Moses' holdings, is worth the last word: he owns nothing but a record and a prayer, and the old man's contract treats that inventory as sufficient — the record, told whole, proved the character; the prayer's God was already guaranteeing the rest. Ten years, a daughter, and a household are the opening balance of a man who walked into Midian with empty hands one morning before.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:28
> Moses responded, “˹Then˺ it is ˹settled˺ between you and I. Whichever term I fulfill, there will be no ˹further˺ obligation on me. And Allah is a Witness to what we say.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Fugitive Who Negotiates**

The answer is a contract clause, and its first lesson is the man delivering it: the refugee with nothing negotiates anyway. Moses accepts the offer — but he adds a term of his own, and the term is the whole speech: whichever of the two terms I complete, let there be no blame and no injustice toward me. The commentators noted the key word's range — ʿudwān names both aggression and injustice — so the clause protects him from either party's overreach: no guilt for taking the shorter term, no penalty for declining the longer. The man who has lived under a system where the strong rewrite terms at will builds the protection into his own contract's text. Slavery in Egypt was, at bottom, the impossibility of saying no to a term; Moses' first act of freedom is to say, in the plainest words available: the contract binds only as written. The fugitive who learned law in Pharaoh's courts writes his liberation's grammar in one sentence at a well in Madyan.

**The Two Terms and the Freedom Between Them**

The clause's content matches the father's offer exactly — eight years certain, ten voluntary — and Moses converts the option into mutual security: if he completes either term, the account closes clean. The commentators observed what the structure accomplishes: the father's hope for ten years is preserved, the groom's right to leave at eight is secured, and neither is hostage to the other's later mood. The arrangement is the Qur'an's contract ethics in miniature — obligations defined, exits priced, discretion honored, and every contingency named before the handshake. It also carries a personal edge worth noticing: Moses insists that completing the shorter term must leave him whole, which is the language of a man who has just spent a decade inside arrangements that kept moving — Pharaoh's household made him its son and then its suspect in the same breath. His insistence on bounded terms is not pettiness; it is the scar tissue of the betrayed, sanctified into law.

**God as Witness Over What We Say**

The seal on the contract is a sentence of theology: God is Guardian over what we say. No ink, no scribes, no institutions — a witnessed oral agreement between two believers, with the Witness named aloud. The commentators took the naming as the highest enforcement available: a party who cheats knows he cheats before God, and the relationship of trust the contract requires is anchored beyond either man's reach. The scene thereby displays the bedrock of the Qur'an's commercial order centuries before its jurists systematized it — that contracts are, at bottom, testimony, and their binding force lives in the presence of the One who hears every clause. It is worth marking who teaches this in the scene: a fugitive murderer with no standing and an old man with every advantage, transacting at a well — the exact social margin where written law never reached. The Qur'an's answer to power imbalance was never silence; it was a third Party at the table.

**A Decade Signed Away, Freely**

What Moses signs, in substance, is his twenties and thirties: eight to ten years of shepherding, marriage, and exile, traded for a wife and a household — and the sūrah will pass over the whole term in a clause, because the contract itself is not the story; the man the contract produces is. Still, the signature's frame deserves its weight: this is the same man who fled Egypt rather than serve a corrupt order, and he now voluntarily binds himself to service — the difference is the terms, the justice of the counterparty, and the witnessed fairness of the exchange. Service under a righteous elder is formation; service under Pharaoh was consumption. The Qur'an will later show God referring to this whole period with the tenderness of an employer's ledger — the years Moses served, the flock he tended — and the contract at the well is where the ledger opens. The fugitive finally has terms, a table to sign on, and a Witness who does not lose the paperwork.

**The Witness and the Legal Imagination**

The closing sentence — God is Guardian over what we say — deserves a second hearing for what it assumes about the two speakers. Neither party treats the invocation as decoration; the sentence functions in the scene exactly as a seal functions on a document, converting an agreement into an obligation that outlives the afternoon's moods. The commentators observed that the formula makes breach of the contract a religious matter before it is a civil one: the wronged party need not sue, because the Witness has already taken delivery of the terms. The phrase also answers the question of what happens when memory fails — men forget clauses, interests redraft them, and decades blur the bargain; the Guardian of what was said does not. Moses, whose whole biography has been an argument about unreliable records — the state's files, the street's rumor, the court's verdict — insists, as his one condition, that this agreement be filed where records do not decay. The legal imagination of the Qur'an begins in exactly such scenes: two ordinary parties, one unequal bargain, and a third Party who keeps the original. And the clause's last word — what we say — keeps the contract's evidence at its most primitive and most binding: not signatures but speech, preserved by the only Recorder whose transcript no century corrupts. The contract's afterlife in the sūrah confirms the seal's worth: when the term was completed, neither party cited the other's breach, and the decade ran clean to its appointed end — witnessed bargains, kept whole.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:29
> When Moses had completed the term and was travelling with his family, he spotted a fire on the side of Mount Ṭûr. He said to his family, “Stay here, ˹for˺ I have spotted a fire. Perhaps from there I can bring you some directions or a torch from the fire so you may warm yourselves.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Term Completed**

The contract runs its course in half a sentence: when Moses had completed the term. The commentators differed on which term — some held he served the eight and chose to complete the full ten; others counted ten and then additional years; the sūrah itself records only completion, and its silence lets the point stand: the man who insisted on bounded terms served them out whole. What the decade produced is visible in the clause that follows — he was travelling with his family: a wife from the household of a prophet, children, flocks, and the settled identity of a householder. The stateless fugitive of verse 22 owns a family now, and the road he travels is the road back toward Egypt, which he left in fear and approaches without yet knowing why. The sūrah's method of compression — three decades in half a verse, a decade in a clause — teaches its reader to measure formation, not duration: the term's worth is in what it built, and the building is done.

**A Fire for a Cold Night**

The reported purpose of the detour is beautifully domestic: directions, and a burning branch so the family can warm themselves. The commentators took the words at face value — night was near, the desert is cold after dark, and a traveler's first needs are news and fire. Nothing in Moses' speech hints at revelation; he sees a light on a mountainside and thinks like a shepherd — likely a camp, maybe a guide, certainly warmth. The Qur'an thereby stages the most consequential moment of Moses' life inside the least presumptuous motive available: a man going to fetch a torch for his wife and children. The pattern matches the sūrah's established doctrine that heaven's appointments wear work clothes — the river carried a basket, the street carried a killing, the well carried a household — and now the mountain carries a fire that an ordinary father climbs toward carrying nothing but family need.

**The Mountain With a Name**

The verse names the mountain — al-Ṭūr — and the naming is unusual enough to mark. Elsewhere the Qur'an calls it the mountain or the blessed spot; here, and in the later Makkan chapters that retell the call, it carries a proper name that tradition identifies with the mountain of the Sinai region. The name functions like an old title on a document: the place already belongs to the story before the story arrives on it. The commentators also noted the direction of travel — Moses is passing the mountain on the way back to Egypt, and the geography itself preaches: the man who fled Egypt by its eastern desert crosses the same ground a decade later, formed and family-bearing, and the land between Midian and Egypt is exactly the land Israel will cross in the other direction within the chapter's horizon. The mountain that will receive the Torah stands on the road between the two halves of his life.

**What He Tells His Family**

The instruction — stay here — is ordinary fieldcraft, and its ordinariness is the point: Moses expects to be back within the hour with a firebrand and news. The family waits at the camp unaware that the hour is about to become a epochal encounter and the errand a commission; the verse keeps their ignorance intact inside Moses' own words, so that the reader holds the same dramatic tension the mountain does. The commentators found in the scene a lesson about divine appointments: they arrive masked as errands, and the man who grumbles at the detour may be walking to his life's assignment. Moses' case is the extreme instance — the errand he describes is so modest that the verse's irony is almost visible: he climbs for a torch and descends, forty years later in the story's arc, carrying the light by which his nation will read its covenant. The family will get their fire; the world will get its law; both arrive from the same mountainside in the same night.

**Between Two Homes**

The scene's placement catches Moses at the exact midpoint of his biography — the decade complete, the return begun, the call not yet sounded. He is between households: the family he made in Midian waits below, the people he was born among wait in Egypt, and the man on the mountainside belongs to both and neither. The Qur'an's Messenger meets his Prophet at precisely such midpoints — Jacob at the well of his exile, Joseph at the crossroads of the caravan, Mary at the palm tree between hiding and return. The verse's cold domestic details — a cold night, a fireless camp, a family told to wait — are the textures of that in-between state, and the sūrah's teaching in them is calm: the midpoint is where the call characteristically comes, because the midpoint is where a man has finished being formed and has not yet been spent. Moses climbed the mountain a householder; he is about to descend it a messenger, and the family below will learn that the errand was never theirs. The night's logistics also honor the family the sūrah never names in detail. Moses tells them where he is going and why; he does not vanish into theophany without a word to those whose bread depends on him. The man who will carry God's law to a nation begins his final night as an ordinary head of household managing his people's warmth — and the Qur'an records the consideration as part of the character. The prophets' first duties were never suspended by their second ones; the family waited only for a fire, and received, in its season, a covenant.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:30
> But when he came to it, he was called from the bush in the sacred ground to the right side of the valley: “O Moses! It is truly I. I am Allah—the Lord of all worlds

**Expanded Commentary**

**Called From the Tree**

The fire he climbed toward answers him by name: he was called from the bush — the tree of the blessed spot, at the valley's right side. The geography is recorded with the care of an eyewitness: a specific slope, a specific side, a specific tree, and a voice issuing from it. The commentators held fast to the plain reading — God addressed His servant from the tree, as the verse says — and the earliest tradition wrapped the site in the vocabulary of sanctuary: the ground is holy because the address happened there, not the address because the ground was special. A bush burning without being consumed is the scene as later scripture frames it; the Qur'an's own frame is the call itself, and its first word is a man's name. Before doctrine, before mission, before signs, the event is this: a shepherd on an errand hears himself called by the Lord of everything, and the calling knows him. The God who watched over the basket on the river has been silent for a decade; the silence ends with His servant's name.

**"I Am Allah, Lord of the Worlds"**

The self-introduction is the verse's weight-bearing wall. Not I am your Lord or the god of your fathers — I am Allah, the Lord of all the worlds: the One whose lordship runs over every realm, every people, every order of existence, including the Egypt he fled. The wording stakes the entire mission before it begins, because the claim Moses will carry into Pharaoh's court is exactly this: the kingdom Pharaoh administers belongs to Another. The commentators connected the introduction to the theology it settles — the One who speaks from the tree is not a local spirit of Midian's mountains but the single Lord whose oneness Moses' people in Egypt will be summoned to acknowledge. The introduction also completes a thread the sūrah has kept running since verse 5: the God who willed favor for the oppressed now signs His decree in person. The signature is the mission's credential — Moses acts as nothing of his own; he carries the self-announcement of the Lord of the worlds into the court of a man who styles himself the highest lord of his.

**The Right Side of the Valley**

The details — the right side of the valley, the sacred ground — drew the commentators into careful readings. Some took the right side as simple direction; others heard in it the language of blessedness and honor, the right hand as the side of favor. What both readings share is precision: the Qur'an does not stage theophany in fog. Place, side, tree, and ground are named so that the event is fixed in the world's geography rather than floating in legend — the same documentary habit the sūrah has shown since the riverbank, where a basket, a river, and a palace were located in real space. The sanctity of the ground, repeated in the parallel accounts where Moses is told to remove his sandals because he stands in the holy valley, teaches the scene's register: the ordinary desert has been entered by its Owner. Creation itself holds still for the address — and the shepherd who approached looking for a firebrand is standing, without knowing how he arrived there, at the hinge of revelation.

**God Spoke to Moses Directly**

The Qur'an states elsewhere, in one unadorned clause, what this verse stages: to Moses, Allah spoke directly (4:164). The directness is the event's uniqueness — among all the messengers, Moses is singled out for speech without intermediary, and the later tradition enshrined him as the one God addressed face to face. The theologians of Islam would fight for centuries over the nature of that speech — whether God's word is created or uncreated, an argument that flared two centuries after the Prophet over the Qur'an's own status — and this verse stood near the center of the storm: if the words from the tree are God's speech, what exactly did Moses' ears receive? The controversy belongs to the schools, but its fuel is this scene's plain claim: the address was God's own, not an angel's relay. The sūrah's concern is the address's content and covenant, not its metaphysics — but the fact it records was heavy enough to organize a millennium of theology.

**The Errand That Became an Audience**

The scene's placement measures what God does with ordinary faithfulness. Moses climbed for a torch, and the bush gave him the divine self-disclosure; he told his family to wait, and the wait became the frontier between his two lives. The sūrah's readers, pressed and poor, heard in the placement their own standing hope: God's calls come to the faithful in the middle of their duties — to the man feeding his family, not the man seeking visions. Nothing in the record suggests Moses was hunting an experience; he was hunting kindling, and the difference matters because the call is grace, not achievement. The God of the worlds chose the hour, the tree, and the shepherd — and chose them after a decade of watching Moses serve an old man's flock and honor a contract. The servants who are found working are the ones the Master addresses; the bush on the mountain is simply where the finding happened to ignite. The address also settles, before any instruction is issued, the one question every messenger's mission lives on: not what to say, but who is saying it. Pharaoh will ask by whose authority Moses speaks; the mountain has answered it already, in the Speaker's own words.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:31
> Now, throw down your staff!” But when he saw it slithering like a snake, he ran away without looking back. ˹Allah reassured him,˺ “O Moses! Draw near, and have no fear. You are perfectly secure

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Comes Before the Explanation**

The first instruction of Moses' mission is a bare imperative: cast down your staff. No purpose is given, no outcome promised — the command arrives the way commands to prophets characteristically arrive in this sūrah: obedience first, understanding later. The pattern has been the sūrah's backbone since the riverbank, where a mother was told to release her child into a river with nothing but a promise to hold. Here the same pedagogy is applied to the man at the summit of the story: the messenger of the Lord of the worlds begins his commission by doing something pointless on its face — dropping a stick on holy ground. The commentators read the whole exchange as preparation: before Moses can argue with Pharaoh, he must learn what his Lord's bare word can do with an ordinary object in an ordinary hand. The staff that drops as wood will rise as the argument that swallows an empire's magic.

**He Ran, Without Looking Back**

The most disarming clause in the scene follows: when he saw it quivering like a serpent, he turned and ran, without looking back — the commentators note the idiom's force, he did not even pause to turn around. The man chosen to speak with God sprints from a stick. The Qur'an leaves the fear in the record without smoothing it, and the honesty is doctrinal: prophethood does not manufacture new nerves; it commands the ones a man already has. Moses' terror is also the scene's evidence of reality — a fabricated legend would give its hero composure, but scripture gives him heels. The word for the serpent, jānn, carries its own shadow in the language: the same root as jinn, the concealed orders of creation, so that what Moses sees is not merely a snake but something uncanny — wood behaving as life. He has seen serpents; shepherds have. He has never seen one come out of his own staff, and the violation of the ordinary is precisely what his nerves report.

**Draw Near: The Counter-Command**

The answer to flight is an invitation: approach, and do not fear — you are among the secure. The sequence is the whole of spiritual formation in three clauses: the command that frightens, the flight, and the recall that promises safety not after the task but inside it. God does not first stop the serpent and then call Moses back; He calls him back while the thing still quivers, because the security offered is not the absence of the terrifying object but standing in the presence of its Commander. The commentators parsed the reassurance's grammar — you are among the safe — as a full grant of protection, the same construction the Qur'an uses for the honored guests of Paradise. The man who ran from a staff is told he stands inside the strongest safety in existence: the safety of the One addressing him. Courage, in this scene, is not self-generated; it is transferred, one recall at a time, from the Presence to the person.

**The Staff's Career Ahead**

What Moses sees as terror, the sūrah's later verses will display as instrument. The same staff, cast at the same command in Pharaoh's court, will devour the sorcerers' illusions (7:117), and God's instruction in that hour tells the whole design: cast what is in your right hand, and it will swallow what they have fabricated (20:69). The trembling serpent on the mountain is therefore the audition of the court scene — Moses must first be mastered by the instrument he will master, because a messenger who has not feared his own sign cannot wield it before the mighty. The staff's biography in this Book runs from a shepherd's prop, to a serpent on holy ground, to the wonder that breaks Egypt's magicians, to the rod that splits the sea; and it begins here, in the gap between a man's running heels and his Lord's recall. The verse teaches what every subsequent sign will confirm: in God's hand, the ordinary is a deposited weapon, waiting for the command that arms it.

**The Shepherd Meets the Shepherd**

Beneath the drama, the scene completes an identification that the sūrah has been assembling for two verses: the Lord who calls Himself the Lord of the worlds is addressing a man whose decade has been spent keeping sheep — and the first exercise of the call is handling a stray, of sorts, that will not stay put. The Prophet's words about the prophets' common trade — every one of them herded sheep (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 2262) — find their deepest illustration here: the skills of the shepherd are exactly the skills the mission will demand, patience with the stubborn, protection of the scattered, and the habit of putting the flock's life above one's own comfort. The serpent that spooked him and the recall that steadied him are the first lesson in that second shepherding: from this night forward, the flock will be a nation, the wolves will be Pharaoh's soldiers, and the staff will be everything it turned out to be. The scene's arithmetic of fear deserves the closing word: the object that terrified him and the object he will carry into court are the same length of wood. What changed was neither the staff nor the shepherd; it was the command's author. A thing in Moses' hand is a stick; a thing thrown at God's word is a sign — and the whole distance between superstition and revelation is measured in whose order set the thing moving.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:32
> Now put your hand through ˹the opening of˺ your collar, it will come out ˹shining˺ white, unblemished. And cross your arms tightly to calm your fears. These are two proofs from your Lord to Pharaoh and his chiefs. They have truly been a rebellious people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hand in the Bosom**

The second sign is administered to Moses' own body: put your hand inside your collar and draw it out white, without blemish. The commentators identified the garment's opening — the jayb, the collar slit of the shirt — and noted the parallel account in chapter 20, where the hand is drawn toward the side; the two wordings describe the same gesture, the hand entering at the chest's opening and emerging transformed. The sign's design is exact: not a wound healed, not a limb restored, but purity produced where no defect was visible — skin shining white without the blight that whiteness usually means in affliction. The sign spoke the language of Egypt's own obsessions: a court that read omens in bodies was handed a body that demonstrated the Giver's command over its substance. And it was given first to Moses in private — the messenger sees the proof on his own arm before the audience sees it on the mountain of their pride, because signs are entrusted before they are displayed.

**Draw Your Arm Against Fear**

Between the two signs comes a gesture: press your arm to yourself against fear. The commentators divided on its reading with their reasons on the table — some understood it as the posture of composure, the opposite of the flung-out arms of panic; others as a physical device for steadying the heart, warmth and containment against terror; the fear itself they traced either to the serpent still in view or to the mission now taking shape. The division matters less than the concession all readings share: God instructs His messenger in the management of his own fear, as a master calms an apprentice. The man who ran from the staff is not scolded; he is coached — body first, then nerve. The Qur'an's doctrine of the human being is in the instruction: fear lives in the body's habits, and the revealed path runs through them, not around them. Moses' arms, crossed over his chest, are the first Equipment of the mission — before the staff, before the signs, the posture of a man holding himself in the Presence.

**Two Proofs, Addressed to a Named Audience**

The verse names the signs' destination before the journey is made: two proofs from your Lord to Pharaoh and his notables. The mission's target is fixed at its birth — not Midian's flocks, not the desert's tribes, but the court that kills sons, and specifically its leadership class. The wording also fixes the signs' nature: they are proofs — authorities, in the Qur'an's legal sense — documents of accreditation from a Sender, not spectacles for entertainment. The same pairing of messengers and proofs will organize the whole confrontation: every escalation in Egypt will be an argument in evidence, until the court has no unread document left. The commentators observed the mercy folded into the naming: Moses, who fears the court that holds his old charge of murder, is told in advance that his credentials are addressed to exactly that court. The signs he carries were not left to chance encounters; they were sealed for the men he most fears.

**"They Have Been a Rebellious People"**

The verse closes with the mission's diagnosis: they are a people given to rebellion — the word the Qur'an uses for overstepping due bounds, the consistent signature of this court since verse 4. The diagnosis is delivered as fact, and its tense deserves attention: they have been — the record is long, the pattern established, the corruption documented across a generation of murdered sons. The announcement accomplishes two things at once. It explains why signs are needed at all: a people whose excess has become habit will not be argued out of interest; they must be confronted with evidence beyond their jurisdiction. And it warns Moses what the reception will be: the signs address the rebellion, and the rebellion will answer with the only weapons it knows — denial, sorcery, and the knife. The mission is thus mapped before it begins: two proofs into a court of overreachers, with the outcome promised to the evidence and not to the envoy's comfort.

**Equipment for the Descending Man**

The verse completes the mountain scene's inventory, and the completeness is the lesson. Moses receives a sign in his hand, a sign in his arm, an instruction for his fear, a destination, an audience, and a diagnosis — the whole kit of the mission issued in one sitting, as if the Lord of the worlds were equipping a servant who must leave for a war he cannot yet imagine. The sūrah's structure then moves with him: the next verses give his objections, the answer to each, and the promise of victory — the descent from the mountain is the descent of the plan. The believers who first heard this sūrah, armed with no sign but a Book and a messenger, read in the scene their own commission's shape: their proof was the recitation, their fear was managed by the same divine coaching, and their named audience was the court of Makkah — the same class of notables this verse sends Moses toward. The equipment differs; the Sender, the diagnosis, and the promise do not. The pairing of the two signs also fixed the mission's method: one sign for the eye, one for the hand — a wonder witnessed and a wonder wielded. Moses will not argue Egypt into belief; he will show it, and the showing has already been rehearsed on his own body in the valley's quiet.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:33
> Moses appealed, “My Lord! I have indeed killed a man from them, so I fear they may kill me

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Old Ledger, Opened at the Summit**

Moses stands at the summit of favor — addressed directly by the Lord of the worlds, equipped with two signs — and the first thing he places before his Lord is the street in Egypt: I killed a man among them. The confession he made once in private (28:16) is now restated at the moment of commission, unprompted, as the objection that seems to him to disqualify the mission. The parallel account in chapter 26 records the same disclosure in the same position — they have a charge against me, so I fear they will kill me — which shows how the Qur'an consistently frames the event: not as a sin re-hidden but as a fact re-declared whenever the mission approaches its danger. The commentators read the candor as the mark of the prophets' scrupulousness: a man who could have let the signs speak and stayed silent chooses instead to put his worst file on the table before the mission starts. The servant who confesses without being asked is the servant whose record needs no investigation.

**The Fear, Stated in Its Exact Weight**

The fear he names is specific: they will kill me. This is not anxiety about failure or stage fright before the court — it is the plain arithmetic of a wanted man walking into his victim's nation. Egypt's machinery has a decade-old killing of one of its own on file, with the perpetrator identified in public speech as a tyrant with ambitions (28:19); the mission requires him to stand in front of that machinery and announce the overthrow of its god. The Qur'an's honesty about this fear is part of its proof: a manufactured hero would arrive at Pharaoh's court already fearless, but the Book's chosen messenger arrives with his danger priced in his own words. The commentators noted the sequence in the prayer — the fact first, then the fear — as the anatomy of honest speech to God: state what you did, state what you dread, and let the two sit in the same sentence without excuses. Moses does not ask to be excused from the mission; he simply refuses to pretend the danger away.

**Courage Is Not the Absence of Fear**

The scene's doctrine of courage is built in the gap between the fear and the mission's continuation: Moses names his terror and goes anyway, because the call stands. The Qur'an never administers anesthesia to its messengers — Abraham before the idols, the boy refused by his people, the Prophet told that they will call you a liar — every mission is issued into fear, and the fear is managed, as the previous verse showed, by the Lord's own coaching. The commentators found the same pattern in the reassurances God attaches to the mission: you will not be reached, you are with Me, be you two not weak — each formula is an answer to a fear someone voiced. Courage, in this Book, is a transfer: the servant brings the fear honestly, the Lord returns security, and the mission runs on the transfer. Moses' fear at the bush is therefore not a stain on the commission; it is the commission's first handled item.

**Why God Sends the Man With a Record**

The question underneath the verse is sharp: if God equips two signs for the court, why send the one man whose file gives the court a counter-case? The sūrah's answer is distributed across its whole theology of choosing: God's selections run on formation and fidelity, not on unstained records — the runner from Midian was chosen while herding, the mother was chosen while hiding, and Moses was chosen after the killing, because the killing was confessed, priced, and forgiven (28:16). The mission itself will carry the lesson to Egypt: the man with a death sentence in the state's ledger stands before the state as God's accredited envoy — the reversal of every human calculation about who is fit to speak for heaven. The commentators' caution kept the killing from being called murder, and their further point stands above it: prophets are more anxious over their accounts than ordinary believers are, and the anxiety itself is a rank. The man most afraid of his own record is the man least likely to misuse what he is about to be given.

**The Objection God Does Not Answer — Yet**

The verse ends with the objection on the table and no reply recorded — the answer waits one verse, and it is not acquittal but reinforcement: a brother, a helper, strength for the arm. The structure teaches by silence: God does not dispute Moses' fear or excuse his past; He equips the present. The killing remains on the ledger — the court will cite it, the fear of it remains rational — but the mission is not conditioned on either. This is the Qur'an's standing pattern with confessed sin: the pardon is real (28:16), the scar remains, and the assignment proceeds through both. For every believer who has measured distance between their worst hour and any service God might ask of them, the scene stands as the standing rebuttal: the file is open, the fear is named, and the call stands anyway — answered not by argument but by provision. The verse's economy with the past is also worth marking: one clause carries the killing, with no rehearsal of the street, the blow, or the cry that followed it. The sūrah has already told that scene in full; at the bush, the file is cited, not reopened. Confession, in the Qur'an's handling, has exactly this character — stated wholly once, owned thereafter in a single honest line, and never defended.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:34
> And my brother Aaron is more eloquent than I, so send him with me as a helper to support what I say, for I truly fear they may reject me.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Strength Admitted**

Moses' second objection turns from his record to his tongue: my brother Aaron is more eloquent than I. The admission is precise — not that he cannot speak, but that his brother's speech is stronger where the mission needs it most, before the most formidable orator-class of the age. The parallel in chapter 20 shows the same confession in fuller anatomy: uplift my heart for me, ease my task for me, untie the knot from my tongue so they may understand my words (20:25–28). Moses speaks of a physical impediment in that account — a knot to be loosened — and the commentators treated the stammer as the plain sense of his request. The courage of the admission should be marked: before the Lord who equips him, the messenger does not perform confidence; he itemizes limits. The man who will carry God's word to Pharaoh begins by telling God exactly what the word will pass through — a heart needing strengthening and a tongue needing help.

**Send Him With Me as a Helper**

The request is carefully framed: not send him instead of me — send him with me, as a support who confirms me. The commentators caught the distinction and its theology: prophethood is not transferable, and Moses is not negotiating his office away; he is asking for reinforcement inside it. The word for helper — one who buttresses from behind, in the root's own picture — names the role Aaron will hold for the whole mission: the elder brother, the first believer, the voice that strengthens the arm when the arm shakes. The structure also teaches what delegations are for in this Book: not the dilution of responsibility but the completion of it — one messenger carries the call, the second shores the carry. Moses' specific request — that Aaron support what I say — keeps the order of the mission intact: the speech remains Moses', the confirmation is Aaron's. God will answer by strengthening Moses' arm through his brother (28:35) — the reinforcement lands on the original messenger.

**The Fear of Being Rejected**

The stated ground of the request is the last clause: I fear they will deny me. The commentators separated the strands: the fear of being killed was the body's fear, voiced in the previous verse; the fear of rejection was the mission's — that the message would not be received, that the words would fall short of the audience's armor. The distinction matters because it shows what Moses thought eloquence was for: not persuasion by performance, but clarity sufficient to remove every excuse. A people whose rebellion is habitual (28:32) will deny a weak voice and deny a strong one; the strong one at least denies them their plea of never hearing it clearly. Moses' request is thus an instrument of justice for his audience as much as a comfort for himself — the rejection he fears is exactly the rejection the Qur'an elsewhere holds the deniers accountable for: hearing a plain warning in their own tongue and refusing its meaning. He asks for Aaron so that no Egypt ear can claim the message was unclear.

**Brotherhood as Design**

The answer that follows — We will strengthen your arm through your brother — turns the request into doctrine: God builds reinforcement into the mission's structure from the start. The pairing of prophets is a signature of the Qur'an's design — Moses and Aaron sent together to Pharaoh with the mandate stated jointly (23:45) — and the elder brother's role prefigures every helper God stations beside His servants: not the servant's replacement but the servant's second. Aaron's own greatness should not be missed in the arrangement: he is a prophet in his own right, granted revelation in the mission's course, and the tradition remembers him as the gentle one — the man who held a frightened people together in his brother's absence at the cost of being misread. The commission of the pair teaches what the sūrah has shown in households all along: God's work runs through kinship strengthened by faith — a mother, a sister, a wife, and now two brothers against a state. Egypt's power is institutional; God's answer to it is a family.

**What the Prayer Teaches About Asking**

The verse's lasting instruction may be its form: a servant in direct audience with his Lord, listing limitations without flattery and asking for help without hedging. No ritual wraps the request — my brother is stronger of tongue, send him with me, I fear they will deny — three clauses of plain need. The Qur'an's prayers are consistently of this anatomy: Abraham building and asking, Zachariah asking for an heir in old age, the mother of Moses handing her son to the river — the speech of need is unadorned because the Hearer needs no ornament. And the answers are consistently more than asked: Moses requests a helper and receives, with the helper, invulnerability to the enemy's reach and a promise of victory. The believers who first heard this verse under their own mission's weight took the lesson directly: the permission to list one's weaknesses before God is itself a strength, and the mission never travels on the servant's unaided tongue. The naming of Aaron — by role, by strength, by kinship — also gives the mission its first human face beyond the brothers themselves: somewhere in Egypt, an elder brother has been praying toward the same deliverance, and the mountain's answer will reach him as a summons. God's provision for His servant's request was already in the field; the asking merely identified it.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:35
> Allah responded, “We will assist you with your brother and grant you both authority, so they cannot harm you. With Our signs, you and those who follow you will ˹certainly˺ prevail.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arm Strengthened Through the Brother**

The answer to both objections comes in one plural verb of gift: We will strengthen your arm through your brother. The idiom is the Arabic of aid — the commentators render it plainly, help you — but its anatomy in the scene is exact: the reinforcement requested arrives through the very person requested. Aaron is not sent as a separate mission; he is fastened to his brother as strength to the arm that bears the staff. The dual continues through the verse — both of you are given authority — so that from this moment the mission is structurally one act in two bodies. The Qur'an's portrait of prophetic brotherhood reaches here its permanent form: two men, one mandate, each holding the other up. The commentators observed what the arrangement predicted: the mission's crises will test the pair, not the man — and the mountain scene of the golden calf, decades on, will show Aaron holding a frightened people and being misjudged for it, still inside the same strengthening that this verse installs.

**Authority They Cannot Reach**

The second grant names the protection: an authority given to both, so that they will not be reached. The key word, sulṭān, is the Qur'an's term for binding authority — proof, warrant, dominion — and the same root describes Satan's admitted limit in his last-day confession: I had no authority over you, except that I called you and you answered me (14:22). Authority, in this Book, is always borrowed and always bounded — the question is whose warrant a power carries. The grant to the brothers is the counter-warrant to Egypt's: the state's whole apparatus of compulsion is bounded by the reach of the court's writ, and the verse sets God's given authority against it with a promise of non-reachability. The promise's fulfillment will be literal — Pharaoh's armies pursue to the sea and touch neither brother — and the commentators folded its meaning wider: no harm reaches the one under God's warrant except what He appoints, and what He appoints is never loss. The mission's protection is not the absence of danger; it is the ceiling on danger's reach.

**The Promise of Victory, Addressed to Followers**

The verse's last clause widens the frame beyond the brothers: with My signs, you and those who follow you will prevail. The promise is the first in the sūrah to include a community — the followers, the party that does not yet exist, enslaved in Egypt while the verse is spoken on the mountain. The commentators read the inclusion as the mission's map: Moses is not commissioned to a private contest with Pharaoh but to the deliverance of a people, and the victory is defined as theirs. The means of the victory are fixed in the same clause — with My signs — so that no reader mistakes the weapon: not the brothers' eloquence, not the followers' numbers, but the evidence God supplies. The word for prevailing is the Qur'an's word for uppermost-ness, and it will be demonstrated at the sea, sealed at Sinai, and sung in the sūrah's closing verses. The promise's grammar — given before the first confrontation — is the standing shape of divine pledges: certainties spoken in advance, so that the waiting is faith and not suspense.

**Two Men Against a State**

The asymmetry deserves its arithmetic. Egypt in this sūrah is the complete power: a ruler styling himself god, a machine of administration (Hāmān), standing armies, and a demography of the knife. Against it, the verse deploys two brothers and a bundle of signs — and declares the outcome in advance. The world has never mistaken which side of that ledger counts its soldiers, and the Qur'an wants the ledger read precisely so: the verse's promises are not confidence-building rhetoric but the announcement of a different arithmetic, in which the Lord of the worlds' warrant outweighs conscription. The commentators' earlier notes gather here — the authority is sent down (11:96), the proof is manifest (23:45) — and the pattern holds across the Book: God's victories are consistently staffed by the few and the weak, because the staffing is the argument. When two men prevail over the age's superpower by signs, no reader can credit the arm that won.

**The Answer That Ends the Objections**

The verse also completes a dialogue that began with a confession: two objections — my record, my tongue — and one composite answer — your brother, My authority, My signs, your victory. God never contests the objections' truth; He dissolves their weight. The structure is the Qur'an's standing answer to every servant who measures the mission against his own insufficiency and finds the mission impossible: the measurement is correct, and it is beside the point, because the mission travels on what is supplied, not on what is owned. Moses asked for a helper; the answer gives him a brother fastened to his arm, a warrant the enemy cannot reach, and a victory already addressed to followers who do not yet exist. The next verse sends him down the mountain into Egypt — and the conversation at the bush is never reopened, because nothing in it was left unanswered. The verse's plural grammar — We will strengthen, We shall grant — should also be heard as it was spoken: the royal plural of the One whose assistance is not delegated. Every party in the coming contest will have its backers — Pharaoh his hosts and Hāmān, Moses his brother — but the verse fixes which backing is sovereign, and it does so in the first person of the Lord of the worlds.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:36
> But when Moses came to them with Our clear signs, they said ˹arrogantly˺, “This is nothing but conjured magic ˹tricks˺. We have never heard of this in ˹the history of˺ our forefathers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Court's Double Defense**

The reception is delivered in two sentences, and together they are the complete defensive architecture of a court cornered by evidence. The first sentence discredits the signs: nothing but invented sorcery — an explanation that accounts for the phenomena while refusing their source. The second sentence discredits the message: we never heard this from our forefathers — an appeal to inheritance as the measure of truth. The commentators treated the two answers as two registers of one refusal: the court cannot deny that something happened, so it denies the classification — magic, not miracle; novelty, not revelation. The design deserves naming because it has never gone out of use: when evidence cannot be denied, its source is relabeled; when doctrine cannot be answered, its novelty is itself made the charge. Moses' signs were clear — the verse says so — and their clarity is exactly what forced the court into explanations this flimsy.

**"We Never Heard This From Our Forefathers"**

The second defense is the verse's lasting exhibit, and the Qur'an hunts it across the Book: when it is said to them, follow what God has sent down, they answer, we follow only what we found our fathers doing — even if their fathers understood nothing and were not guided? (2:170). One commentator made the verse's function explicit: it is a standing condemnation of blind conformity to ancestral belief, the argument that truth expires at the horizon of inherited memory. The reasoning's flaw is arithmetic: every doctrine now old was new at its beginning; every forefather honored by this court received his rites from someone who first introduced them. The Qur'an's own word for the load the ancestors carried is burden — the imagery of men carrying their loads on their backs (6:25 area) — inherited wrong honored precisely because it was inherited. Against it the verse sets Moses' claim: guidance is judged by its source and its proof, not by its age in a family's memory. The court's forefathers-argument measures truth with a clock; the verse's answer is that clocks do not think.

**Fabricated, Because the Source Was Refused**

The first defense drew a precise note from the commentator tradition: the signs were called fabricated because in the court's eyes Moses lied about their source. The logic is worth stating plainly, because it is the logic of every denial: if there is no God who sends signs, then the man claiming signs is either deluded or lying, and the phenomena must be assigned to human craft — sorcery. The classification then does double duty, explaining the evidence and poisoning its messenger in one word. The Qur'an's counter runs through the whole confrontation: Egypt's own sorcerers, the professionals of that very craft, will be the first to break ranks and declare the source real — falling prostrate before a sign they know is not their trade. The court's label, in other words, could not survive contact with the craftsmen it named. Denial's vocabulary always carries this weakness: it must explain the evidence better than the explanation it refuses, and invented sorcery never did.

**The Same Court in Every Age**

The verse's two defenses became the Qur'an's permanent inventory of rejection, and the sūrah's own audience had heard both recently in Makkah: the recitation called sorcery, and its message dismissed as what the ancients said or did not say. The commentators noted the pattern's reach — Abraham's people claiming they found their fathers doing thus (21:53), the general verdict that none believes a warning unless he follows the way of the ancients. The persistence is the lesson: reception of truth is not primarily an information problem. Egypt's court had the signs in front of them — clear ones — and answered with a category error and a genealogy check. The verse thereby prepares its readers for their own missions' reception: the first defense will be against your evidence's source, the second against your evidence's novelty, and neither will be an argument. The messenger's job, as the next verse shows, is not to win the exchange but to place the criterion on record before the court that refuses it.

**Signs Delivered, Refused, and Stacked**

What the verse records, structurally, is the beginning of the confrontation's evidence pile. Moses came with Our signs — the staff and the hand, delivered as credentials from the Lord of the worlds — and the court's answer spent no examination on the signs themselves: no request for method, no test, no debate with Aaron at his brother's side. The refusal was total and instant, which is why the escalation that follows — the plagues the Qur'an counts elsewhere — proceeds by addition: sign upon sign upon sign, each one removing the last excuse for the last refusal. The verse is thus the first entry in the court's indictment, the moment when the evidence arrived and was classified as crime instead. The commentators' legal instinct was sound: the verse's tense and tone read like a docket. The signs are on the record now; the court's answer is on the record; and the rest of the story is the record's execution. The verse also marks the moment the confrontation leaves Moses' control entirely: the signs have been delivered, the defenses have been entered, and from here the initiative shifts to the Sender. The court believes it has closed the case with a classification; the verse's structure knows better — the case has only been accepted for hearing, and the Judge has been notified.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:37
> Moses responded, “My Lord knows best who has come with ˹true˺ guidance from Him and will fare best in the end. Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Case Handed Up**

Moses' answer to the court's double defense is not a debate but a referral: my Lord knows best. The refusal to argue on the court's terms is the sentence's doctrine. The court has offered two classifications — sorcery and novelty — and Moses answers by relocating the question to the only bench with jurisdiction: who came with guidance from Him, and whose is the outcome of the Home. The commentators noted what the reply refuses: no entanglement in the court's categories, no defense of the signs' mechanics, no genealogical counter-argument about whose forefathers said what. The referral is not evasion; it is precision — the question the court is actually deciding is whose claim is true, and that question has exactly one competent Judge. A messenger's authority is never strengthened by accepting the opposition's framing; Moses answers the frame by dissolving it.

**Who Brings Guidance From Him**

The first half of the referral states the mission's evidentiary standard: the test of a messenger is his source. Guidance from Him — from God — is the only accreditation that matters in this courtroom, and Moses puts the question exactly where the court cannot reach it: the court can examine no chains, conduct no experiments, issue no subpoenas to heaven. The verse thereby exposes the real nature of the confrontation: not a competition of explanations but a contest between jurisdictions — the court's writ versus the Sender's. The commentators connected the phrase to the standing pattern of the Qur'an's disputes: every prophet's opponents challenge the source, and every prophet's answer returns the challenge to the source's Owner. The pattern's logic is exact: if the claim is from God, the evidence will carry His signature — and the signs already do; if it is not, no eloquence will sustain it. The court believed it was judging Moses; Moses' answer informs the court that it is being judged.

**Whose Is the End of the Home**

The second half of the referral moves the timeline: the true outcome is not the court's verdict today but the end of the Home — and the end, the commentators explained, carries both meanings the Arabic holds: the final outcome of the matter and the final abode, the Hereafter in its beatitude. The phrase relocates victory and loss to a horizon the court's power cannot touch: Pharaoh can rule the outcome of an afternoon; he cannot rule outcomes. The same standard appears on Moses' own lips to his people in chapter 7 — seek help through patience and prayer; the earth is God's, He bequeaths it to whom He wills, and the outcome belongs to the reverent (7:128) — and the sūrah itself will reserve the eternal Home for those who seek neither tyranny nor corruption on the earth (28:83). The referral's timeline is thus the mission's deepest comfort: the court's verdicts are provisional by definition, and the only verdict that stands is scheduled.

**The Wrongdoers Will Not Prosper**

The referral closes with a verdict of its own: the wrongdoers will not succeed — stated not as threat but as standing law. The word for wrongdoers is the sūrah's own legal category, established in verse 4 against Pharaoh's administration: those who place things out of their right station. The sentence's certainty is the grammar of a universe whose Owner has not abdicated: rebellion can delay its own judgment, never cancel it. The commentators gathered the refrain's occurrences across the Book and heard in its repetition a doctrine being worn into the believer's reflexes: the apparent success of wrong is a running account, not a settled one. In the sūrah's narrative, the sentence functions as the hinge between the court's refusal and its fall: three verses from here, the same wrongdoers are swallowed by the same sea that opened for their victims, and the refrain is shown operating in real time.

**How a Messenger Answers a Court**

The verse is a portrait of contested daʿwah at its moment of maximum provocation, and its restraint is the instruction. Moses has just been called a sorcerer and his message a novelty; his reply spends no words on the insults and all its words on the criterion. The commentators' larger point stands behind the restraint: the messenger's task is to deliver the terms of judgment, not to win the exchange — the outcome belongs to the One who knows best who is guided. There is no record here of Moses performing wonders on demand or answering the genealogy argument point by point; the signs have been delivered, the referral has been entered, and the court's response is now between it and its Lord. For every later preacher shouted down by institutions, the verse fixed the composure: state the source, name the true end, recite the law of outcomes — and leave the bench to its rightful Judge. The answer's two-part structure also models how a believer argues with power: the first part removes the dispute from the opponents' competence — you cannot know who is guided, and neither will I assert it as my own verdict; the second part states the principle that does the deciding — the end belongs to another court, and wrong has a fixed failure rate. Neither clause is negotiable, and neither is hostile: the sentence simply tells the truth about jurisdiction and then stops. Moses does not threaten Pharaoh with defeat; he reminds him whose subject he already is. The restraint cost nothing and preserved everything: the mission's dignity, the message's clarity, and the messenger's standing as a warner rather than a disputant. When the end arrived, the court's own riverbed became the record of which party had argued from the stronger file.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:38
> Pharaoh declared, “O chiefs! I know of no other god for you but myself. So bake bricks out of clay for me, O Hamân, and build a high tower so I may look at the God of Moses, although I am sure he is a liar.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Theology of the Tyrant, Stated Aloud**

Pharaoh's sentence is the most honest thing he says in the Qur'an: I know of no god for you other than myself. The claim is not new in his mouth — the sūrah's parallel accounts record him proclaiming to Egypt, I am your lord, the most high (79:24), and claiming not to know any lord besides himself — but here it is spoken in council, as state doctrine, with the chiefs convened to hear it. The commentators heard in the declaration the completed theology of verse 4: the ruler who elevated himself in the land now names himself its only god. The sentence also explains the persecution's logic retroactively: if Pharaoh is the nation's god, then Israel's God is not a rival religion but a rival throne, and the murder of sons was always theology by other means. The verse's precision is that it lets the tyrant state his own creed — the Qur'an does not need to editorialize when the confession is this complete.

**Bricks, Fire, and a Tower**

The practical command follows the creed: bake bricks for me, O Hāmān, and build a tower. The order lands where the sūrah's readers expect it — with the officer named at the persecution's beginning (28:6) and again at the basket's landing (28:8), the permanent second man of the regime, here commissioning construction. The detail of the bricks carries its own history: fired clay and mortar belong to the building tradition of Egypt's Nile valley — where wood is scarce and kiln-baked brick is the old technique — and the Qur'an's choice of material fits the setting with the precision its critics spent a century denying. The tower's stated purpose is the creed's architecture: a stairway to the skies, to reach the God of Moses — or in this verse's wording, to look at Him. The commentators noted whether the structure was ever begun; the credible readings treat the command as theater — a demonstration for the chiefs, staging the state's answer to heaven's evidence. The bricks are the regime's reply to the signs: proof answered with public works.

**"So I May Look at the God of Moses"**

The tower's declared aim is an epistemology: Pharaoh will climb until he sees Moses' God, and sight will settle the dispute. The commentators heard the irony the story was built to deliver: the God of Moses is not located upward in creation's attic, to be observed from a taller building — He is the Lord of the worlds, nearer than the climber's own jugular, and the tower is theology's category error built in baked clay. The Qur'an's account of Moses himself makes the point from the believer's side: even Moses, God's spoken-to prophet, could not bear the theophany when he asked for it, and the mountain crashed to dust (7:143). Pharaoh's project thus answers the wrong question with the wrong instrument: God is not found by ascending, but received by submission — and the tower, even finished, could only deliver its builder to a longer fall. The commentators preserved the legendary versions — the arrow shot skyward, returning bloody — and the same tradition dismissed them; the Qur'an's tower is never said to be built, because its function in the verse is complete as rhetoric: it is the creed, drawn to scale.

**"Though I Consider Him a Liar"**

The sentence's last clause is the psychological key: although I consider him a liar. The clause contradicts the project in the same breath — if Moses is a liar, his God is a fiction, and no tower is needed to inspect a fiction; one builds toward what one, at some level, fears to be real. The commentators caught the contradiction and read the clause as the court's public posture: the denial is maintained for the chiefs' benefit while the building program admits the question is live. This is the Qur'an's consistent portrait of Pharaoh's inner court: certainty denied aloud, fear administered in policy. The same doubleness governs his magicians' defeat, his chiefs' private counsel, and the last-moment faith the sea will force. The tower project, in this light, is denial's architecture: enormous, expensive, and aimed at a God the builder professes not to believe in — the monument denial builds whenever it wants to keep checking.

**The Council That Cannot Say No**

The scene's staging completes its meaning: the declaration is addressed to the chiefs, the commission to Hāmān — the regime's whole machinery mobilized around one man's self-deification. No voice in the council registers the absurdity, because the system that produces the absurdity is the council: verse 4's factions, the notables class the Qur'an convicts across its pages, the administrators of the sons' murder. The commentators' political reading is exact: tyranny's final stage is not the tyrant's madness but the court's silence — the point at which no instrument of the state can cost the creed anything. The sūrah has already produced its contrast in the believing man of Pharaoh's house who will argue alone in this same council (40:28), and his loneliness is the measure of the court's capture. Pharaoh says there is no god for you but me, and the silence that answers is the kingdom's true confession: it has one god, and he is speaking. The verse's placement in the dialogue sharpens its absurdity into doctrine: Moses has just referred the dispute to a Lord who knows; Pharaoh answers by dissolving the Lordship altogether. Between those two sentences — my Lord knows best, and no god but me — the entire quarrel of the sūrah is drawn, and the tower is what the second sentence builds to avoid hearing the first.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:39
> And so he and his soldiers behaved arrogantly in the land with no right, thinking they would never be returned to Us

**Expanded Commentary**

**Arrogance in the Land, Without Right**

The verse reads like a coroner's summary: he and his hosts exalted in the land without right. The word for their self-exaltation is the same root the sūrah used of Pharaoh at his introduction — he exalted himself in the land (28:4) — and the verse's placement closes the biography with the same verb it opened: the man's whole career was one long rising in his own estimate. The phrase without right carries the legal weight: arrogance is not attitude but trespass — authority exercised where none was granted. The Qur'an's accounting of Pharaoh is consistent on this point from beginning to end: his crime is not strength but the claim that his strength owes nothing, the usurpation of a lordship that belongs, as his victim-nation's prayer will put it, to God alone. The soldiers are included in the verdict because they are the trespass's instruments: every hierarchy of violence that marched behind the claim shares its ledger.

**The Denial That Runs the Machine**

The second clause gives the machine its fuel: they thought they would never be returned to Us. The commentators identified the denial as the standing creed of the arrogant — the belief, practical rather than always doctrinal, that no accounting waits at the end of power. The Qur'an states the certainty's opposite across its pages: the return is real, and every denier of it is not a philosopher but a debtor pretending the creditor is dead. The psychology deserves precision: Pharaoh's court did not lack evidence of mortality — sons died by its own decree — but the denial operated where it mattered, at the level of consequence: their acts had no residue beyond their reach. The sūrah has traced the creed's works since its fourth verse: a man certain of no return can sort a population, legislate infanticide, and deify himself, because each act is priced at zero. The verse names the pricing mechanism — no return — as the engine of the whole regime, which is why the Qur'an fights the denial of the Hereafter with the urgency it reserves for God's oneness itself.

**The Creed Measured Against the Verse**

The verse's two clauses are one syllogism in the sūrah's argument: arrogance in the land is enabled by the assumption of no return. The pairing recurs across the Qur'an — those who exult in the land and scheme against the blessing of God, and God's answer: My plan is flawless while they are give respite (7:183 area); the ones who thought they would not return, and the opening of the gates of heaven's punishment. The commentators' refrain fits here: the certainty of the return is the load-bearing wall of all public morality. Remove it, and the strong do exactly what this verse records — exalt, and trespass, and price the weak at nothing. The sūrah's own closing verses will hang the moral directly: that Home of the Hereafter is given to those who seek no exaltation in the earth and no corruption, and the outcome belongs to the reverent (28:83). Pharaoh's biography and the believers' hope are set on the same scale: one creed of no return, one creed of the Home — and the sea will grade the exam.

**What They Thought, and What Was Already Moving**

The verse's placement is its quietest irony: it describes the regime at its maximum confidence — the tower commissioned, the council silent, the creed announced — while the reader already holds the knowledge Pharaoh lacks: a messenger is in the field, signs are stacking, and the return they deny is already in motion toward them. The Qur'an's grammar of thought — they thought — is the grammar of doom in this Book: it marks the moment a people's confidence is recorded against the facts that will refute it. The commentators noted the same construction in the last-day confessions, where every false assumption is read back to its holder. Pharaoh's army thought it would never be returned; within the verse's horizon, the same soldiers are walking into the seabed between walls of water, and the return they denied will be the last thing they learn. The verse thus teaches its readers to hear certainty of impunity as a forecast, and the forecast's record in this Book is perfect.

**The Biography in Two Clauses**

Compressed as it is, the verse completes the sūrah's portrait of the man who opened it. Verse 4: he exalted himself, divided the people, slaughtered sons. Verse 38: he deified himself and commissioned a tower. Verse 39: he exalted with his hosts, without right, denying the return. The three verses are one rising curve of self-worship — arrogance at home, arrogance deified, arrogance institutionalized — and the curve's geometry is the argument: tyranny is not a policy error but a theology, growing from self-exaltation through self-deification to the denial of any throne above one's own. The Qur'an's answer to that theology was never a counter-army; it was two brothers with signs and a promise. The verse stands as the last frame of the regime at full height, and the height is the measure of the fall the next verse delivers. The verse's double bookkeeping — arrogance in the land, denial of the return — also arms the reader with a diagnostic pair that works in any century: wherever power behaves as if unaccountable, trace the account it thinks has been closed. Tyranny is always, at root, a theory about the ledger, and the Qur'an's answer is always the same entry in dispute: everything is coming back, and everything is coming due.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:40
> So We seized him and his soldiers, casting them into the sea. See then what was the end of the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seizure**

The sentence that ends the regime has three clauses and no battle: So We seized him and his hosts, and cast them into the sea. The grammar of the seizure is entirely God's — the subject of every verb — and the compression is the point: a regime that occupied a decade of the sūrah's narrative is terminated in one clause, because its termination required exactly one act. The commentators read the verb's force literally: a taking in hand, the way power takes what is powerless to resist. The army that had exalted in the land without right is handled by the right it denied; the sea that received a basket in the story's first act now receives the army in the last. The Qur'an's other accounts fill the frame — the pursuit, the parted water, the wall of sea on either hand — and this sūrah's restraint leaves the essence bare: the return they denied arrived, and it arrived as custody.

**The Sea That Opened Both Ways**

The instrument deserves its own reading, because the sea is the story's oldest character. It carried the condemned infant to the palace in verse 8; it carries the condemners to the bottom in verse 40. The same water that served the decree's authors at their height executes the decree's judgment at their fall, and the symmetry is the sūrah's signature of providence: the plans of the powerful are written in a medium that belongs to Someone else. The Qur'an's fuller accounts add the detail the symmetry needs: the sea opened for Israel and closed over Egypt — the identical water serving as corridor and coffin within one night, selection exercised not by the tide but by the Judge. The drowning is recorded across the Book in seven separate chapters, and the repetition is doctrine: the event functions as the standing exhibit of how the return Pharaoh denied actually operates — suddenly, terminally, and by the very nature he ruled.

**The Believer's Water, the Tyrant's**

The Qur'an preserves one scene from inside the drowning that this sūrah's brevity omits: as the water closed, Pharaoh cried that he believed in what the Children of Israel believed (10:90) — and the answer he received fixed the scene's lesson for every reader: faith at the moment of the seizure is not the return, it is the return's arrival. The body of the man who claimed godhood was displayed as a sign for those after him, preserved where his army was not, a monument not of honor but of evidence. The commentators treated the episode as the completion of verse 38's tower: Pharaoh sought to look at the God of Moses from above, and he saw Him, in the end, from below — from the one vantage the sea provides. The tower's irony is thus paid in full: he wanted sight; he received knowledge; and the difference between them is exactly the distance between arrogance and belief.

**"Behold How the Wrongdoers Ended"**

The closing command — behold how the wrongdoers fared in the end — converts the event into an assignment. The refrain is one of the Qur'an's standing instruments: travel through the land and see how the deniers ended, addressed to every generation with roads and ruins (12:109, 40:21). The seeing commanded is not tourism; it is induction — the reader is told to treat history as evidence, to walk the earth's wrecked empires and read the pattern the sea just displayed. The commentators heard in the refrain the verse's whole argument: God's judgments on the arrogant are not hidden events but public records, open to any walker with eyes. The command's plural address includes the sūrah's own audience — the persecuted Makkans, whose city's chiefs held the same creed as Pharaoh's — and its logic was meant to be acted on: caravans passed the dead towns of ʿĀd and Thamūd on every northbound journey, and the Qur'an kept asking what they saw there.

**The Verdict of Verse 4, Executed**

With this verse, the sūrah's first movement closes its circle. Verse 4 filed the charge — Pharaoh exalted himself, divided the people, slew the sons, worked corruption; verse 5 announced the sentence in reverse — favor to the oppressed, succession in the land, the feared shown to the fearsome. Verse 40 executes the file: the corruptor is seized, his machine drowned, and the oppressed's inheritance set in motion. The arithmetic of the story's justice is exact: what Pharaoh attempted by water — the drowning of sons — returned by water; what he feared — the loss of control — he watched from inside the loss; and the child his household pulled from that same river is the agent of his end. The refrain's command to behold is therefore also a command to trace: the sea, the basket, the tower, the knife — every instrument in the story came from one hand, and the hand that started the file has now signed it closed. The command's addressee is worth one final note: behold — the seeing is assigned to everyone after, including every reader who has never stood near that sea. The Qur'an does not narrate Egypt's fall as an item of ancient interest; it exhibits it as standing proof, and proof demands a verdict from each generation that hears of it. The drowned army is the Book's permanent exhibit in the case of the return denied — and the exhibit's silence is louder than any argument, because it was arranged by the One whom every tyrant, in every capital since, still insists on not hearing.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:41
> We made them leaders inviting ˹others˺ to the Fire. And on the Day of Judgment they will not be helped

**Expanded Commentary**

**Leadership Turned Inside Out**

The verse hands Pharaoh and his party the sūrah's oldest title — and reverses it. Verse 5 promised the oppressed they would be made models of faith; verse 41 announces the opposite appointment: We made them leaders inviting to the Fire. The same divine governance that raises the crushed to imamate raises the corrupt to infamy, and the parallel is the argument: leadership in this Book is always functional — a position from which others are led somewhere. Pharaoh led Egypt's institutions, its soldiers, its cult; the road he pointed down ended at the Fire, and the verse says so in the same grammar verse 5 used for the covenant. No reader can miss the symmetry or its harshness: God does not merely punish tyrants; He converts their rank into the measure of their ruin, because the higher the post, the wider the gate it opens for those who follow.

**The Foremost Who Led by Precedent**

The commentators read the leadership precisely: those foremost in deeds and beliefs that lead to the Fire, and in calling others to follow — the ones whose example other people copy. The reading matters because it assigns guilt by influence, not only by act. Pharaoh's court did not merely commit corruption; it normalized it — legislated the culling of sons into policy, deified its king into doctrine, and taught a civilization to call the arrangement order. Every hierarchy that took its signal from the palace inherited the vector. The verse thus completes the sūrah's political anatomy: in verse 4 the corruptor divides; in verse 41 the corruptor is revealed as a signpost — and the people who walked behind the signpost are not acquitted, but the signpost carries the heavier load, because it is the reason the road looked walkable.

**"They Will Not Be Helped"**

The closing clause strips the one currency tyrannies always bank on: rescue. On the Day of Judgment they will not be helped — no alliance, no army, no intercession, no appeal. The phrase is short, and its brevity is its function: the regime that commanded every resource of the age's superpower is told, in six words, that none of it transfers to that day. The commentators heard in the denial the specific abolition of the help Egypt had always assumed — the gods of its cult, the solidity of its monuments, the loyalty of its hosts. All help is creaturely, and creatures do not answer on that day. The verse's audience in Makkah — the weak, watching the strong of their own city command every visible resource — were being taught to price those resources correctly: everything visible is rented, and the lease does not survive the grave.

**Why the Verse Says "We Made"**

The verse attributes the appointment to God — We made them leaders — and the attribution deserves its weight rather than embarrassment. The classical tradition read it as divine justice operating through permission: God allows the arrogant to rise to the exact stations from which their fall will be most instructive, stations whose occupants cannot hide. A tyrant among peasants is a crime; a tyrant enthroned is a lesson. The same hand that turned the basket toward the palace turned Pharaoh toward his tower, and both turnings served one pedagogy. This is not God authoring evil; it is God administering the stage on which chosen evil will be fully displayed and fully answered. The believer who reads the news as this verse teaches reads it twice — once as menace, once as exhibition — and the second reading is what steadies the first.

**The Anti-Image of Moses' Party**

Set beside the promise to the followers of the signs — you and those who follow you will prevail (28:35) — the verse completes a matched pair of processions. Two leaders, two invitations, two destinations: Moses calls to the Lord of the worlds through evidence; Pharaoh calls to himself through decree; and each party is defined by whose voice it walks toward.
