The commentators heard in the formula both a boundary and a mercy: the boundary — I am not the auditor of your conduct, and you are not mine — and the mercy — the quarrel is not personalized, because the accounting has been handed to the only competent Judge. The phrase rescues the believer from the two corruptions of disagreement: vigilantism, in which the believer polices another's soul, and entanglement, in which the believer's deeds become hostage to the quarrel. The accounts are kept separate so that neither party's sins can be billed to the other's response. In a city where reputation was currency, the declaration is also a withdrawal from the exchange rate: their deeds are not priced in your deeds.

**Peace as a Complete Reply**

The reply they give is one word of surrender that is not surrender: peace be upon you. The commentators preserved the incident the tradition attached to the verse: certain People of the Book, entering Islam before Abū Jahl, were followed from the gathering by his abuse — and answered only with peace. The reply's lineage runs deep in the Qur'an: Abraham says it to his idolatrous father — peace be upon you; I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness (19:47) — and its final form is the parting of ways spoken to deniers: you have your way, and I have my way (109:6). Peace, in this lineage, is not appeasement; it is the refusal to accept the quarrel's premise — that worth is contested in conversation. The word hands the speaker's dignity back to its Owner and closes the exchange without a casualty.

**We Do Not Court the Ignorant**

The declaration's last clause is the policy statement: we do not seek out the ignorant — no appetite for the company of those whose religion is noise. The commentators were careful with the scope: the avoidance is of the ignorant conduct and its commerce, not of the ignorant person as such — the same Qur'an commands the Prophet to counsel with the ignorant in kindness when the purpose is their rescue. The distinction is the verse's wisdom: engage the soul, refuse the spectacle. The believers of this verse are not hermits; they are citizens who decline one specific marketplace — the one where words are stripped of weight. Their avoidance is itself testimony: a community that will not trade in idle talk advertises, by absence, that it has something heavier to carry.

**Forbearance Without Surrender**

The verse completes the portrait that verse 54 began — patience, beautiful response, spending — and its placement is the lesson: the slander-response follows the double wage because forbearance is the wage's hardest installment. The commentators' summary deserves the close: these believers possess truth without needing the last word, which is the test of possession. The quarrelsome are still bargaining; the secure have left the market. And the sūrah's own history sharpens the point: the same chapter that recorded Moses' restrained referral before Pharaoh's court (28:37) now records the converts' restrained peace before Abū Jahl's abuse — the two scenes are one discipline at two scales, and the Qur'an pays both with the same currency: the Hereafter's weights, where the last word is worth nothing and the kept soul is worth everything.

**Peace Against the Honor Code**

The believers' reply detonated the operating code of their culture. Arabia ran on honor, and honor ran on retaliation: an insult unanswered was a debt unpaid, and the market price of a man was set by how loudly he answered provocation. Into that economy the verse introduces a word that settles all accounts: peace — spoken to abusers, in public, by men strong enough to answer otherwise. The commentators noted the reply's dual effect: it deprived the abuser of the war he was opening, and it displayed, in the hearing of the crowd, a strength the culture had no category for — the strength of a man whose dignity is not for sale in the quarrel. The verse thus redefines courage a generation before the community would need the redefinition in blood: the bravest act in a culture of retaliation is the refusal to retaliate, because it fights the culture itself rather than its latest messenger. The believers' peace was not the absence of power; it was power's deliberate disarmament — and every generation that has answered contempt with the same word has spent the same currency, at the same exchange rate, in the same market the verse first taught.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:56
> You surely cannot guide whoever you like ˹O Prophet˺, but it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills, and He knows best who are ˹fit to be˺ guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Beloved Uncle**

The tradition attached this verse to the hardest scene of the Prophet's life: the deathbed of Abū Ṭālib, the uncle who had raised him, shielded him through the boycott and the years of mockery, and refused the faith to the end. The Prophet urged him toward the word by which he could plead for him before God — and the old man's answer, recorded in the soundest collections, was that he would not say it lest the Quraysh mock that he said it only in fear of death (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1360). The verse descended on that grief: you do not guide whom you love. No verse in the Qur'an carries more consolation per word for the earnest, because it addresses the exact wound of every guide — that love is not a passport into hearts, and the best-beloved may die outside the door the guide stood knocking on for forty years.

**Love Is Not a Lever**

The verse's teaching generalizes through the Qur'an's own gallery: Adam's son murdered his brother though their father was the first prophet; Noah's son refused the ark and drowned in sight of his father's call; Abraham pleaded for his idolatrous father until revelation closed the file — Abraham was tender-hearted and forbearing (9:114). The commentators gathered the cases into the verse's doctrine: guidance is not a fluid that flows along affection, and prophethood itself confers no jurisdiction over the will. The teaching's mercy is its severity: it forbids the guide to measure his effort by its yield, since the yield was never his to command. The Prophet, who could persuade an empire's worth of strangers, could not move the man whose shoulder he had slept against — and the verse tells him, and every guide after him, that this asymmetry is the design, not the failure.

**The Guide's Office, Correctly Sized**

What, then, is the guide? The Qur'an's own answer runs through the verse's grammar: the messenger delivers, God converts. The commentators paired this verse with the standing job description — you are only a warner, and the weighing belongs to God; your duty is only the conveyance. The sizing liberates in both directions: it forbids the despair that quits when results lag, and it forbids the pride that claims what only grace performed. The Prophet's own practice held the line: he argued, grieved, softened, warned — and never once forced a heart, because the verse had told him the heart was not in his hands. The believer who internalizes the verse stops auditing his sincerity by other people's verdicts and returns to the only audit the verse leaves open: was the message delivered, and delivered with mercy.

**"He Knows Best Who Are Guided"**

The verse's last clause assigns the hidden file to its Owner: He knows best who are rightly guided. The commentators heard two registers in the knowledge: the register of allotment — God guides whom He wills — and the register of records — God knows the depth of every turning, including the turns no observer saw. The clause protects the faith of the weak who converted with no spectacle, and it withholds the verdict on the strong who died apparently believing: the interior belongs to the interior's Maker. For the readers of the sūrah, the clause completes the theme that has run since verse 13 — most people do not know — now applied to the knowledge guides most want: who was actually reached. The answer is: the One who did the reaching, and He does not publish the ledger early.

**Consolation's Exact Dose**

The verse's placement in the sūrah is a study in divine tact: it follows the portrait of the believing People of the Book — the conversions the Prophet could see — with the reminder that the conversions he could not see, and the refusals he could not undo, are governed by the same Hand. The commentators' summary is the verse's lasting gift: the guide is commanded to love, forbidden to count. Abū Ṭālib died, and the verse remained; every guide since has stood where the Prophet stood, holding a message for someone who will not take it, and the verse stands there too — not to chill the love, but to carry the part of it that love cannot: the outcome. The work is ours, says the verse; the guiding is His; and He knows best — which is the only sentence grief can build on.

**Grief, Governed**

The verse does not abolish the guide's grief; it gives it a government. The Prophet loved Abū Ṭālib without qualification and lost him without remedy — and the Qur'an, which could have silenced the sorrow with a bare precept, instead gave it a frame: love whom you must, labor as you can, and hand the unbending door to the One who holds its hinges. The commentators' care with the scene shows the tradition's own tenderness: they preserved the Prophet's pleadings and the old man's refusals without editorial softening, because the grief itself was revelation's context. The teaching's balance is exact: despair for a soul is forbidden — no one may write another off while the door is open — and responsibility for a soul is likewise forbidden — no guide is the landlord of another's choice. Between those two fences runs the whole narrow road of loving the unconverted: with hope, without despair; with effort, without ownership. Every guide who has watched a beloved heart walk past the message learns the road's name eventually, and the verse is its map — drawn at the deathbed of the one man whose loss taught it best.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:57
> They say ˹to the Prophet˺, “If we were to follow ˹true˺ guidance with you, we would certainly be snatched away from our land.” Have We not established for them a safe haven ˹in Mecca˺ to which fruits of all kinds are brought as a provision from Us? But most of them do not know ˹this favour˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fear They Confessed**

The verse quotes the Quraysh's true reason in their own words: if we follow the guidance with you, we will be snatched from our land. The commentators read the admission with care: the speaker concedes the guidance is real — the objection is not truth but consequence; they feared being ostracized by the surrounding Arabs, whose idols their sanctuary's economy served. The confession is the verse's exhibit: disbelief, in Makkah, was an economic position. The men who demanded miracles in verse 48 are revealed here as men who believed enough to calculate the price of believing — and calculated against it. The Qur'an's honesty about its opponents is again its mark: they are not quoted as fools but as men with interests, which is the more damning file.

**The Snatching They Feared, Named Precisely**

What was this snatching? The commentators explained the politics: Makkah's standing among the tribes rested on its custodianship of the idols of the sanctuary; a city that worshipped its ancestors' gods could count on pilgrimage, trade, and alliance — a city that worshipped the One could count on boycott. The fear was not imaginary: the young community would soon be driven to two migrations, besieged, and starved. But the verse answers the fear with a fact they walked past daily: the snatching they feared was already prevented — by the sanctuary itself. Have We not established for them a safe haven: the security whose dividends they banked was a standing gift, and the verse forces the account open: the men afraid of losing God's protection had never acknowledged God's protection as theirs.

**A Sanctuary With a Supply Chain**

The verse describes the haven with a provisioner's precision: to which the fruits of everything are brought — a safe harbor whose supplies arrive from every horizon. The commentators located the geography: Makkah, barren ground that grows almost nothing, fed by the caravans its sanctuary attracted — the Quraysh's own winter and summer journeys, the trading runs to Yemen and Syria, secured by the House's peace. The sūrah of the Quraysh names the same economy in its four verses: their security in the journeys and the feeding against hunger and the security against fear — all of it from the Lord of the House (106:4). The verse's point is the ledger's first line: the land they feared to lose was never their achievement; it was provision from His presence, delivered through the very sanctity they were trading away.

**"But Most of Them Do Not Know"**

The closing clause returns the sūrah's oldest refrain — most people do not know — now aimed at the benefactors themselves: most of them know not that it is God who established them and kept them safe. The commentators' reading is exact: the ignorance is not of facts but of authorship. The Quraysh knew their market days and their alliance networks; what they did not know — or refused to file — was whose gift the arrangement was. The verse thus completes the chapter's epistemology in miniature: the palace never knew its nurse was the mother (28:13), and the sanctuary never knew its Keeper was the Giver (28:57). The same blindness, at two scales — and the cure is the same: a messenger, reciting, until the gift is traced to its Giver.

**The Excuse's Economics**

The verse dismantles a form of refusal that has never gone out of production: belief postponed for the sake of position. The commentators' wider reading catches its inner form too — every soul that declines the spiritual road because the cost seems too high is quoting the Quraysh: if I follow, I will be snatched from my arrangements. The verse's answer travels with it: the arrangements are already gifts, held on tenure that the Giver can end by the same word that began it — and the safest ground in existence is obedience, the one haven no alliance can revoke. The Quraysh wagered their souls that the sanctuary's peace was self-made; within the sūrah's own horizon, Makkah would enter Islam intact, its sanctuary standing, its fruits still arriving — and the wager's loss would be recorded by the very people who thought belief was the risky position.

**Gratitude as the Missing Knowledge**

The verse names the cure for their ignorance without using the modern word: they know not the favor — and in the Qur'an, knowing a favor means tracing it to its Giver, which is the first act of gratitude. The commentators' distinction holds the verse together: the Quraysh knew their markets, their harvests of trade, their immunity in the sacred precinct — what they lacked was the knowledge that attaches a gift to its source, and without that attachment the knowledge they had only deepened their account. Gratitude, in this Book, is therefore not a sentiment but an epistemology: to thank is to have understood; to enjoy without tracing is the practical atheism the verse is prosecuting. The teaching strips gratitude of its greeting-card softness: it is a reading skill — the same skill the sūrah demanded at verse 13, when the palace handled the promise and never knew. The favored who learn to trace — security to its Keeper, plenty to its Provider, the city's peace to its Lord — have already crossed from most people into the remnant that knows; and the crossing is completed not by feeling thankful but by acting the tenant: obeying the Landlord whose lease they have finally read.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:58
> ˹Imagine˺ how many societies We have destroyed that had been spoiled by their ˹comfortable˺ living! Those are their residences, never inhabited after them except passingly. And We ˹alone˺ were the Successor

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question With a Catalogue Behind It**

The verse opens with the Qur'an's standing rhetorical survey: how many a town have We destroyed — a question the Book asks in a dozen registers, each one pointing at ruins its audience could visit. The commentators listed the companion verses: the towns destroyed for their wrongdoing in chapter 7, the overturned in chapter 21, the mightier towns of chapter 47 — how many societies We destroyed that were far superior in might (47:13). The question's force is experiential: Arabia's caravans camped in these dead cities; the ruined wells and fallen towers were the landscape's own museums. The verse is not speculation — it is a guided tour with an itinerary the listeners had walked, and the guide's question has one purpose: make them count what comfort bought the previous tenants.

**"Exulting in Their Ease"**

The destroyed are identified by their signature: they exulted in their life of ease — the word the commentators glossed as prosperity without gratitude, plenty enjoyed as if self-made. The term links the verse directly to Pharaoh's biography, which is the sūrah's own illustration: his hosts exulted in the land without right (28:39), and the verse's placement shows the same file being read across history — every ruined town was a smaller Pharaoh's Egypt. The commentators' note is precise: the ease was not the crime; the crime was the ease's interpretation — wealth read as endorsement, comfort read as permanence. The Qur'an's doctrine of prosperity, established in its earliest pages, runs through here: ease is a test's furniture, and the spoiled are those who thought the furniture was the house.

**Dwellings That Outlive Their Owners**

The verse records the ruins' afterlife with a traveler's eye: there are their dwellings, not inhabited after them except a little — and the commentators corrected the obvious misreading: it is not that a few people survived; it is that the structures survived, sheltering caravans, herders, and the occasional passerby while the nations that built them were gone. The image is the verse's coldest statistic: a civilization's total output, reduced to windbreaks. The commentators connected the motif to the Qur'an's funeral verse for the drowned of Pharaoh's party: neither heaven nor earth wept over them, nor were they reprieved (44:29) — extinction without eulogy. The dwellings standing over the absence are the verse's own epitaph: the world kept the buildings and lost the builders, because the buildings were never the civilization.

**"And We Are the Inheritor"**

The verse's final clause states the legal outcome: We are the Inheritor — and the commentators gave the reading its exact context: the destruction was so complete that none were left to inherit; God alone remained the estate's holder. The clause universalizes: all things return to Him, and He is the Inheritor of all — so that inheritance in the Qur'an's teaching is never finally transferred, only lent. The word's placement after the ruins makes the lesson inescapable: property's chain of title runs through graves, and the last holder is always the same. The Quraysh, sitting on the sanctuary's trade, are being shown their own eventual file: their houses too are dwellings; their tenure too is tenancy; the Inheritor has outlasted every previous claimant and does not need the estate argued.

**The Verse Beside the Excuse**

The commentators connected this verse to the previous one as argument to excuse: the Quraysh refused guidance to protect their holdings — and the verse answers with the holdings' actuarial record: every protection ever mounted against loss failed the towns that tried it, and ease was the marker of the ones most thoroughly lost. The logic completes the sūrah's political economy: verse 5's succession for the oppressed; verse 58's inheritance reclaimed from the exulting. The sequence teaches the believer to hold property in the Qur'an's tense: held now, transferred surely — and the transfer's terms are public. A people who learn this verse's lesson build differently: not smaller, but lighter, their wealth handled as equipment rather than identity, because they have read the dwellings' sign and know who signs the next lease.

**The Reader Is the Passerby**

The verse's most intimate word is its smallest: the dwellings are inhabited after them only a little — and the ones doing the inhabiting, across every century since, are people exactly like the reader. The caravan sheltering in ʿĀd's fallen walls, the herder camping in Thamūd's carved porches, the tourist photographing the empty treasury of a desert city — each of them steps into the verse's sentence as its subject: the little habitation that outlived habits. The Qur'an's guided tour therefore has no spectators; everyone who walks the earth's ruins is conscripted as evidence-handler, and the commentary tradition drew the lesson the verse implies: walk them humbly, because you are lodging in another people's unfinished story. The traveler who reads the verse before reading the ruins sees differently — not vanished peoples to romanticize, but appraisals posted by the Owner: here, comfort read as permanence; here, the Inheritor closed the account. The verse turns geography into curriculum, and every generation's feet are the enrollment. The lesson's daily form is smaller and nearer than the ruins: the inherited house, the family firm, the city quarter that will outlive its present tenants — each is a dwelling in training, and the verse's question waits inside all of them: who signed for this, and who is scheduled to inherit? A life lived in the verse's presence holds its keys loosely, which is, in the end, the only way to hold anything the Inheritor has not already entered in His ledger.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:59
> Your Lord would never destroy a society until He had sent to its capital a messenger, reciting Our revelations to them. Nor would We ever destroy a society unless its people persisted in wrongdoing

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mother City Named**

The verse fixes the venue of divine notice: your Lord does not destroy a town until He sends to its mother-city a messenger. The commentators identified Makkah behind the phrase — the city whose ancient title, Mother of Towns, the tradition preserved — while noting the broader readings: the mother-city as the greatest town of any people, the metropolis whose fall takes the region with it. The precision of the address matters jurisprudentially: notice is served at the capital, in the hearing of the center, so that the periphery cannot plead distance and the center cannot plead ignorance. God's judgments in the Qur'an are served at the top first — Pharaoh's court, Noah's notables, the assemblies of every destroyed people — because the center is where the refusal was manufactured and distributed. The verse's geography is thus its doctrine: accountability flows from the head of the table down.

**Due Process for Nations**

The verse states the Qur'an's standing rule of collective judgment: never destroy without a messenger — the same law recorded elsewhere in its own words: We would never punish until We have sent a messenger (17:15), and never destroy a society for its wrongdoing while its people are unaware (6:131). The commentators gathered the parallel verses into a single doctrine: destruction without notice is not in God's government. The rule's severity lies in its completeness: no civilization is condemned on averages, no generation punished for its ancestors' files. Before any town falls, a man stands in it, reciting signs, in its own tongue, at its own center — and the verse's placement in the sūrah, after the Prophet's commission to the never-warned Arabs (28:46), fixes Makkah's position on the timeline: notice has been served.

**The Messenger Reciting Our Signs**

The content of the notice is specified: reciting Our revelations — not a sealed verdict delivered from the sky, but a public reading, signs recited in the hearing of the town. The commentators noted what this implies about the destroyed: their ruin came with the evidence already in their ears. The rule dignifies the messenger's office as the hinge of nations: the difference between a city that repents and a city that falls is, humanly speaking, one reciter's persistence. And the rule explains the Qur'an's own historical moment: the Prophet in Makkah is the recitation in process — which means the verse is not describing a past custom only; it is describing the present tense of the sūrah's own audience, whose capital currently contains a man reciting.

**"Save When Its People Are Wrongdoers"**

The condition of destruction is stated without appeal: the people must be wrongdoers — the Qur'an's standing legal category, the same word the sūrah pinned on Pharaoh in its fourth verse. The commentators stressed the pairing: notice plus wrongdoing — both required, neither sufficient alone. A warned but innocent town does not fall; a wrongdoing but unwarned town is not yet judged. The condition's placement corrects two errors: it forbids the believer to read every disaster as heaven's verdict (the unwarned are not tried), and it forbids the wrongdoer to read survival as acquittal (the warned are already in the case). The word's choice also echoes the sūrah's first movement: the charge that brought Egypt down is the charge now being announced as the standing criterion — the criteria of judgment do not vary by empire.

**The Verse That Closes the Excuse**

The verse completes the circuit that verse 47 opened: the deniers' alibi — no messenger came — is here retired by statute, and the sūrah's audience hears the statute read while the messenger stands in their market. The commentators' summary is the verse's lasting use: God's justice is procedural, and the procedure is audible — whoever is destroyed without a warner, the record will show; whoever is destroyed with one, the record shows that too. The verse thereby arms the believer with the right question for every fallen power in history: was notice served, and what did the notice say? The ruins of the previous verse answer for the past; the recitation of the present verse answers for the present; and both answer in the same voice — the Lord who destroys nothing He has not first addressed.

**Notice Served at the Center**

The verse's jurisprudence explains why the messenger is sent to the mother-city, and the reasoning holds for every structure of collective guilt. A society's refusals are manufactured at its center — the court that sets the creed, the market that prices the truth, the council that licenses the corruption — and the periphery mostly inherits what the center decides. Notice served at the capital is therefore notice to the whole body: when Makkah hears, Arabia has heard, because Makkah's voice is the one Arabia repeats. The commentators' broader reading — the greatest city of any people — makes the rule universal: God's government addresses nations through their heads, and no province can plead that the warning never crossed its border while its capital held the reciter for years. The rule's modern edge is unmistakable: in every age the accountable addresses are the visible ones — the pulpit, the market, the court — and the verse assigns them first position in the queue of warning. What a civilization does with its center's notice is the file its ruins will eventually be asked to explain. The rule also fixes the timeline's mercy: between notice and sentence stands the whole length of a messenger's residence — years of recitation in which any argument could be answered, any heart could turn — so that the delay the wrongdoers mistook for weakness was, all along, the case being assembled in their favor.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:60
> Whatever ˹pleasure˺ you have been given is no more than ˹a fleeting˺ enjoyment and adornment of this worldly life. But what is with Allah is far better and more lasting. Will you not then understand

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Appraises Everything**

The sūrah's first movement was an inventory of wealth — Egypt's granaries, Pharaoh's towers, Hamān's bricks, the Quraysh's caravan-secured sanctuary — and this verse is its appraisal: whatever you are given is the enjoyment and adornment of the present life, and nothing more. The commentators read the two nouns as the world's whole balance sheet: enjoyment — the use a thing yields while it lasts — and adornment — the shine it throws while it lasts. The pairing is exact and complete: everything creaturely is either being used or being admired, and the verse refuses both categories the status of permanence. The appraisal does not condemn the goods; it prices them — and the pricing is mercy, because mispriced wealth ruins its holders, as the spoiled towns of the previous verse demonstrated at scale.

**"Better and More Lasting"**

The comparison's two adjectives do the verse's work: what is with God is better and more lasting — and the commentators carried the tradition's arithmetic: this world against the Hereafter is a speck against the sea, and the Hereafter's goods are lasting because they do not end, while everything here has an end by its very nature. The Qur'an's short sūrah at the Book's end states the comparison in its compressed form: the Hereafter is better and more lasting (87:17) — the same two adjectives, the same verdict. The word better matters as much as lasting: the next life is not merely longer; it is superior in kind — the appetite that the world's pleasures fatigue is met there without fatigue. The verse's economy should be noted: it does not say the world is worthless; it says the world is the lesser asset, and the believer is being directed to the greater one while holding the lesser.

**Adornment: The Word's Own Precision**

Why adornment? The commentators connected the term to its sibling verse — the world's goods named as an ornament (33:28) — and the choice of word carries the teaching: adornment is for display, and display is for others. Much of what wealth purchases is not use but appearance — the watched house, the heard reputation, the envied ease — and the verse files all of it under decoration: beautiful, temporal, and inert. The word also explains the world's seduction mechanism: adornment works on the eye from a distance and loses detail on approach — which is why the Qur'an insists the Hereafter's goods are better, not merely later: seen up close, the world's shine is thinner than its invoice. The verse thus trains a habit of appraisal: ask of every possession whether it is being enjoyed or merely displayed, and note that neither verb survives the grave.

**"Will You Not Understand"**

The closing question is the verse's audit: will you not reason? The commentators placed it among the Qur'an's standing interrogations — will you not understand, will you not reflect, will you not see — each one aimed at a faculty the audience already owns. The question's placement after fifty-nine verses of demonstrated patterns — the drowned court, the ruined towns, the delivered Word — makes it a demand rather than a prompt: the evidence has been exhibited; the appraisal has been read; the only remaining variable is the audience's reasoning. The question also dignifies the reader: the Qur'an does not close its case with authority alone; it closes it with an appeal to the reader's own calculation, because the verdict being sought — preference for the lasting — is one only the reader can enter.

**From Pharaoh's Treasury to the Verse's Scale**

The verse's placement seals the sūrah's whole argument: chapter 28 opened with a baby carried by a river into a tyrant's treasury and closes with that treasury's category revealed — enjoyment and adornment, priced against the lasting. Pharaoh is the verse's completed case study: he held the age's maximum of both nouns, spent them on a tower to inspect God, and learned, in the sea, the difference between holding and keeping. The commentators' summary carries the verse into the reader's own ledger: whatever is in your hands is the world's currency — spendable, displayable, perishable; what is with God is the other currency, purchasable only by transfer — faith, obedience, patience, charity — and the exchange rate is published in this verse. Will you not understand is therefore not rhetoric; it is the investment question of a lifetime, asked while the markets of both worlds are still open.

**Enjoyment Named Honestly**

Notice what the verse refuses to do: it does not deny that the world's goods are enjoyed. The word it chooses — enjoyment — concedes the pleasure as real: food delights, wealth comforts, beauty moves, and the Qur'an is not the enemy of any of these. The Book's anti-ascetic streak runs elsewhere in its own verses — monasticism as an invention, abstention from good things as no part of devotion — and this verse keeps the same honesty: the appraisal is of duration and rank, not of reality. The commentator tradition's image captures the balance: the world is a speck against the sea of the Hereafter — but a speck is still wet; the world's pleasures are still pleasures, briefly. The believer's relation to them is therefore tenancy without idolatry: eat, and thank; earn, and share; adorn, and remember the decoration's shelf life. What the verse forbids is not the enjoying but the confusing — treating the speck as the sea — and the confusion, the sūrah's fifty-nine preceding verses have shown, is the exact error that filled Egypt's towers, spoiled the towns, and emptied the marketplaces of their judgment. Honesty about pleasure is the first defense against being ruled by it.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:61
> Can those to whom We have made a fine promise—which they will see fulfilled—be like those who We have allowed to enjoy the pleasures of this worldly life, but on the Day of Judgment will be brought ˹for punishment˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Prices Two Lives**

The question sets two men side by side and asks whether they can be the same. The first is the one given a fine promise — the Garden, pledged in revelation — and who will see the pledge paid. The second is the one handed the world's enjoyment and nothing else, summoned on the Day of Resurrection among the arraigned. The commentators explained the comparison's target: the poor and afflicted believers of Makkah were being told, against every visible measure, that they outranked the comfortable deniers — and the verse's own tradition named the two archetypes, the Prophet and Abū Jahl, standing at the two ends of the same city. The comparison's power is its refusal of appearances: by every observable index — food, security, followers — the denier lived better. The verse removes the visible index from the scales and weighs what remains: a promise with a Guarantor against an enjoyment with a foreclosure date.

**A Promise Made, a Promise Kept**

The first man's definition rests on two facts: the promise was made to him, and he will receive it. The commentators noted the grammar of certainty in the clause — which they will see fulfilled — so the verse is not weighing hope against pleasure but fulfillment against pleasure. The promise is the believer's asset precisely because it is documented: pledged in revelation, repeated in prayer, sealed by the One whose record of payment stands behind the sūrah's whole history — Israel's inheritance was not a metaphor; the oppressed were installed in the land within the chapter's own pages. Against that instrument, the denier's enjoyment is a payment made in advance, and every advance reduces what remains. The verse's question — can these two be alike? — is thus an accounting question, and its answer is the arithmetic the believer lives by.

**The Comfortable Are Not Being Envied**

The second man's description deserves its exactness: We gave him enjoyment — the verb's subject is God. The world's pleasures flow to deniers by heaven's own permission, and the verse does not disguise that fact or resent it. The commentators' larger teaching carries the point: the world, in the Prophet's own words, is contemptible before God — as reported from Sahl ibn Saʿd, if the world weighed, in the sight of Allah, the wing of a mosquito, He would not have given a disbeliever a drink of water from it — its wide distribution to those who deny Him being the proof of its small price. Enjoyment is therefore not evidence of favor, and deprivation is not evidence of anger. The verse un-teaches the oldest confusion of religious life — reading circumstances as verdicts — by placing the verdict somewhere circumstances cannot reach: the arraignment.

**Arraigned: The Day's Legal Picture**

The word the verse ends on is a courtroom word — brought, summoned, arraigned — and the commentators glossed its force: the denier's appointment is with punishment, into which he will be made present whether he consents or not. The image completes the contrast's geometry: the believer's appointment is with a promise he chose to hold; the denier's is with a summons he spent his life denying would come. Both are dates; the difference is what is waiting at them. The Qur'an's constant insistence that the Hereafter is where the real trial concludes finds here one of its cleanest statements — because the verse does not describe the punishment; it describes the posture: arraigned, brought in, made present. The man who would not attend God's appointments voluntarily will attend the final one involuntarily.

**Which End of the Verse a Reader Stands On**

The verse's second-person weight falls on its listeners: the question's structure invites every audience to locate itself. The commentators' summary holds it open across the ages: whoever holds the promise — by faith, patience, and obedience, however thin the visible world treats him — stands in the first clause; whoever holds only the enjoyment — however thick the visible world treats him — stands in the second. The verse does not say the first man's life is easy; the sūrah's whole biography of Moses denies that. It says the first man's life is kept: every hardship is inside a pledge that will be honored in his presence. And that difference — between being kept and being merely comfortable — is the difference the question asks the reader to weigh, on the only scales that will still exist when both men's mornings and meals are long forgotten.

**The Promise as the Poor Believer's Portfolio**

The verse's first man holds one asset, and the verse insists the asset is enough. The commentators' Makkah context gives the sentence its teeth: the believers being addressed owned, in many cases, nothing but the pledge — their wealth had been boycotted, their trades strangled, their persons hunted — and the verse certifies their single holding as outperforming the enemy's entire balance sheet. The teaching's mechanism is the promise's Guarantor: a pledge is priced by its maker, and this pledge's maker had just spent eighty verses demonstrating His payment record — a mother repaid, a fugitive employed, an oppressed nation installed. The poor believer's portfolio therefore requires no diversification; it requires only holding. And the holding is the verse's active verb — the promise is made to those who stay within reach of it, in faith and obedience, while the enjoyment-cohort's holdings require no fidelity at all. That asymmetry is the question's last turn: the man with nothing but the pledge must keep faith with it; the man with everything but the pledge will arrive holding everything but what is asked for.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:62
> ˹Watch for˺ the Day He will call to them, “Where are those you claimed were My associate-gods?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Ends All Questions**

The verse stages the Day's opening interrogation as a single question: where are those you claimed were My associate-gods? The call is addressed to the misleaders — the commentators tied the pronoun to the leaders whose disavowal fills the next verse — and the question's genius is its smallness. Not a lecture on theophysics, not a recital of their sins: one question about location. Where are they? The objects of a lifetime's worship are summoned to appear, and their absence is the whole verdict. The commentators listed the claimed partners' kinds — idols, jinn, angels, the righteous dead — and the question works identically on each: worship's premise is presence with power to respond, and on that Day the premise is tested by a single roll call.

**"Those You Claimed"**

The phrasing indicts with one word: claimed. The partners were never real — they were assertions, and the question names them as such: those you claimed. The commentators noted that the same question recurs across the Qur'an — in chapter 6, chapter 16, chapter 40 — asked on the Day when every assertion meets its audit. The wording also distributes guilt precisely: the claimers are the ones being questioned, because the claim was theirs — fabricated, maintained, and marketed. A lifetime of assertion converts to zero on the Day when assertion and existence are finally compared. The question's grammar thus anticipates verse 63's confession: the leaders will disavow, because the question has already established that the things they promoted were products of their own mouths.

**Knowledge That Arrives Too Late to Save**

The commentators drew a hard theological fact from the scene: the resurrected will know, on that Day, the full truth of God's oneness and the prophets' message — but the knowledge will have come by necessity, not by choice. The distinction is the verse's buried doctrine: a knowledge compelled by unveiling saves no one, because faith's whole value lies in being given before compulsion. The deniers will be, in that hour, the world's most orthodox monotheists — certain, unanimous, unblinking — and their certainty will purchase nothing but the accuracy of their own sentence. The verse thereby teaches why the present moment is the only market for faith: the commodity trades freely now, under rumor and uncertainty; the Day's clearing price will be paid by all, and it will buy back nothing.

**The Roll Call's Echo Through the Sūrah**

The question is a refrain: verse 74 will ask it again, word for word, before the sūrah's final testimony scene — the repetition drumming the scenario into the listener's reflexes. The commentators connected the doubling to the Qur'an's oral pedagogy: a scene of such consequence is shown twice so that no reciter's audience can claim it was mentioned once. The scene also closes a circle the sūrah opened: Pharaoh's court legislated worship of the king (28:38) — the claim itself — and here is the claim's day in court, with the king and his theology both absent. The tyrant who demanded to be the nation's god will stand in a crowd of claimants, all equally discoverable by the same question, none of them locatable.

**What the Question Asks the Living**

The verse's address, in the end, is to the living: watch for that Day — the idiom's force is attendance, the listener positioned as witness at a trial he is scheduled to appear in. The question will be asked of others there, but it is asked of the reader here, in the only form that still matters: what are you claiming, and can it attend you where you are going? Every allegiance, every trust, every fear bowed to — salary, status, tribe, self — is a candidate for the roll call, and the verse lets the reader rehearse the answer now, while rehearsal still changes the record. The Day's question has one property no present question has: it is unanswerable by paperwork. Only what was real will be findable — and the verse's whole business, from its first basket on the river to its recited word in the marketplace, is to move the reader's dependencies from the claimed to the real while the finding is still possible.

**The Partners' Silence as Present Evidence**

The question's verdict rests on evidence available before the Day: the claimed gods have never once spoken, acted, or appeared — and their non-appearance at the roll call will simply be the continuation of a record already complete. The commentators' teaching on the scene's fairness follows: no one will be surprised into the Fire; the associates' muteness was the daily experience of their worshippers, and worship without response was maintained by insistence, not evidence. The verse therefore trains a habit of checking claims against performance: whatever you are said to be able to reach through your devotions — and the test covers modern associates as thoroughly as carved ones — ask where it has appeared, what it has answered, when it has arrived. The audit takes minutes, and its result is the same in every century: the claimed never show. Only the One who answers — who answered this Book's mothers, fugitives, and prophets within its own pages — survives the performance review, and the review's earliest session is open now, at whatever hour the reader stops insisting and starts checking. The record of that checking is called belief, and it is the only possession the verse's question will find presentable on the Day it is asked for real. Everything else in the inventory — every claim, every borrowed assurance, every delegated hope — will be standing exactly where the question leaves it: nowhere.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:63
> Those ˹misleaders˺ against whom the decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will cry, “Our Lord! These ˹followers˺ are the ones we caused to deviate. We led them into deviance, for we ourselves were deviant. We disassociate ourselves ˹from them˺ before You. It was not us that they used to worship.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence Come Due**

The speakers are identified by their legal status before they speak: those against whom the Word has come due — the Qur'an's idiom for a sentence whose term has fully run, the requital that was pre-recorded and has now matured. The commentators traced the phrase's uses: the Word of punishment, falling when a people's refusal completes its quota. That these men speak at all — that the misleaders get a speech in the Hereafter's court — is the scene's sharpest lesson: the Day is not silent; it is interrogative, and even the damned must file their answers. Their answer, however, is not defense but surrender of their last asset: their following. The men who spent their lives collecting allegiance spend their first words of the Hereafter liquidating it.

**The Confession's Anatomy**

The speech proceeds in four moves, and the commentators anatomized each: they led these people astray; they themselves were astray first; they disavow their followers before God; and — the stunning close — it was not us they worshipped. The first two clauses establish the chain of corruption: deviation propagates like infection, and the carrier was infected before he infected — which is why verse 42's curse was earned. The third clause is the severance: disavowal, the same scene the Qur'an stages in chapter 2 — those who were followed will disown those who followed, when they see the punishment, and the bonds between them will be cut. The fourth clause is the scene's coldest fact: the followers did not, in the end, worship their leaders; they worshipped their own attachments, using the leaders as furniture.

**"It Was Not Us They Worshipped"**

The final clause deserves its own reading, because it rewrites the whole economy of misguidance. The commentators gave the classical explanation: the followers' worship was never really directed at the men they followed — it was directed at their own desires, and the leaders merely embodied the desires' permissions. The idol, the chief, the influencer of any age is a mirror-mechanism: the crowd worships its own reflected appetite, polished by a personality. The leaders' declaration is thus technically true and morally worthless — a precise confession deployed as an alibi. The Qur'an elsewhere shows where the filing ends: every soul will be pinned with what it did, and the desires that selected the false objects of worship are the worshipper's own. The clause teaches the living to audit their worship's real object: whatever cannot be renounced is the thing being served, whatever name it passes under.

**The Followers Left Holding Nothing**

The scene's asymmetry completes its warning: the misleaders keep their confession, and the misled keep the bill. The Qur'an returns to this severance repeatedly — the followers will wish they had a means of return when they see the punishment — and the commentators read the pattern as the standing fate of secondhand religion: whoever lets another carry his accountability has handed his deed to a man with his own sentence to serve. The bond that united them, chapter 2 says, will be cut; chapter 23 adds that no kinship will remain between them on that Day. The misleaders' speech is thus the last thing their followers ever receive from them — a public disavowal — and the Qur'an shows it to the living precisely so that no one buys dependence twice: once with attention now, and once with damnation then.

**The Confession as Curriculum**

Set beside the sūrah's arc, the verse completes the misleaders' biography: verse 41 made them leaders inviting to the Fire; verse 63 shows them resigning the post at the Fire's gate. The resignation's usefulness is entirely downstream — it teaches the living. The commentators' practical reading stands behind the scene: follow only whom you would trust to speak for you in that court, because follow is a legal act with a Hereafter clause. The misleaders' own words are the believer's best inoculation: they were astray first; they are disavowing; they were never the object anyway. A follower who hears this verse has been shown his leader's future speech in advance — and the verse's quiet question to every reader is whether the speech he is pre-reading is one he intends to deserve.

**The Misleader's Own Explanation of Misguidance**

The speech is also the Qur'an's clearest self-report of how corruption propagates, delivered by the propagator: we led them astray because we were astray first. The commentators' root-study holds the sentence's mechanism — the enticer and the erring share one family of words, because the second state produces the first: no one drags others toward a pit he does not believe is a road. The confession thereby instructs the living twice over. The follower learns that his misleader's guidance was sincere delusion, not magic — a man walking downhill, waving — and can therefore be outwalked by anyone who turns. And the would-be guide learns the sequence's precondition: no one qualifies to lead others anywhere until his own road is audited, because the audit's result is the direction his influence will flow. The verse's placement in the sūrah — right after Pharaoh's dynasty and Korah's treasury have both been introduced — makes the census of the astray uncomfortably crowded: the misleaders of this chapter are kings and financiers, and the confession awaiting them is one the living can still hear being rehearsed. The verse's gift is that the rehearsal happens in the courtroom of the Book rather than the courtroom of the fire — heard here, the confession can be pre-empted; heard there, it can only be entered into the record. Between the two venues stands the whole remaining length of the reader's life, which is exactly as long as the window for choosing which kind of guide to be and which kind to leave.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:64
> It will be said ˹to the disbelievers˺, “Call upon your associate-gods ˹for help˺.” So they will call them, but will receive no response. And they will face the punishment, wishing they had been ˹rightly˺ guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Is a Sentence**

The Day's procedure continues: they are told to call upon their partners — and the command is the punishment wearing imperative's grammar. The commentators explained the scene's cruelty-by-exactness: the command gives them precisely what they chose, since calling upon other than God was the life's whole practice; now the practice is permitted to complete itself, once, in the one hour calling matters. The commentators added the scene's coldest note: not even the callers will expect an answer — the call is part of the chastisement, an exercise whose futility they will perceive as they perform it. To pray into silence, knowing the silence is final, is the sentence's signature: hell's irony is administrative.

**No Answer**

The verse states the outcome in three words: they will not answer them. The objects called — idols, jinn, the exalted dead — share one property in the Qur'an's accounting: they have never once answered, in any age, in any court. The commentators gathered the pattern from earlier chapters: those you call upon cannot create a fly, cannot own a speck, cannot hear — the call's futility is not new information on the Last Day; it is old information finally admitted. The scene's teaching for the living is therefore about the present tense: the gods that never answered in life will not begin in death, and the evidence of their muteness was available every day the worshipper called and nothing came. The Day simply moves the discovery from the deniable register to the undeniable one.

**Wishing They Had Been Guided**

The verse closes on the regret's exact anatomy: they will see the punishment and wish they had been rightly guided. The commentators read the wish as the conditional's exposed half — had they been guided, they would be saved from the Fire — and the reading matters because guidance's absence is now priced: it cost them everything visible from this side of the punishment. The regret completes the pattern the sūrah has built: the wrongdoer will bite his nails in regret, wishing he had taken the way along with the Messenger. Wishing, in the Hereafter, is the only currency the unguided hold — and the verse's placement teaches the living that wishing is issued too late to be legal tender: the time that mints regret into something else — repentance, return, reform — is the time the wishers spent declining.

**The Liturgy They Practiced, Graded**

The scene is, in a strict sense, an examination of practice: the disbelievers are tested on their lifelong liturgy, and the test's result is the practice's own record. The commentators' wider point stands behind the specific scene: every person's worship will be examined by the same method — call upon what you actually served. The verse thereby defines idolatry functionally rather than ritually: it is whatever a person calls upon — wealth, position, strength, approval — when certainty collapses and the real need arrives. Most people will discover their theology only in crisis; the verse simply schedules the discovery. The believer's discipline, learned here, is to conduct the audit in advance: in the small panics of an ordinary week, observe what gets called — and correct the dial before the Day grades it.

**Answered Prayer as the Mark of the Real**

The scene's dark background lights up the believer's situation by contrast: the God of this sūrah answers — He answered a mother by a river, a fugitive under a tree, a prophet's plea at a burning bush, and the verse's whole chapter is an archive of responses. The difference between the two courts is the difference between a religion of answered address and a liturgy of recorded silence. The commentators' summary carries the contrast into worship's quality: the believer calls upon One who answered before, and the answer's memory is itself a form of presence. To call and be heard is the creature's oldest dignity; the verse shows what its loss looks like when the loss becomes permanent — and the showing is the last mercy the scene contains.

**The Crowd That Will Watch the Call Fail**

The verse's scene has spectators, and the commentators' wider reading of the Qur'an's Hereafter crowd-scapes includes them: the audience of the failed call is every soul whose worship was directed correctly. The design is instructional — the destruction of the fabrication is displayed, not merely recorded, because its end is part of the lesson for those who never built one. The scene's geometry completes the sūrah's doctrine of testimony: the fabricated gods fail in public so that the Truth's monopoly is public too; the verse 75 witness stands in the same courtroom where this silence is performed. For the living, the scene assigns a vantage: the believer is being seated, in advance, in the section of the court where answered prayer is the norm — and the seating is earned in the present tense, one directed call at a time. The verse's last mercy is its address: it shows the wrong crowd's end to the right crowd, while both crowds are still reachable by the same verse, in the same room, on the same morning. That shared reachability is the whole meaning of warning season — and the season, unlike the scene, is running out in real time. The verse's economy with the scene — a command, a silence, a wish — should be read as its own act of mercy: three lines to absorb in this life, sparing the reader the tuition the scene's first audience is paying forever.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:65
> And ˹watch for˺ the Day He will call to them, asking, “What response did you give to the messengers?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Audit Question**

The Day's interrogations proceed by roll call, and this one is addressed to the communities themselves: what answer did you give to the messengers? The question is an audit of reception — heaven is not asking what the messengers said, which is on the record, but what was done with what was said. The commentators connected the scene to its parallel in chapter 5, where the messengers themselves are gathered and asked the same question, and answer with the knowledge formula: We have no knowledge; You are the Knower of all unseen. The two scenes interlock: the messengers disclaim knowledge of their audiences' responses, and the audiences are then asked to supply them. The structure teaches the Day's accounting method: every delivery is traced — sent, received, answered — and the answers are collected from the mouths that owed them.

**Why the Messengers Disclaim**

The messengers' answer in the parallel verse — we have no knowledge — is among the Qur'an's most instructive moments, and the commentators glossed its purpose: the envoy certifies his own limits, declaring that the response was never his to manufacture. The disclaimers protect the audit's integrity: no messenger will pad his report, and no community can appeal to its prophet's sympathy — the question comes from the Sender, and the answer comes from the receiver. The scene thereby honors the messenger's office at the exact moment it strips him of intercessory pretense: he was a conveyor of signs, and the conveyance is complete. What remains is between each soul and the message's Sender — which is precisely where the verse's question places it.

**The Response That Was Given**

The question's phrasing carries its own reminder: a response was given — every audience answered, in practice if never in words. The commentators noted the Qur'an's realism here: disbelief is itself a reply, and so is delay, so is selective obedience, so is the applause that changes nothing. The audit does not ask whether you answered — it asks what your answer was. The scene's doctrine is the completion of verse 59's rule: a messenger was sent to every mother-city that was judged, reciting the signs; therefore the file for every people contains both the recitation and the response, matched and dated. On the Day, the reading of the file is a formality — which is why the verse's question sounds less like inquiry than like sentencing's preamble.

**The Question's Present Tense**

The verse's address to the living runs through its future scene: the response being audited there is the one being composed here. The commentators' practical reading follows: every hearer of a messenger is, right now, writing the answer that will be read back to him — and the writing happens in decisions too small to feel historic: the postponed prayer, the unshared truth, the entertained contempt. The verse dignifies the ordinary by revealing its destiny: nothing answered to a messenger is forgotten, because the response is the subject of heaven's oldest open file. The living reader who grasps this stops treating sermons as weather and starts treating them as correspondence — each one a letter awaiting a reply that will one day be read aloud to its author, in the presence of the Sender.

**Mercy's Mirror in the Audit**

The verse's hard procedure sits inside a mercy that the sūrah has documented for eighty verses: the audit exists because the deliveries existed. No community is questioned about a message it never received — the rule verse 59 legislated — and no question is asked here except the one whose answer the hearers already possess, having lived it. The Day's questions, in the Qur'an's portraits, never solicit new information; they transfer the file from the hidden register to the public one. The mercy is in the timing: the same question — what did you do with the message? — can be asked by the reader's own conscience tonight, at no cost, with the full means of changing the answer still on the table. The verse's scene is thus a preview sold at the price of attention: the cheapest audit ever offered, and the only one whose failure can still be cured.

**The Messengers' Presence at the Audit**

The verse's question implies the messengers' attendance — one cannot ask a community what it answered to its messengers unless the messengers can hear the answer — and the scene's staging honors the office one final time. The commentators' parallel makes the picture complete: the messengers are gathered, questioned, and rendered witnesses; their communities are questioned, and their answers read against the sendings. The envoy who spent his mission ignored, mocked, or exiled stands present at the reading of his own work's reception — and the standing is itself the reward the office was promised: the message delivered is the mission accomplished, whatever the market did with the delivery. The scene thus closes the emotional account of every rejected warner the sūrah has shown: Noah shouting through the years, Moses before the court, the Prophet in the boycott — all of them are present at the audit, vindicated not by their audiences' conversions but by the completeness of the exchange. The verse's lesson for every carrier of the message since is carried in that image: deliver it fully, and let the response be the response — the audit will keep both records straight. And the audit's symmetry comforts the audience too: no one will be asked what they never received, and no one who received will be asked nothing. The exchange, on that Day, is complete in both directions — which is the whole difference between a God who audited His deliveries and a world that never audited anything at all.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:66
> They will be too dumbstruck on that Day to ask one another ˹for answers˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Silence After the Questions**

The verse records the crowd's condition rather than any answer: the tidings will be obscured for them on that Day, and they will not question one another. The commentators gave the two classical readings — the arguments they might have raised are blotted out, or the news of one another is blotted out — and both converge on the same portrait: a humanity reduced to its own file, with no collateral left to consult. The scene follows the audit question of verse 65: asked what they answered to the messengers, the assembled have nothing to offer — no plea to trade, no neighbor to poll, no precedent to cite. Speech itself is the faculty being sentenced, in a day where speech was the currency of every refusal they ever made.

**No One to Consult**

The second reading is the scene's social wound: they will not be able to ask one another. The commentators balanced this against the verses that show mutual questioning among the condemned and the blessed — and the reconciliation matters: questioning of the consultative kind, the kind that builds an excuse, is over. The commentators tied the silence to the terror of the Day, and the psychology is exact: consultation is a leisure activity; it presumes time, safety, and the possibility that the answer changes something. On that Day all three presumptions die together. Every soul arrives preoccupied with itself — the Qur'an says the guilty will wish to be ransomed from the punishment of that Day by their children — and the crowd becomes, in the strictest sense, a crowd of ones.

**The End of the Excuse Economy**

The first reading carries the verse's legal weight: the tidings — the pleas, the arguments — are obscured, meaning no valid argument remains available. The commentators' summary is precise: the Hereafter is the one venue where advocacy runs out, because its cases were all adjudicable in life and every extension has expired. The Qur'an has documented the excuse economy across this sūrah — no messenger came (retired by statute in verse 59), the message was sorcery (refuted by its own craftsmen's surrender), the standard was Moses (answered by his people's own converts) — and verse 66 shows the economy's final closure: not one surviving plea, not even the mutual reassurance that wrong is normal. The silence is not imposed from outside; it is the natural condition of a market whose goods have all expired.

**A Day Without Small Talk**

The verse's portrait includes what will not happen — no inquiries about one another — and the detail measures the Day's transformation of ordinary sociality. In this life, questioning one another is constant: news, comparison, alliance, gossip — the whole traffic of mutual reference. The commentators pointed to the opposite scenes: the Garden's people ask one another in peace, and the questioning is part of the felicity. The difference teaches what talk is for: in the abode of security, speech is surplus, spent on one another freely; in the abode of exposure, speech collapses to each soul's minimum. The verse's reader holds both pictures and the hinge between them: today, while asking one another is still possible, the only questions worth the traffic are the ones whose answers survive the obscuring — what did I answer to the message, and what am I doing about it before the asking ends.

**Silence as the Sentence's First Installment**

The verse's placement makes the silence a sentence in stages: the questions are asked (65), the answers fail, and then the whole apparatus of collective defense shuts down (66). The commentators heard in the sequence the Day's pedagogy: humanity, which refused every private warning in committee and chorus, is silenced as a chorus first — each voice left alone with its own file. For the believer, the verse describes the one silence worth rehearsing: the quiet in which a soul, stripped of its networks, meets its own record with nothing between them. The rehearsals happen in this life's small hospitals of solitude — the sleepless hour, the graveside, the honest night prayer — and each rehearsal makes the great silence less of a stranger. The verse's promise, read from the far side, is also the believer's comfort: the day that silences the crowd leaves the individual conscience standing, and that is the one faculty heaven spent a lifetime training.

**The Question That Will Still Be Possible**

The verse's silence has one exception, and the commentators located it in the sūrah's own sequence: the questions God asks will be asked — the roll call, the audit, the demand for proof — while the questions creatures ask one another will be over. The asymmetry defines the Day's protocol: inquiry flows one direction, from the throne outward, because the only remaining unknowns belong to the records; nothing anyone could ask a neighbor would alter a file already closed. The verse's present-tense instruction follows: the season for horizontal questions is now — ask the learned what the Book means, ask the honest how they repented, ask yourself what your own record is accruing — because this is the one venue where the asking still moves the ledger. The commentators' practical counsel fits the verse like a glove: spend the days of open consultation on the questions that will survive the obscuring, and treat every deferred inquiry as an appointment being made for the Day when the consultation desks close and the file speaks for itself. The verse's hush, read from this side of the grave, is therefore not a threat but a schedule: the questions that matter are being asked now, quietly, in every heart that reads this page — and the wisdom of the age is to answer them while the answering is still cheap.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:67
> As for those who repent, believe, and do good ˹in this world˺, it is right to hope that they will be among the successful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Door the Verse Restores**

After four verses of arraignment, silence, and unanswered calls, the verse swings open a door: but as for one who repents — the particle of exception doing the work of a whole gospel. The commentators placed the verse's mercy precisely: it stands between the scenes of the condemned, so that no listener leaves the recitation trapped in the courtroom. The sequence is the Qur'an's standard mercy-map: show the end of refusal in full — the roll call, the disavowal, the unanswered prayer — and then, while the fear is still warm, name the exit: repentance, faith, righteous work. The door's placement is its eloquence: it is announced at the exact moment the listener can feel what it is worth.

**The Three Verbs in Their Order**

The verse's ladder is three verbs, and the commentators read the order as doctrine. Repentance comes first: the return that undoes the past — and its placement before belief teaches that God's door opens before the creed is complete, on the strength of the turning itself. Belief comes second: the interior re-founding that repentance begins. Righteous work comes third: the faith's public body, deeds as the belief's circulation. The order forbids two errors at once: it refuses the ritualist's claim that works without return avail, and it refuses the sentimentalist's claim that feeling without work completes anything. The ladder also answers the sūrah's own darkest scenes — Pharaoh's last-moment cry was belief without a life, and the sea graded it; the repentance this verse honors is the one with a lifetime's work attached, however short the lifetime.

**"It Is Right to Hope"**

The promise's phrasing is among the gentlest in the Book: perhaps he will be among the successful — and the commentators delivered the classical rule about such particles: when God speaks a perhaps, it is not uncertainty but mercy of address — a promise made to sound like hope so that the striving it commands feels like pursuit rather than compliance. The rule's consequence is exact: the verse is a guarantee dressed as a possibility, and the dress is for the wearer's dignity — success that arrives as pure decree would produce no love. The commentators also heard the word's echo of verse 61's fine promise: the same receiver of the pledge, now described by his verbs. The hope is thus the currency of the relationship: God speaks in hope's grammar so that the servant may live in hope's energy.

**Successful: The Day's Only Rank**

The verse's goal-word — the successful — is the Qur'an's standing term for the winners of the Hereafter, the people of the Garden in its repeated refrain. The commentators noted the word's commercial root: to prosper, to achieve one's aim — success as the outcome of a venture, not the accident of a status. The choice of term retroactively appraises the sūrah's whole cast: Pharaoh, who held the age's maximum of everything, fails; Korah, introduced in the verses just ahead as the superpower of wealth, fails; and the repentant workman, holding nothing visible, is ranked with the winners. The vocabulary teaches the reader the Day's ledger: all other balances are preliminaries; success is the one column that survives.

**The Verse's Reach Backward and Forward**

The verse's exception clause reaches backward through the entire sūrah — the mother who believed a promise, the daughter who advised a hire, the believing man of Pharaoh's court, the convert of the People of the Book — all of them are the verse's category in narrative form. And it reaches forward into the reader's own file: whatever the record holds — the sūrah has shown murders, flights, and failures all forgiven on a single sentence of return — the three verbs re-rank a life. The commentators' summary is the verse's standing use: the Hereafter's scenes are shown in severity so that the severity will be believed, and the exception is shown in the same breath so that the severity will not be the last word. The last word belongs, in this Book, to whoever turns.

**The Word "Successful" Against the Chapter's Cast**

The verse's goal-word retroactively grades every biography the sūrah has told, and the grading is the verse's quietest argument. Pharaoh held a nation, a title, and a river — unsuccessful. Korah, about to be introduced, will hold the age's maximum fortune — unsuccessful. The mother of Moses held a promise and one night's obedience — of her the chapter says she was among the believers; of the man who owned the river's bank it says he was of the corrupters. The commentators' commercial reading of the word carries the lesson home: success is achieving one's actual aim, and the verse has just re-stated what the aim is — the promised reward, attained by the turning, the believing, and the working. Every other aim the chapter's cast pursued — thrones, towers, treasuries, the square's applause — failed by definition, because they were aimed at the world's ledger. The verse leaves its reader a single diagnostic question, portable to any life: by the Day's definition, is what you are pursuing success — or only payment? The question needs no scholarship to answer, only honesty about the aim, and honesty is the first of the three verbs the verse has just made the door. And the door's placement in this chapter is not decoration: it is the verse's claim that after Pharaoh's sea and before Korah's quake, with the wreckage of both extremes still on the page, the ordinary reader — unremarkable, unpromoted, unparaded — is being told the standing odds of the successful, and they run in his favor the moment he turns.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:68
> Your Lord creates and chooses whatever He wills—the choice is not theirs. Glorified and Exalted is Allah above what they associate ˹with Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Chosen**

The verse answers the oldest objection to every messenger: your Lord creates what He wills and chooses — and the choice is not theirs. The commentators named the objectors: Qurayshis who thought the prophetic office belonged to a man of wealth and standing rather than to a poor orphan's son, and Jews who thought the prophet should have come from Israel rather than Arabia. The verse's reply is a doctrine of selection: God creates candidates and chooses messengers by His own counsel, and no committee of the passed-over holds veto. The objection's persistence is its own evidence — every age has re-filed it, proposing its own criteria, and every age has been answered by the same verse: the choice was never in the market's hands.

**"No Choice Is Theirs"**

The clause's scope drew the commentators' wider readings: the choice of prophets, the prerogative of intercession, and — widest — all matters: it is God, not the creature, who decides. The core sense is fixed: prophethood is not an election, and the divine selections' unpopularity is part of their signature — Moses the fugitive with a speech impediment, the boy Muhammad of the orphaned household, chosen over the notables who financed the idols. The doctrine protects the message from its audience: a prophet chosen by consensus would preach the consensus, and revelation exists precisely because the consensus is wrong. The clause also disciplines the believer's own preferences: the fit and the chosen are not the same file, and heaven's appointments regularly scandalize the qualified.

**Creation and Choice as One Act**

The verse pairs the two verbs — creates and chooses — and the pairing is its theology: the same hand that makes the material selects the instrument. The commentators transmitted al-Ṭabarī's preferred reading — Your Lord creates what He wills and chooses the choicest of what they have — God selecting the best available from among the very people objecting to the selection. The reading turns the objection inside out: the objectors are, in the same breath, the inventory being drawn from — the choice ran through their own households and passed over their own criteria. The doctrine lands on every generation's merited discomfort: God's choices are made from among us, which means our exclusion from any office is never scarcity of candidates but the election's freedom — and freedom is the Sender's, as the verse says twice.

**Glorified Above the Association**

The verse closes with the liturgical formula of distance: glory to God, and high is He above what they associate. The commentators read the clause as the creed that follows the doctrine: since the choice is His alone, associating partners with Him is not merely error but category invasion — creatures ranking themselves into the selection, or gods into the Godhead. The formula's placement after the election doctrine ties idolatry to insubordination: the same men who resisted the Prophet's choice were the men maintaining idols — both are rejections of heaven's right to select. The Qur'an's logic is seamless here: acknowledge His creation and choice, and the associations collapse; contest either, and the idols stand ready to fill the vacancy the objector has engineered.

**Submission as the Trained Outcome**

The commentators transmitted, from the Sufi master al-Shādhilī, the verse's inward fruit: forsake the directing of things, and if you must direct, direct yourself not to direct — the art of aligning one's will with the Will that chooses. The teaching is not passivity: the creature's sphere of choice — effort, repentance, work — is real and commanded; what is surrendered is the directorship of outcomes and offices. The verse thus heals the believer's two chronic inflammations: envy of others' appointments (the choice was not yours to give) and anxiety over one's own (the choice was not yours to secure). Between those two releases lives the actual spiritual life: work assigned, results owned by the Chooser, and the heart's constant refrain matching the verse's close — glory to Him above what we would have arranged instead.

**The Choice Demonstrated in the Chapter's Own Cast**

The verse's doctrine is not abstract to this sūrah: the chapter is a gallery of selections that no human committee would have ratified. Moses — a fugitive with a killing on his record and a stammer on his tongue — chosen over Egypt's trained orators. The mother of Moses — an unnamed slave-nation woman — chosen to receive direct inspiration over the palace's priestly class. The sister — a child — assigned the surveillance the armies missed. The believing man inside Pharaoh's court — one out of a whole notability — chosen to argue alone against the unanimous vote.
