And the Prophet of Makkah — the orphan of the clan that lost its fathers early — chosen over the chiefs who financed the idols. The commentators' principle reads the gallery whole: God's choices are made from among the people, and the people's surprise at each selection is the measure of how far their criteria sit from His. The verse thus converts the reader's resentment of any election — including, hardest of all, God's choices in one's own life — into the trained reflex of the submitter: the Chooser has a record, and the record is this Book. Read its cast list again after the objection next rises in the chest — and the objection will find itself standing in the company of the passed-over notables of Makkah, whose criteria the verse has already overruled in the first person of the Creator. The reader's only remaining office is trust, extended on evidence this wide.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:69
> And your Lord knows what their hearts conceal and what they reveal

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Inner Docket**

After the verse of public choice comes the verse of private knowledge: your Lord knows what they conceal and what they disclose. The commentators connected it directly to the opponents' situation: God knows their inner enmity toward the Prophet and their contempt for His choice — not merely the abuse they shouted in the market. The verse thus extends the previous doctrine into its protected zone: the creature's last privacy, the chest, is not private from the One whose choice was being resented. The Qur'an returns to this knowledge across its pages — the breasts' contents named, counted, known — and the repetition builds the believer's settled picture: the interior is a lived-in place, inspected by its Maker, not a vault with a combination only the self can reach.

**Conceal and Disclose: The Full Spectrum**

The verse's two verbs bracket the whole range of the inner life: what is hidden and what is shown. The commentators' note on the breadth matters: God's knowledge is not limited to the secrets people successfully keep; it covers the full spectrum, including the disclosed — the things worn openly — and the concealments that failed. The pairing corrects two opposite errors: the exhibitionist's belief that the shown self is the whole self, and the conspirator's belief that the hidden self is safe. Neither register is unknown to the Reader. For the believer, the verse converts hypocrisy into a technical impossibility: the discrepancy between breast and speech is recorded as a discrepancy, not discovered by accident. The only strategy the verse leaves standing is congruence — to become, by repentance and practice, what one appears.

**Why This Verse Follows the Choice**

The verse's placement after your Lord creates and chooses is surgical: the objectors' resistance to the Prophet was conducted, like most resistance, in a double register — public mockery over private fear, printed scorn over secret conviction. The commentators' historical reading supplies the texture: some of the Prophet's fiercest opponents moved their lips at the recitation when no one watched, and some of his quietest listeners were the ones whose chests concealed the most. The verse tells the Messenger — and every messenger's successor in daʿwah — that the reception he observes is incomplete data: the contempt is seen from above, and so is the crack in the contempt. The verse thereby forbids the preacher both despair at visible rejection and confidence in visible acceptance: the file being written is written in a room he does not see.

**The Chest in the Qur'an's Anatomy**

The commentators traced the theme's reach: God knows what is in the breasts is one of the Qur'an's refrains, and the breast — ṣudūr — is the Book's named compartment for intention. The anatomy is deliberate: deeds are visible, words are audible, but motive — the deed's salt — lives in the chest, and revelation insists on naming it as the true site of the deed's worth. Two men give identical charity; the chests differ; the deeds differ. The verse's doctrine therefore lands on worship's quality control: the believer's task is not to improve the display but to audit the chest — because the display is already read, and the audit is the only work the Reader has left to the writer. The Qur'an's own prayers model the audit's language: whoever knows what is in the breasts is exactly the One to ask for a clean one.

**The Comfort Inside the Exposure**

For the believer, the verse is not only warning but asylum. The same knowledge that removes the denier's cover removes the believer's loneliness: the efforts that no one saw — the repentance no one believed, the patience no one counted, the faith that lived its hardest hours entirely unseen — are disclosed to the One audience whose notice is the notice. The commentators' reading of the verse's address to the Prophet carries this: his sincerity, doubted by every market, was certified at a single address, and the certification's persistence is the verse. Between the two registers — concealment and disclosure — the verse leaves the believer one strategy, and it is the sūrah's whole program: say and be the same thing, work for the One who reads the chest, and leave the market'sguesswork to the market.

**The Verse Against Self-Deception's Last Room**

The verse's reach includes the reader's own concealment from himself, and this is its most practical service. Most self-deception is conducted in the chest — the motive renamed before it is acted on, the desire dressed as principle, the grudge filed under honesty — and the verse closes the practice's last hiding place: the management of one's own self-report. The commentators' doctrine of the inspected breast carries a mercy inside its severity: since the interior is already read, the effort spent curating it is wasted, and the effort redirected from curating to cleaning is the only investment the file records. The believer's liberation follows: say the true thing to God about the chest's contents — the Qur'an's own prayers model the practice — because the confession that feels like exposure is actually the only conversation with a Reader who was never deceived. The verse's discipline is thus the end of double-entry bookkeeping in the soul: one ledger, kept in one place, already in the right hands — and the hands, the sūrah has spent eighty verses proving, are the ones that forgive what is honestly surrendered. The verse's last liberty is therefore the reader's: to stop performing for an audience of One who cannot be performed for, and to start speaking in the chest's own voice — the voice that will be heard exactly as it is, which is the only speech worth practicing.

---

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:70
> He is Allah. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. All praise belongs to Him in this life and the next. All authority is His. And to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Creed Stated Plain**

The verse sets down the whole creed in four clauses, without argument: He is Allah — there is no god but He; His is the praise, in the first and the last; His is the judgment; and to Him you will be returned. The commentators read the placement as the sūrah's theological seal: after eighty verses of narrative — baskets, courts, plagues, seas, treasures, trials — the chapter's argument arrives at the identity of its Narrator, stated in the naked formula of worship. The position is deliberate: the creed lands with the stories' weight behind it, because the listener has just watched this Allah act — choose, deliver, drown, provide, forgive, choose again. The verse does not ask for the formula's acceptance; it presents the formula as the summary of everything the listener has already been shown.

**Praise in the First and the Last**

The verse locates praise in two places: the first — the beginning of time, this world — and the last — the Hereafter. The commentators glossed the pairing as the totality of praise's jurisdiction: no era, world, or order exists where praise is not His due. The phrase also quietly answers the verse's own moment: the deniers praise their idols in the first world, and the verse reassigns the payment — the praise they spent elsewhere was owed here. The believer's discipline follows: praise that is practiced in the first is rehearsal for the praise that will be uncontested in the last; the Qur'an pictures the Garden's inhabitants praising morning and evening, and this verse is that eternity's job posting. Praise, in the doctrine, is the creature's home frequency — the verse merely states where it is payable and who has been collecting it in error.

**His Is the Judgment — and Then the Return**

The third clause names the running sovereignty: His is the judgment — the word carrying decision, rule, and wisdom together. The commentators paired its two registers: in this world, all creaturely authority functions only within God's delegated judgment; in the next, He judges directly between His creatures with no intermediary left standing. The clause's placement after the praise clause is doctrine: the One praised is the One ruling — worship and governance are not separate departments. And the fourth clause closes the verse on return: to Him you will be brought back — the verb of permanent arrival, the same return Pharaoh denied (28:39) and the verse's whole argument has now demonstrated at scale. The sequence — praise, judgment, return — is the believer's whole orientation compressed into a single breath: live praised, governed, and homeward.

**The Formula Against the Sūrah's Cast**

The verse's four clauses quietly grade the sūrah's entire cast list. Pharaoh claimed lordship — the third clause answers: judgment is His. Hamān administered the claim — the fourth answers: everyone returns. The daughters' household trusted God's promise — the first clause names the only trustworthy Object. Korah, arriving next, will attribute wealth to his own knowledge — the second clause reassigns praise. The commentators' method of reading such verses against the narrative is the sūrah's own design: the formula is not abstract theology; it is the file label for every story just told. The listener who recites the verse has, in four clauses, the verdict on each character — including the one holding the Book.

**The Seal's Practical Weight**

The verse's placement at the section's close also gives the reader his marching order. The creed stated here is not information to be stored but a structure to be lived in: praise practiced as a habit, authority consulted before every decision, return expected before every acquisition. The commentators' summary carries the verse's use: whoever recites it has re-registered every transaction of his day under new management — meals under praise, plans under judgment, possessions under the return. The verse is thus the sūrah's hinge in miniature: behind it, the stories; ahead of it, Korah's treasure, the trial's verses, and the Lord of the worlds still choosing, providing, and replying. The creed holds the chapter together because it holds the world together — and the verse's plainness is its credential: the truth, at the end of all the stories, is short enough to say in one breath.

**Praise as the Verse's Opening Movement**

Notice that the creed begins with praise, not command: His is the praise — before the judgment, before the return, the verse establishes the relationship's register. The commentators' placement-reading catches the design: the sūrah has just displayed eighty verses of God's acting — and the creed's first clause teaches the correct response to the display: not terror, not negotiation, but praise. The sequence matters pastorally: a creature who meets sovereignty first learns to hide; a creature who meets praise-worthiness first learns to sing. The verse's order is the Qur'an's consistent catechesis — the God of the burning bush introduced Himself with mercy before commission — and the believer's own prayer life is trained by the same order: the chapters of the Book open with praise, the day's prayers open with praise, and the Hereafter's liturgy, as the Garden scenes show, is praise without interruption. The creed's four clauses thus begin where the relationship begins: His is the praise — and everything the verse says after that is said inside the warmth of that first clause. A reader who has learned the order will find it changing the reflexes: the blessing answered with blessing's name, the trial endured inside the praise's jurisdiction, and the whole day, first to last, conducted as the verse conducts its own sentence — from praise, through judgment, homeward.

---

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:71
> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Imagine if Allah were to make the night perpetual for you until the Day of Judgment, which god other than Allah could bring you sunlight? Will you not then listen?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Thought Experiment of the Locked Night**

The verse issues a challenge in the form of a hypothesis: if God made the night permanent until the Day of Resurrection — which god besides Allah could bring you light? The commentators identified the challenge's audience precisely: the idolaters who worshipped powers other than God were asked to test their powers against a single counterfactual — one switched setting in the sky. The question's structure is the Qur'an's signature method at its cleanest: no new miracle is demanded of heaven; the existing miracle is simply withdrawn in imagination. The day's reliability is such that no one doubts it — and the verse asks the audience to notice what that reliability certifies: whatever holds the night's ledger holds everything below it. The challenge's power is that it cannot be answered: no idol, no astral power, no chief has ever opened a morning.

**"Which God Other Than Allah"**

The question's phrase does the theology: which god other than Allah — inviting the listener to poll every candidate he has ever honored. The commentators noted the question's silence about the candidates' names: it lets the hearer supply his own pantheon, which is why the verse has never expired. The test is functional rather than theoretical: a god is that whose invocation addresses the need — and the need here is the most universal one in the species' history, light. Against the equation, every associate-god is discovered to be decoration: worshipped, feared, fed, and utterly without jurisdiction over a single sunrise. The phrase also carries the verse's irony: the deniers' gods are being asked, for once, to do something observable — and their advocates' silence at the question is the answer.

**"Will You Not Then Listen"**

The verse closes on an odd verb for an argument about light: will you not hear? The commentators caught the pairing's design: the ear is the night's organ — in darkness, hearing is the sense that carries a person — so the question of the perpetual night is addressed to the faculty that the night proves. The correspondence is exact: verse 72's day-question ends with will you not see?, the day's organ. The two questions are thus tailored to their hemispheres, and the tailoring teaches the verse's deeper method: God's signs are audited by whichever sense each sign serves — and each of the reader's own faculties is being recruited as its own witness. The listener who will not hear a question about hearing has exhausted the verse's remedies; the question's repetition in the next verse shows the Reader willing to try the other sense.

**The Mercy Inside the Hypothesis**

The perpetual night is hypothetical — and the hypothesis's non-fulfillment is the daily evidence. Every morning in the audience's lifetime had arrived on schedule: the sun that Pharaoh claimed lordship over, the light the Quraysh traded under, the dawn Moses' mother rose to — the same event, relaunched without a single failure across the species' whole memory. The commentators' reading of the verse as an invitation to gratitude follows: the counterfactual's horror is the gratitude's ledger — the switching of night and day is not a natural right but a standing mercy, withheld from no generation by the One who could withhold it by a single command. The verse thus converts an astronomical routine into a theological audit: what would you pay, in that locked night, for one hour of this morning you walked through untouched?

**The Question's Recipient List**

The verse's final reach is inward: everyone who prays to anything for anything is on the question's recipient list. The commentator tradition's wider application holds: the perpetual night stands for every darkness a life can enter — the lost livelihood, the buried hope, the long grief — and the question holds there too: whom do you call when the lights go out? The named gods fail at both scales — they cannot spin the planet, and they cannot lift the chest. The One who can is the One the verse names, and the verse's gift to the listener is the discovery that the two needs — cosmic and personal — are one supply chain. The reader who has ever been answered in a small darkness holds experimental evidence for the large one; the question's last office is to make the evidence conscious: will you not then listen?

**The Question's Pedagogical Kindness**

The verse's method deserves a final word of appreciation: instead of announcing God's power over light, the verse asks the audience to perform the thought experiment themselves — the conclusion is reached in the reader's own mind, which is why it cannot be shaken off. The commentators' attention to the question's form marks the Qur'an's larger pedagogy: it does not merely inform; it makes the listener conduct the inquiry, because self-reached conclusions become property while announced ones remain luggage. The kindness in the method is its humility: the verse assumes the listener can reason, hands him the premises, and steps back. No coercion, no threat — one question about the sky, addressed to the sense that operates in the dark. The believer equipped with this verse owns a calling instrument of perfect gentleness: ask the question as it stands, in any company, in any age — and let the listener's own mind close the circuit the verse opened. A question that completes itself in the hearer's chest needs no defender afterward, which is why the verse's argument has outlived every empire that once forbade it — the sky kept supplying the evidence, and the question kept handing it over. The verse stands, in that sense, as the argument's permanent supply line: unanswerable, nightly renewed, and free.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:72
> Ask ˹them also˺, “Imagine if Allah were to make the day perpetual for you until the Day of Judgment, which god other than Allah could bring you night to rest in? Will you not then see?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror Challenge: The Locked Day**

The counterfactual flips: if God made the day permanent until the Day of Resurrection — which god besides Allah could bring you night to rest in? The pairing with verse 71 is deliberate architecture: the two hypotheses together cover the whole cycle, proving that whichever half is withdrawn, the other half's restoration is beyond every associate-god's reach. The commentators explained the day's gift that the hypothesis removes: rest — the night as the creature's appointed shelter from exposure and labor. A perpetual day is not a blessing multiplied; it is a blessing weaponized: work without end, sight without mercy, heat without shadow. The verse's insight is psychological as much as astronomical: the human being does not only need light — he needs the ending of light, and no power he worships can supply an ending.

**Rest as a Named Provision**

The verse is unusually explicit about the night's purpose: that you may rest therein. The commentators connected this to the Qur'an's wider doctrine of the night's design — the night as a covering, the sleep as a rest — and the explicitness matters against the age's own economics: rest is treated by the striving world as loss, and the verse files it as provision. The creature's dignity includes the right to stop, and the stop is engineered into the sky. The doctrine quietly re-grades a life's inventory: the person who can sleep is rich in a currency no market lists, and the verse's counterfactual prices it — the locked day's subjects would trade their whole endless productivity for one hour of the dark they currently curse at bedtime. Rest, the verse teaches, is delivered nightly by the same Hand the deniers refuse to name.

**"Will You Not Then See"**

The closing question switches organs: will you not see? — the day's faculty, as the commentators noted, matching verse 71's ear. The design completes the two-verse apparatus: each hemisphere's question is addressed to the sense that hemisphere serves, so that the reader's own body convicts him — the eyes that enjoy the day's light cannot fail to see the day's Argument, and the ears that carry him through the night cannot fail to hear the night's. The rhetoric leaves no neutral faculty: every sense is deputized. The commentators' wider point follows: denial is not an information failure — the evidence is piped through the deny-er's own senses, daily, at full brightness; refusal is the only word left for it, and the verse's question makes the refusal conscious by asking it to look at what it is refusing.

**The Two Verses as One Proof**

Taken together, the twin hypotheses constitute the Qur'an's argument from cosmic maintenance: the alternation of night and day is a service, and every service requires a maintainer. The commentators listed the theme's stations across the Book — the night and day as two signs in chapter 17, the alternation as a sign for the people who reason — and this sūrah's version adds the experiment: interrupt the service in either direction, and watch every rival explanation stand silent. The proof's strength is its daily observability: it does not depend on reports of ancient wonders or texts of disputed transmission — the evidence is in the sky at this moment, and the challenge is simply to imagine its suspension. The verse thereby equips the believer with an argument he can run anywhere on earth, at any hour, without a single book: no one below can end the day; no one below can end the night; the Being who does so hourly is the One the Verse names.

**The Spiritual Reading the Tradition Added**

The commentators transmitted the passage's inward reading: night and day as the soul's own seasons — the days of clarity and expansion, the nights of confusion and contraction — and the same Lord governing both. The teaching's balance is the Sufi tradition's stock-in-trade: too much daylight in the soul breeds boldness and importunity; too much night breeds despair and timidity; the alternation is the curriculum. The verse's counterfactual then reads inwardly as well: which of your idols could end either season? — no possession can shorten a night of grief, no achievement can lengthen a day of openness. The Reader who governs the outer cycle governs the inner one, and the believer's skill is to receive both hemispheres as provision: work when the day is given, rest when the night is sent — in the sky, and in the chest.

**The Night as the Day's Proof of Mercy**

The verse's hypothesis exposes a fact the working world forgets: the ending of the day is as much provision as the day itself. The commentators' reading of the night's gifts gathers them — the covering that restores, the cooling that spares the earth's surface, the stillness that returns the birds and quiets the markets — and each is a service no associate-god ever rendered. The locked-day scenario also answers a question the prosperous rarely ask: what keeps acquisition from consuming the acquirer? The night does — the scheduled, universal stop that no empire has ever negotiated away. Every curfew of nature, from the sleepless executive's collapse to the closed exchanges of the weekend, is the night's jurisdiction reasserting itself. The verse teaches the reader to file the stop correctly: not as interruption of the blessing but as its other half — mercy, rotating — and to distrust, on schedule, any life built as if the daylight were permanent.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:73
> It is out of His mercy that He has made for you the day and night so that you may rest ˹in the latter˺ and seek His bounty ˹in the former˺, and perhaps you will be grateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to Both Hypotheses**

The verse resolves the twin experiments with the actual arrangement: out of His mercy He made for you the night and the day. The commentators read it as the hypothesis's answer: the alternation the listener was asked to imagine suspended is, in reality, an active mercy — designed, maintained, and aimed at the creature's twin needs. The sentence's structure assigns the design its purpose in both directions: rest in the night, seeking in the day — the two clauses matching the two counterfactuals verse by verse. The verse thereby converts the sky from backdrop to correspondence: every dusk and dawn is a delivery, addressed to you, sent from mercy. The word mercy is the verse's thesis: the cycle is not a neutral mechanism but care, in rotation.

**Rest and Seeking as Paired Offices**

The verse names the two halves' functions precisely: that you may rest, and that you may seek of His bounty. The commentators noted the division of labor: night for restoration, day for pursuit — and the pursuit is defined as seeking His bounty, which quietly re-labels all labor. Work, in the verse's grammar, is not self-supply but petition in motion: the farmer, the merchant, the scholar are all seekers at a Provider's door, and the day is the door's opening hours. The pairing also balances a culture at each extreme: the idle are corrected by the day's command to seek, and the strive-sick are corrected by the night's command to stop. A life lived in the verse's rhythm holds both offices in rotation, and the rotation — as the previous verses showed — is under management.

**"Made for You"**

The verse's middle phrase carries its warmth: He made for you — the design addressed to its beneficiaries. The commentators heard in the construction the personal register of all provision: the cycle's scale is planetary, but its addressee is singular — you. The teaching dignifies the ordinary: the reader's last night's sleep was a designed delivery, and this morning's light is addressed mail. The phrase also grounds the verse's argument from mercy: provision that is for you implies a Giver who knows you — and the knowledge theme of verse 69 stands behind this one: the One who reads the chest designed the schedule the chest rests by. The believer's gratitude, in this light, is not a mood but an accurate reading of the arrangement's address label.

**"Perhaps You Will Be Grateful"**

The verse closes on its standing purpose: perhaps you will give thanks — the Qur'an's gentlest imperative, and the commentators' rule about the divine perhaps applies: a certainty in mercy's clothing. Gratitude is named as the cycle's intended harvest, and the placement teaches the mechanism: the experience of rest and provision is designed to produce the response — but the response is the reader's. The verse's realism holds: most mornings pass unthanked, which is why the perhaps. The teaching's practical form is the tradition's oldest prescription: tie the thanks to the event — the waking, the meal, the earned wage — because gratitude unattached to its occasions evaporates into sentiment. The verse's design gives the occasions hourly; the harvest is left to the field.

**Mercy's Rotation as the Believer's Clock**

The verse's lasting gift is a way of keeping time. The believer who reads it owns a clock with theology built in: every dusk is mercy's rest-shift beginning, every dawn mercy's seeking-shift — and the schedule's reliability, as the two hypothesis-verses proved, has never once failed. The commentators' spiritual reading completes the clock's face: the soul's own nights and days run on the same mercy — contraction for rest, expansion for seeking — and the same Gratitude is the intended harvest of both. The verse thus leaves its reader with a day structured as worship: sleep as trust, work as petition, and the two handovers — dusk and dawn — as the hooks on which the thanks is hung. Whoever keeps this clock has already answered the verse's perhaps, and the answer will keep arriving, twice a day, for the length of a life.

**The Seeker's License in the Day's Design**

The verse's clause about the day — that you may seek of His bounty — deserves its own hearing as permission. Labor, in the verse's design, is not the curse of a fallen world but the assigned channel of provision: the day is made for the seeking, and the seeking is directed at His bounty — which makes every honest occupation a form of prayer conducted with the hands. The commentators' reading dignifies the whole spectrum of work: the farmer's field, the merchant's caravan, the craftman's bench are all doors of the same bounty, and the verse removes the false ranking between "spiritual" and "worldly" effort — both are the day's business, and both are addressed to the Giver. The clause also carries the seeking's ethics in miniature: bounty sought of Him is bounty sought lawfully, because the address defines the channel. A generation that reads its work through this clause acquires the believer's working posture: diligence as trust's evidence, earnings as correspondence, and the evening's ledger closed with the same word the verse ends on — thanks. The rotation's mercy, practiced long enough, also trains the confidence the hypotheses demanded: the seeker who has watched provision arrive through a hundred mornings stops asking whether the schedule will hold, and starts asking what the schedule's Keeper wants from the day's seeking — which is the only question the verse's perhaps was designed to produce.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:74
> And ˹watch for˺ the Day He will call to them, “Where are those you claimed were My associate-gods?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question, Repeated**

The Day's question returns word for word: where are those you claimed were My associate-gods? The commentators noted the sūrah now carries the scene twice — verse 62 and verse 74 — and the repetition is deliberate pedagogy: the single most devastating fact of the Hereafter, the non-appearance of the claimed gods, is recited into the community's memory as a refrain, until no listener could ever claim the scenario was unfamiliar. Between the two askings stands the whole anatomy of the reckoning — the misleaders' disavowal, the unanswered calls, the silenced crowd, the open door of repentance, the Lord who creates and chooses, and the sky's nightly mercy — so that the second asking arrives with its evidence stack complete. The question has not changed; the listener has.

**The Refrain's Placement**

The verse's placement between the mercy verses is the second asking's sharpest feature: verse 73 has just displayed the daily mercy of night and day — and the question follows immediately, addressed to those who credited the mercy elsewhere. The commentators' logic: the associates are asked for twice because they were claimed everywhere — in the creed and in the provision's bookkeeping; the same partners were named as gods and as providers of safety and harvest. The Day's audit therefore re-opens the ledger at the provision section: the Quraysh who thanked their idols for the sanctuary's security and the Qurashi rains are summoned to produce the beneficiaries of their gratitude — and the products, as verse 57 proved, were always from God's presence. The second asking is thus narrower and deeper: where are the gods you thanked?

**Where They Will Not Be**

The answer the question anticipates is the same one it received at the first asking: nowhere. The commentators' catalogue of the candidates — idols, jinn, angels, saints, the mighty dead — shares the single property of unfindability. The Day's interrogations in the Qur'an consistently proceed by location because presence is the one credential worship requires: a partner must be there to receive the call, share the dominion, split the authority. The absence discovered at the roll call is not an accident of scheduling; it is the eternal fact the worshipper declined to check in life. The verse's second telling doubles the warning's lead time: the listener has now heard the question before he will meet it — and hearing it twice before dying is the Qur'an's entire theory of warning.

**The Question's Third Audience**

Twice in the sūrah, the question addresses the deniers of Makkah; the tradition's wider reading extends the list: everyone who has ever deferred to anything alongside God — the tribe whose approval outweighed the command, the wealth whose logic overrode the prayer, the self whose comfort selected the creed. The question's form gives the self-audit its protocol: name your claimed associates — not the ones you confess, the ones you claim; the functional ones, consulted daily. Then attempt, honestly, to locate them: where are they, in the hour of the chest's real need? The audit's result, the Qur'an promises, matches the Day's: nothing locatable. The difference between the audit now and the roll call then is the entire distance the sūrah's open door occupies — repentance re-registers the dependencies while re-registration is still possible.

**Asked Twice, Answered Once**

The verse's repetition leaves its reader a task that the Day will not repeat: the question will be asked of each soul once, in public, with the evidence settled. Before that, the question is asked in the recitation as often as the recitation is opened — and each asking is an appointment with the answer's construction. The commentators' summary is the refrain's whole use: the Questioner who asks on the Day is the One who supplied the question's answer-key in the Book, years in advance, twice in this sūrah alone — because He is the One who does not wrong His servants, and a question whose answer was never published would be an ambush. The roll call is thus the most announced event in the creature's future; the only variable still open is whose name the reader's life has been adding to the roster of the found.

**The Refrain's Form and the Oral Book**

The verse's repetition is also a fact about the Book's medium, and the commentators' oral emphasis explains it: a scripture recited aloud builds its teachings through recurrence — the roll-call question functions in the listener's memory the way a chorus functions in a hymn, returning with accumulated weight each time. The first asking introduced the scenario; the second asking, stripped of any new information, works by pure rehearsal — the listener's own imagination supplies the Day's silence now, because the scene has been installed. The technique is the Qur'an's signature at the level of form: its warnings recur until they are not warnings any longer but furniture of the believing mind — present unbidden, at the market stall, at the graveside, at the moment of temptation. The verse's double presence in one chapter is thus a small lesson in the Book's whole method: what is heard once may be forgotten, but what is heard as refrain becomes the voice the conscience borrows — and the refrain's question, asked twice here, will be asked once there, when it can no longer be rehearsed away. Between the second asking in the Book and the first asking in the court lies the reader's whole remaining span of mornings — and the verse's placement at the edge of Korah's story suggests how quickly that span can convert from warning season to sentencing season, without a change in the weather.

---

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:75
> And We will bring forth a witness from every faith-community and ask ˹the polytheists˺, “Show ˹Us˺ your proof.” Then they will ˹come to˺ know that the truth is with Allah ˹alone˺. And whatever ˹gods˺ they fabricated will fail them

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Witness From Every Community**

The verse opens the Day's testimony phase: a witness drawn from every community — and the commentators identified the witnesses: the prophets, on the authority of the Qur'an's own parallel — how will it be when We bring a witness from every community and bring you as a witness over these? — with the wider reading extending the roster to every community's faithful who kept the trust across generations without a living prophet among them. The design is exact: no people is tried without representation, and no people can claim its case was heard in absentia. The scene's fairness is its terror: the witness arrives who knows the community's whole record — the message delivered, the responses given, the excuses drafted — and his testimony is not hostile but simply complete. Against complete testimony, the denier's file has nowhere to hide.

**"Bring Your Proof"**

The command is issued to the rejecting communities: bring your proof — and the commentators glossed its target: any evidence that their rejection of the messengers was justified. The burden's placement follows the Qur'an's whole jurisprudence: the denial was always a claim — that the messengers lied, that the signs were sorcery, that the scripture was borrowed — and claims owe proofs. On the Day, the claimants are asked to produce what their position always owed: one artifact, one witness, one reading of the evidence that survived contact with the Truth. The scene's silence thereafter is the verdict: no community's exhibit list survives the venue change, because the proofs' natural habitat — rumor, sophistry, consensus — was this world, and nothing fabricated clears customs into the Hereafter.

**They Will Know the Truth Is Allah's**

The verse records the discovery rather than the sentence: then they will know that the truth belongs to God — knowledge arriving as the case closes. The commentators noted the tense's precision: they will know — the verb of the unveiled, the same knowledge-by-necessity verse 62 described, arriving after the window in which knowing could have been chosen. The scene thus completes the Qur'an's standing pattern: faith's value lies in its voluntariness before evidence becomes unavoidable; the Day supplies certainty to everyone and credit to no one. The phrase's content matters equally: the truth is God's — He held it throughout the dispute; the communities' versions were borrowings against it, and the borrowing is now called due. Every argument the deniers ever filed was, in the end, an attempt to relocate the truth's ownership — and the verse announces the repossession.

**Their Fabrications Forsake Them**

The verse's closing clause ends the partnership: what they used to fabricate will fail them — and the commentators listed the fabricated: idols invented, lies told, the whole constructed religion of convenience. The word fails them — the idiom of an ally deserting the field — completes the sūrah's longest arc: the associates who could not be found (62), could not answer (64), and now formally defect. The abandonment is the fabrication's own nature coming due: things that were made hold only as long as their makers hold, and the maker's death dissolves the make. The scene's teaching for the living is inventory doctrine: audit what you lean on, and ask what it is made of — everything fabricated holds in this world's weather and fails in the next world's. Only what was never fabricated — the Truth, which is God's — survives the transfer of venue.

**The Court's Completeness as Mercy**

The verse's procedure, read whole, is the Day's mercy in procedural form: witnesses so no one is tried unheard; burden of proof so no one is condemned by assertion; unveiled truth so no one is sentenced in confusion; abandoned fabrications so no one's account is inflated by allies who never existed. The commentators' wider point stands behind the jurisprudence: God's court is the perfect inverse of every human court that ever condemned a prophet — those courts convicted without proof, silenced the witnesses, and released the fabrication into law. The verse shows the appellate hearing every such verdict receives. For the believer, the scene is assurance filed in advance: the same procedure that closes on the false will vindicate the true — every faithful witness, every rejected messenger, every mocked record — and the verse's placement in the sūrah, between the refrain-question and Korah's treasury, announces the court's jurisdiction over both creeds and ledgers.

**The Believer's Community in the Same Verse**

The verse's procedure names every community — and the believers should not read it from outside. The commentators' wider reading includes in the witnesses all who kept the trust across the generations, which means the verse's court will also hear the believing communities' record: what was done with the clear Book, the preserved prayer, the unbroken fast, the accumulated knowledge. The scene's fairness cuts in both directions — the falsifiers fail, and the faithful's fidelity is finally legible — but the direction that reforms is the second: the verse is an audit notice for the successful too. Every community that inherits a religion inherits the verse's question with it: your proof — not your inheritance certificate. The commentators' counsel follows: credentials by descent are the fabricated gods' cousin, and they will fail the same field test; what stands in that court is what was actually practiced, taught, and handed over alive. The verse thus keeps the believer's confidence honest — safe nowhere in the Book but in the record.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:76
> Indeed, Korah was from the people of Moses, but he behaved arrogantly towards them. We had granted him such treasures that even their keys would burden a group of strong men. ˹Some of˺ his people advised him, “Do not be prideful! Surely Allah does not like the prideful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wealthiest Man in the Sūrah's World**

The chapter introduces its second superpower: Korah — Qārūn — of the people of Moses, whose treasures required a relay of strong men just to carry the keys. The commentators placed him in the Bible's record: Korah of Numbers 16, the prominent Israelite who rebelled against Moses — but the Qur'an's portrait adds the dimension the story needs: he was one of the oppressed nation's own sons, raised from slavery's ranks into wealth that embarrassed empires. The introduction's purpose is stated by the commentators directly: his case exemplifies verse 60's appraisal — that whatever is given is the world's enjoyment and adornment — because Korah is what maximum acquisition looks like when the appraisal is refused. Pharaoh held power; Korah held gold; the sūrah files both under the same verdict by the chapter's end.

**"He Behaved Arrogantly Toward His People"**

The crime is stated with its direction: he transgressed against his people — baghā, the overstepping that aims itself at others. The commentators spread the reading across its layers: contempt toward his own people born of wealth; refusal of the charity their right demanded; flaunting his fortune; some adding jealousy of Moses and Aaron's station. The detail that sharpens the charge is kinship: this is not a foreign tyrant crushing strangers — it is the community's own son towering over it, the boy from the oppressed households whose elevation taught him to look down at the households he came from. The Qur'an's social doctrine runs through the charge: wealth's first temptation is vertical — to convert fortune into distance from one's own — and Korah's transgression is that conversion complete, held up as the cautionary exhibit.

**The Keys as the Measure**

The verse measures the wealth by its handles: so heavy were the keys that a company of strong men labored under them. The commentators gave the readings their due: literal keys to multiple treasuries; or keys so numerous they defied accounting; or — the widest — keys of knowledge, mastery so manifold that a crowd of specialists could not match him, the same key-imagery the Qur'an uses for God's own keys of the unseen. The image's function is fixed across the readings: wealth described by the weight of its access is wealth that has outgrown its owner — the man's estate no longer fits through his own arms. The commentators' note on the knowledge-reading adds the cautionary second layer: even if the keys were capabilities, the verse's verdict holds, because capability hoarded as superiority is the same arrogance in a subtler vault.

**The Counsel of His Own People**

Into the display steps the verse's conscience: his people counseled him — exult not, for God loves not the exultant. The commentators' reading of the counsel is exact: it warned against wantonness and miserliness — against denying wealth its proper circulation, the rights of the poor folded into the fortune — and against celebrating the world as if the Hereafter were rumor. The scene's sociology deserves note: the counsel comes from Korah's own community — the people his arrogance targeted are the ones advising him — and the counsel's tone is fraternal, not revolutionary. The Qur'an's portrait of the believers here is the standing model of correction: they did not curse him, envy him, or rebel against him; they advised him, with the theology attached — God loves not the exultant. The counsel's failure will be his sentence's first exhibit: advised, and unheeding.

**God Loves Not the Exultant**

The counsel's closing clause is the verse's doctrine: God does not love the exultant — the ones who rejoice in what they hold as if holding were deserving. The commentators tied the clause to the chapter's running theme: exulting in the land without right (28:39), the spoiled towns exulting in their ease (28:58) — exultation is the signature sin of every prosperous refusal in the Book. The doctrine's precision matters: the crime is not wealth but the self-worship wealth induces — the conversion of gift into merit, possession into identity. The verse's placement at Korah's introduction hands the reader the verdict before the biography finishes: the man is being introduced by the clause that will bury him, and every line between — the council's advice, the show of his adornment, the earth's command — is the working out of a sentence the reader has already read.

**A Fortune Inside the Oppressed Nation**

Korah's placement inside Moses' community makes his case more instructive than a foreign tyrant's: the commentators emphasized that the oppressed nation, fresh from generations of slavery, produced — within a single generation of elevation — a magnate whose keys humbled strongmen. The lesson is about the speed of wealth's chemistry: oppression did not inoculate the Israelites against arrogance; prosperity began re-sorting them immediately, and the community's own son became the exhibit. The Qur'an's social realism here is bracing: victimhood confers no permanent virtue, and deliverance's first tests are often internal — the freed who rise and forget the pit. The counsel scene shows the community's immune system working as designed: the people advised their own magnate rather than envying or revolting — the correct first response to a straying wealthy man. That the counsel failed was Korah's doing, not the system's; and the sūrah preserves the advising scene to teach every future community its first move: before the treasury falls, open with the truth, in kindness, to one of your own.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:77
> Rather, seek the ˹reward˺ of the Hereafter by means of what Allah has granted you, without forgetting your share of this world. And be good ˹to others˺ as Allah has been good to you. Do not seek to spread corruption in the land, for Allah certainly does not like the corruptors.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Complete Economics of a Believer**

The counsel to Korah is, in form, the Qur'an's complete wealth doctrine delivered in three clauses: seek the Hereafter by means of what God has given you; do not forget your portion of the world; do good as God has done good to you. The commentators read the address's generosity: the advice, though aimed at one man, is written as standing counsel for every holder of anything. The first clause establishes wealth's direction: it is a means, and the destination is named — the Abode of the Hereafter. The clause quietly abolishes both poles of the age's economics: wealth as the destination (Korah's error) and wealth as contamination (the ascetic's error) — the believer's fortune is a vehicle, licensed, fueled, and assigned a road.

**"And Do Not Forget Your Portion of the World"**

The middle clause is the verse's most quoted balance: do not forget your portion of the world. The commentators gave the readings: do not waste your life neglecting good works and preparation; and do not fall into extremes of self-denial — enjoy what God has made permissible. The tradition sealed the balance with the saying the commentators transmitted from Ibn ʿUmar — work for your world as though you would live forever, and work for the Hereafter as though you were to die tomorrow. The clause's placement in counsel to a rich man matters: the Qur'an does not counsel Korah to abandon the fortune but to hold it correctly — his portion is real, designed, and lawful; what was missing was the portion's subordination. Some in the tradition even read the portion as the grave itself — the one acquisition no wealth escapes — which turns the clause's counsel from balance to urgency: take your share, but know which share is taken last.

**"As God Has Been Good to You"**

The third clause sets charity's measure: do good as God has been good to you — and the commentators noted the root's breadth: excellence, beauty, virtue in one word. The standard is neither tithe-arithmetic nor impulse: it is calibrated gratitude — the doer asks how thoroughly, how graciously, how unasked God's goodness arrived, and matches the mirror. The clause's theology underwrites its economics: since the wealth was His before it was yours, kindness through you is the wealth resuming its Source's character. The counsel's placement is pointed: Korah's failing was precisely the refusal of this mirroring — received mercy without transmitted mercy — and the verse's audience hears the clause as their own standing commission: every provision is issued with a match to be played.

**Corruption Forbidden by Name**

The counsel closes with the sūrah's oldest prohibition: do not seek corruption in the land — God loves not the corruptors. The phrase ties Korah's counsel back to verse 4, where the same word pinned Pharaoh: the regime that murdered sons and the magnate who withheld from them are one category in the Book's law. The commentators read Korah's potential corruption concretely: wealth that refuses its rights starves the poor, bends the market, purchases judges — corruption is what hoarded fortune does to a society's plumbing. The prohibition's scope also dignifies the counsel's positive clauses: the believer's spending is not merely charity but anti-corruption — circulation as the wealth's righteousness, hoarding as its crime scene. The sūrah's two large men, Pharaoh and Korah, are thus charged under the same statute, and the counsel shows the statute's alternative: the wealth that moves is the wealth exonerated.

**Advice Refused as the Character's Verdict**

The verse's form — advice, addressed and refused — is its narrative function: Korah's damnation begins not at the earthquake but at the counsel, because the counsel is where obedience was still possible. The commentators' summary carries the verse's use: every rich reader of this verse is standing in Korah's courtyard, with the same advice being offered in the same three clauses, and the biography's remaining verses exist to show what the refusal costs — so that the refusing, this time, is done with eyes open. The verse's balance sheet survives every economy: means with a destination, portion without forgetfulness, goodness on the model of the Giver, corruption under prohibition. It is, clause for clause, the charter of wealth in Islam — issued, fittingly, to the richest man in the chapter.

**The Counsel as the Qur'an's Economic Charter**

Gather the verse's clauses and read them as a charter: wealth is licensed as a means to a named destination; a portion of the world is lawful and assigned; goodness is owed on the scale of received goodness; corruption is prohibited in the land. The commentators' tradition carried the charter into practice across the centuries — the jurists' doctrines of lawful earning, the obligatory share, and the prohibited usury are this verse's offspring at legal scale. The charter's genius is its refusal of both poles: it forbids the world-renouncing error and the world-worshiping error in the same breath, because both misunderstand what property is — a trust with a term. The verse's audience hears the charter read to the age's richest man before his fall, which dates the document: it was current at the maximum of temptation, not composed after it. Every economy since — including the reader's own household budget — can be graded against its four clauses, and the grading question is the charter's own: where does this money point, what portion does it keep, whom does it mirror, and what does it corrupt?

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:78
> He replied, “I have been granted all this because of some knowledge I have.” Did he not know that Allah had already destroyed some from the generations before him who were far superior to him in power and greater in accumulating ˹wealth˺? There will be no need for the wicked to be asked about their sins

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Self-Made Man's Creed**

Korah's reply is the creed of the self-made in one clause: this came to me on account of a knowledge I possess. The commentators parsed the claim's layers: some took the knowledge as commercial skill — the crafts and competencies of a fortune; some transmitted the reports that he was an alchemist, reports the tradition itself dismissed; others read the phrase as opinion — in my view, I earned this. Every reading converges on the doctrine beneath: the attribution of provision to the self. The claim is the mirror-image of verse 57's error: the Quraysh knew not that God established them; Korah actively asserted the opposite credit. Between passive ignorance and active self-crediting, the Qur'an files both under the same verse — most of them know not — because the knowledge owed is the same: that what the hands gather was handed.

**The Reply to the Counsel**

The answer's placement — immediately after his people's counsel — measures the man: offered the theology of gratitude, he returns the ideology of merit. The commentators noted what the reply forecloses: the counsel's three clauses — direction, balance, goodness — all presuppose a Giver, and Korah's premise deletes the Giver from the ledger before the advice can operate. His reply is thus not merely rude but structurally complete: a whole alternative theology in one sentence, with the self as source, the wealth as wage, and the poor as uncontracted. The Qur'an's portrait of the rich refuser is therefore not a portrait of temper but of system: the sentence he speaks in verse 78 is the operating system that runs everything the following verses display.

**The Question That Answers Him**

The verse answers the creed with a question: did he not know that God destroyed generations before him, stronger in might and greater in gathering? The commentators listed the parallel challenges — have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them, more established in the land, with rivers flowing at their feet? (6:6) — the standing argument from history's actuarial tables. The question's logic is exact: if wealth followed from knowledge and power, the greater accumulators would still be standing; the ruins of the greater are the falsification of the merit-creed — and the falsification was standing in plain view of any man with Korah's education. The question's opening — did he not know — indicts by prior access: the information that refutes his creed was not hidden; it was unexamined, because the creed was doing too much work for its owner to audit it.

**No Interrogation Needed**

The verse closes on the sentence's procedure: the guilty will not be questioned about their sins. The commentators harmonized this with the verses that promise questioning — different kinds of questions: information-seeking, account-calling, rebuke — and the reading lands here as: no inquiry will be needed. The reason is in the clause: their sins — the deeds are already known, itemized by the Knower of what breasts conceal (28:69); the Day's questions exist for the record's completion, not for the Judge's information. The commentators added the sharper sense: the guilty will have no avenue to make amends — questioning implies a hearing that could yet go somewhere, and Korah's file is closed. The clause thus completes the refutation of his creed at both ends: he claimed his fortune was earned knowledge; the verse shows that the only knowledge that would have saved him was the knowledge he refused.

**The Creed's Standing Refutation**

The verse's use outruns its scene: the self-attribution of success is the permanent religion of the prospering, in every economy and every age, and the verse is its standing refutation. The commentators' wider reading supplies the believer's own maintenance: the creed's mild form lives in the religious too — the practiced skill credited as self-made, the answered prayer remembered as competence. The Sufi counsel transmitted in the tradition states the cure: one who glories in his own attainments should be counted wretched — because the glory is the theft. The verse's question remains the diagnostic: did you not know the greater who fell? — and the believer who keeps the ruins in view holds the creed's antidote in permanent supply: everything gathered was granted, the gathering included.

**Knowledge Stolen From Its Rightful Claim**

Korah's specific word — knowledge — deserves the last audit, because he stole the right word. The tradition's counsel-scene shows what knowledge in Israel actually claimed: gratitude, proportion, the Hereafter's precedence — and Korah cites the same vocabulary's root to certify hoarding. The commentators' note on his possible crafts sharpens the point: even where the knowledge was real — skill, alchemy, accounting — the knowledge was never his in the absolute sense his sentence claims: its faculties were lent, its opportunities arranged, its market timed by a Hand he never mentions. The verse's refutation by history completes the theft's exposure: the mightier and greater gathered before him, and their gathering did not save them — so whatever knowledge he claims was already falsified by the record he refused to read. The believer's takeaway is a discipline of attribution: every skill on the résumé gets audited annually against verse 76's question — who taught you this, who opened that door, whose sun ripened your harvest — and the audit's honest output is the creed Korah refused: mine is included in granted.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:79
> Then he came out before his people in all his glamour. Those who desired the life of this world wished, “If only we could have something like what Korah has been given. He is truly a man of great fortune!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parade**

The verse stages the spectacle: he came out to his people in his adornment — the commentators preserving the tradition's descriptions of the display: the loaded processions, the mule, the retinue, the wardrobe that made wealth visible at a distance. The parade's function in the story is rhetorical: Korah answers the counsel of verse 77 not with argument but with exhibition — theology is met with a pageant. The commentators noted the parade's audience-design: he came out to his people — the same people whose counsel he had declined — the display aimed at the very constituency that advised him, as if to say: you counseled the owner; behold the owned. The Qur'an's verb is spare — he came out — and the sparseness is the indictment: the man's reply to heaven's economics is a costume.

**The Audience That Wished**

The verse splits the crowd at the first showing: those who desired the home of the world wished they had the like of what Korah was given. The commentators' framing is precise: the wishing cohort is defined by its desire — not the poor as such, but those whose religion was acquisition; the display sorted the square by appetite. The wish itself deserves its anatomy: would that we had the like of what Korah has been given — the commentators noted its resemblance to the deniers' demands for the Prophet: would that he were given the like of what Moses was given — envy as the standard engine of refusal. And the crowd's closing verdict — he is the owner of tremendous fortune — is the parade's intended reception: wealth read as endorsement, the display mistaken for a verdict from heaven.

**Power as a Public Argument**

The scene teaches the political function of display: the parade is an argument in the language of things, and it works on exactly the audience it targets. The commentators' realism holds across ages: no tyrant's court and no magnate's lobby ever relied on reason alone; the procession, the palace, the launched product are arguments addressed to the eyes — because sight is the belief-channel of the undisciplined. The Qur'an's counter is the next verse's knowing cohort, but this verse's honesty comes first: the display works. A treasury paraded through a public square recruits more believers than a sermon, and the recruits' creed is stated in their wish — fortune belongs to the displayed. The verse's gift to the reader is the parade's secret, told in advance: what converts the crowd is the lexis of things, and things are the world's own adornment (28:60) — rented, and priced to fail.

**The Wish as a Mirror**

The wishing crowd functions in the story as Korah's mirror: his creed — this is mine by my knowledge — and their creed — this is what winning looks like — are the same religion viewed from the altar and the pews. The commentators' wider reading extends the mirror to the reader: every generation has its parades and its wishing cohorts, and the verse's diagnostic is the wish itself. What do you wish for when the adornment passes? The question's honesty matters: the Qur'an does not deny the wish's pull — the display was engineered to be enviable — it denies the wish's verdict: the fortune was not fortune but forwarding, and the forwarding address is coming in the next verses. The crowd that wished is not condemned for seeing the shine; it is condemned for concluding from it.

**The Set-Up for the Fall**

The verse's placement is the fall's staging: display at maximum — the wish confirmed, the counsel silenced, the square converted — and the demolition arrives one verse later, in front of the same audience. The commentators heard the design: the greater the spectacle, the clearer the lesson when the ground opens; God's instructional method with Korah requires the parade to succeed completely before the earth is commanded. The scene thus teaches the reader the value of watching empires at their maximum: the height is not the safety but the staging. Every fortune that parades is, in the Qur'an's geometry, one sentence from its ground-command — and the crowd that has learned this verse holds its wishes for the next showing.

**Adornment as the Display's Whole Truth**

The verse's chosen word for what Korah wore is adornment — the same word verse 60 used to price the world — and the choice is the display's sentence before the earthquake: he came out in his decoration. The commentators' eye catches what the word concedes and denies at once: the display was genuinely beautiful — the silks shone, the procession gleamed — and it was genuinely surface, the world's own category, priced as enjoyment and ornament. The parade thus demonstrates verse 60 in motion: maximum adornment, zero duration. The verse's diagnostic for the reader is wearable too: every presentation a life makes — the clothes, the credentials, the curated feed — falls under the same file label. Decoration is lawful, even lovely; the error is the crowd's: reading the decoration as a verdict about the man. When the ground opened, the verse 60 appraisal was shown to be the only accurate caption the parade ever had — and the knowing cohort, standing in the same square, had read the caption correctly at first showing. The verse's reader inherits their skill: see the shine, name it honestly as the world's own category, and let the naming hold until the display's end arrives on schedule — which, the chapter promises, it always does.

---

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:80
> But those gifted with knowledge said, “Shame on you! Allah’s reward is far better for those who believe and do good. But none will attain this except the steadfast.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Cohort Speaks**

The square contained a second audience, and the verse gives it the verdict: those given knowledge said — woe to you. The commentators identified the speakers: the people of piety and wisdom in Korah's community, some transmitters naming the scholars of Israel — with the honest note that the anachronism of later titles makes the general reading the sounder one. What matters is the contrast the verse constructs: same parade, same square, and the crowd divides again — the desire-cohort wishes, the knowledge-cohort rules. The knowing reply begins with rebuke — the commentators render it as woe to you or shame — because the wish of the previous verse was not innocent longing but doctrinal error: the crowd had just certified a creed, and the creed's licensed refuters answered.

**The Refutation's Content**

The knowing men's argument is complete in two clauses: God's reward is better for those who believe and work righteousness — and none attains it except the patient. The first clause re-prices the exhibit: the parade's fortune is real but inferior — the Garden's wage outranks it on the declared scale of the One who pays both. The second clause states the access rule: the better reward is gated by patience — and the commentators tied the clause to its echo elsewhere: only those who endure patiently will be given their reward without limit. The pairing dismantles the wish-cohort's premise at the root: what Korah displayed was not the reward of virtue but the props of an unpatient life — and the goods the crowd envied are the goods patience declines to worship.

**Knowledge Versus Desire**

The verse's sociology is its lasting gift: two audiences watched one procession and reached opposite verdicts, and the verse names the differentiator — knowledge. The commentators' larger doctrine stands behind the scene: the difference between the cohorts was not information — both saw the same gold — but formation: the knowing held the scale that prices the seen against the unseen, and the desiring held nothing but appetite. The scene therefore teaches where the believer's resources must go: not into competing with parades but into acquiring the scale — the knowledge by which display is correctly read. Every society's squares will be filled again with procession and wishing; the Qur'an's project, verse by verse, is to build the minority that says woe at the right moment, with the argument attached.

**The Patience Clause**

The reply's last clause deserves its own weight: none attains it except the patient — and the commentators heard in it the patient's full job description: steadfast in obedience, in restraint from forbidden gain, and in the long wait between sowing and harvest. The clause quietly explains Korah's failure: his fortune was the impatient man's shortcut — acquisition without waiting, display without accountability — and his tragedy was scheduled by the very impatience that built him. The clause also dignifies the believers' poverty in the square: the knowers' position looks like loss to the wishing crowd — no mule, no retinue — but the verse prices their position correctly: they hold the one asset that gates the unlimited reward. Patience, in this economy, is not passive endurance; it is the active discipline of declining the parade's terms while the parade is winning.

**The Reply Between the Counsel and the Earth**

The verse's placement completes the sequence the sūrah has been assembling: counsel (77), creed refused (78), parade (79), and now the public refutation (80) — the debate conducted in the square, in front of the exhibit, on the record. The commentators' narrative instinct sees the design: the knowing men's rebuke is Korah's last unmuzzled hearing — the wisdom he silenced in private now answered him in public — and the earth's command, one verse away, will ratify the rebuke in front of the same audience. The verse thus leaves the reader standing in the square with the knowers: the parade is passing, the wish-cohort is murmuring, and the question the scene presses on every generation is cohort assignment — not which procession to join, but which cohort to stand in when the next one passes: the wishing, or the knowing, whose rebuke time always vindicates.

**"Woe to You": Rebuke as the Knowing Men's Duty**

The knowing men's answer opens with a word the comfortable skip: woe to you — a public rebuke, addressed to the wishing crowd, in the parade's own venue. The commentators' care with the scene shows the rebuke's license and its limits: the knowing spoke because the error was doctrinal and the speakers were qualified — this is correction, not abuse; and the rebuke's content, once delivered, is the verse's real payload: God's reward is better, and patience is its gate. The scene thus teaches the community's second immune response: after private counsel to the straying magnate (77) fails, the qualified must correct the audience his display recruits — because the parade's real damage is the wishes it manufactures, and wishes are corrected in public or not at all. The tradition's balance applies: the rebuke names the error and the better wage, without cursing the wishers — they are the rebuke's intended beneficiaries, not its targets. Every generation's knowing cohort inherits the same duty at the next parade: say the woe, state the scale, name the gate — and let the ground do the rest. The division of labor between the tongue and the earthquake is the scene's lasting structure: the knowing speak while speaking can still convert, and the earth commands only when the speaking season has closed — which is why the knowing men's few words in the square outrank, in the Qur'an's accounting, the loudest display the age's fortune ever staged.

---

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:81
> Then We caused the earth to swallow him up, along with his home. There was no one to help him against Allah, nor could he even help himself

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earth Answers**

The verse carries out the sentence the counsel verses delayed: We caused the earth to swallow him and his home. The instrument is the point — no invading army, no rival fortune, no plague; the ground itself, the most stable thing a treasure-owner knows, receives the command. The commentators related the reports that Moses was granted the power to call upon the earth for this — the prophet's staff, his hand, and now the ground beneath the fortune all conscripted as evidence. The detail about the house matters as much as the man: the earth took the mansion too, the decorated storage of the wealth, so that the square's last sight of Korah's world was its total disappearance — owner and archive in one swallow. The display of verse 79 had been built to say this cannot end; the earth's command said otherwise in one motion, and the verse reports the motion without embellishment, because the fact needed none.

**No Party, No Self**

The closing clause prices the fortune's protection at zero twice over: he had no party to help him against God, and he could not help himself. The word for party, the commentators noted, is the Arabic of a battle-support company — the allies a man musters in war. Korah, who could fund any company, discovered the one battlefield where money conscripts nothing: against God's command, no retinue marches. The second failure is starker: the man of verse 78's self-credit — all this by my knowledge — could not devise one application of that knowledge to save his own body. The verse's arithmetic is the chapter's verdict on the self-made creed: the knowledge that could not gather what God withheld could not retain what God recalled. Wealth's two promised protections — patronage and competence — are shown failing in the same breath, on the record, before the same audience the parade had recruited.

**The Speed of the Reversal**

The verse's placement measures the interval: one verse ago the crowd was wishing, and now the earth has closed over the wished-for object. The commentators' reading of the sequence draws the doctrine of suddenness: God's delays — decades of counsel offered, signs ignored — end in moments when the term completes. Korah's fall did not escalate; it detonated. The speed is itself instruction: the same man who converted a morning's display into a reputation for permanent fortune learned that the permanent thing was a lease with a one-verse term. The Qur'an's audiences — the first one in Makkah, with their own magnates parading through the fairs — were being taught the actuarial truth their instincts resist: the distance between the parade and the pit is exactly the distance between one recited verse and the next, and no wealth has ever purchased an extension of the interval.

**The Home That Went Down With Him**

The inclusion of the house repays its own reading, because a fortune is not only coin — it is the architecture of a life: the halls that hosted, the storerooms that secured, the threshold that defined arrival. The earth took the building with the builder, and the commentators' tradition read in the pairing the totality of the loss: nothing of Korah's world survived above ground, not even the address. The lesson is addressed to every owner who has mistaken his holdings for his geography: the deed's real issuer holds the land, and the land executes. Verse 58's ruined dwellings housed strangers; Korah's dwelling was denied even that afterlife — swallowed whole, with its contents and its lord — so that no traveler's shelter, no borrowed wall, would ever carry his name. The display that filled a square emptied a plot, and the plot's silence became the sūrah's loudest verse on property.

**Where the Counsel's Words Were Standing**

One reading of the scene turns the verse back toward the readers: when the ground moved, the counsel of verses 76–77 was still standing in the square's memory — do not exult, seek the Hereafter by what God gave you, do good as He did — and the swallowing was the counsel's final authentication. The commentators' larger point completes it: every warning refused in the Book is eventually ratified by the event it predicted, and the ratification is public. Korah's people had delivered the exact words the situation required; their vindication is the story's consolation and its warning braided together — the community that advises rightly may still watch the refusal, but it does not share the sentence, and the advice survives the adviser's hearer. The verse teaches the advisors of every age to keep counseling precisely because the counsel's words will be standing, in the record, when the ground gives its answer.

**The Counselors of Verse 76, Vindicated**

The swallowing scene completes the biography of the advising community, and the completion is honorable: the people who counseled Korah in private did not initiate the earth's command, claim credit for it, or inherit his ruins — they are simply found correct. The commentators' sociological note deserves the emphasis: a community's first duty toward its own wealthy strayer is the honest word, and the community that delivers it stands clear of the fall whatever the outcome. The scene thereby answers a question every congregation eventually faces: what is advising for, if the advisee refuses? The counsel's value was never contingent on acceptance — it located the community on the record, trained it in the doctrine aloud, and left the strayer without the last excuse of the uncounseled. Verse 78's sentence — the guilty will not be questioned about their sins — reads differently after this: the questioning's absence does not spare the record; it reflects that the record, counsel included, was already complete.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:82
> And those who had craved his position the previous day began to say, “Ah! It is certainly Allah Who gives abundant or limited provisions to whoever He wills of His servants. Had it not been for the grace of Allah, He could have surely caused the earth to swallow us up! Oh, indeed! The disbelievers will never succeed.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Overnight Conversion of the Wishing Crowd**

The verse returns to the parade's audience, and the scene's irony is complete: those who wished for his position yesterday — the desire-cohort of verse 79 — are standing at the crater today, reciting theology. The commentators noted the shift's completeness: the same crowd that read the display as fortune now reads the disappearance as doctrine — it is certainly Allah who outspreads and straitens provision for whom He wills of His servants. The conversion's speed is the lesson, not a comedy: the crowd was never far from the truth; it was near-sighted, not blind. One event re-calibrated the whole faculty. The Qur'an's realism about audiences includes this encouraging fact — the parade's recruits are recoverable, sometimes overnight, because what recruited them was evidence, and evidence moves.

**The Creed They Recite**

The confession they produce is the Qur'an's own doctrine of provision: God outspreads and straitens for whom He wills — the formula repeated across the Book, in the thirteenth chapter: Allah gives abundant or limited provisions to whoever He wills (13:26), and the seventeenth: Surely your Lord gives abundant or limited provisions to whoever He wills (17:30). The commentators gave the particles their due: the alas at the sentence's head is read as emphasis or as a cry against the audience's own yesterday — know that God outspreads — so the recitation is both creed and self-reproach. The doctrine's content repairs exactly the error the parade had planted: provision is not earned by the displayed, nor denied to the hidden; it is administered, by will, across a population the watcher cannot audit. The crowd learned in one morning what Korah refused across a fortune's lifetime.

**"Had It Not Been for God's Grace"**

The confession's middle clause is the sentence's humility: had it not been for the grace of God, He could have caused the earth to swallow us. The crowd does not distance itself from the fallen man; it locates itself one ungiven grace away from his crater. The commentators' tradition reads the clause as the knowledge that matters most in a reversal: the difference between the envied and the envier is not merit but assignment — and the assignment can be revised in either direction by the Assigner.
