The clause also completes verse 82's teaching on gratitude: the crowd's security had been real all along, and unacknowledged — now they acknowledge it retroactively, as the thing that was holding the ground still beneath them. Every spectator at every crater is being taught the same arithmetic: what you are standing on is grace, and grace is load-bearing.

**"The Disbelievers Will Never Succeed"**

The crowd's last sentence converts the event into a standing law: the disbelievers will not succeed — the commentators noting the word's arrival at the end of thewish: the sentiment they once attached to Korah's ledger is now attached to his creed. The sentence's placement teaches how lessons generalize: the crowd does not conclude that Korah failed; it concludes that his category fails — disbelief as a life-plan is falsified not by argument but by exhibit. The Qur'an's method throughout the sūrah has been exactly this: one displayed case, then the transferable verdict. The crowd's sentence is the sūrah's whole argument spoken by its least likely witnesses — the square's materialists, converted by the ground's demonstration into theologists, handing the next passerby the conclusion it took them a parade and a crater to earn.

**The Two Mornings**

The verse's architecture — yesterday's wish, this morning's confession — is the sūrah's doctrine of time in miniature. The commentators' reading of the phrase morning found them catches the speed: a single rotation of the earth separated the crowds' two religions. The teaching cuts both ways: no reader should trust today's appetite to be tomorrow's judgment — the wishing self and the confessing self are the same person at different hours — and no reader should despair of anyone's conversion while mornings keep arriving. The crowd's journey, from envy to creed in one dawn, is the standing evidence that hearts are re-printable; the sūrah's darker cases — Pharaoh, Korah — are not evidence that hearts are fixed, only that the printing press closes without notice. The verse's gift is the interval: between the wish and the confession lies one night, and the reader is always somewhere inside such an interval, holding whichever self the last parade produced.

**The Confession's Teacher Was the Event**

The crowd's new creed did not arrive from a lecture — it was read off the crater, and the verse thus displays the Qur'an's standing claim that creation's events are themselves a curriculum. The commentators' broader doctrine holds that the alternation of God's giving — outspreading here, straitening there — is a displayed text for whoever reads outcomes; Korah's morning is that text at full volume. The teaching's accessibility is its distinction: no literacy, no scholars, no scripture required — a square full of onlookers and one event sufficed. The lesson generalizes to every reversal a reader will ever witness: each is a printed page in the same hand, and the believer trained by this verse reads reversals theologically rather than gossipily — asking what the event says about the Administrator, not what it says about the fallen. The crowd's overnight scholarship is therefore available to everyone at every calamity; the tuition is attention, and the textbook is the news.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:83
> That ˹eternal˺ Home in the Hereafter We reserve ˹only˺ for those who seek neither tyranny nor corruption on the earth. The ultimate outcome belongs ˹only˺ to the righteous

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Home After the Crater**

The verse lifts the reader's eyes from Korah's crater to the Home that matters: the Abode of the Hereafter, assigned by We — the same pronoun that administered the swallow now administers the estate. The commentators identified the Home as the Garden, and the placement is the verse's rhetoric: shown at maximum what the earth's holdings are worth, the listener is immediately shown the holding that does not swallow. The contrast's geometry is exact — Korah sought the earth's interior for storage; the Home's holders get an abode no ground opens under; the man who would not give from his treasury lost even his house, and the verse's next estate is described as given, ordained, reserved. The Abode's arrival at this exact position in the chapter is the Qur'an's habit: every demolition is followed, within a breath, by the address of the real estate that survives.

**Neither Tyranny Nor Corruption**

The Home's gatekeepers are named as two refusals: those who desire neither dominance upon the earth nor corruption. The word for dominance, the commentators explained, carries exaltedness, ascendancy, grandeur — the lust for height, the Pharaoh-suite of ambitions; and corruption — literally rot — is its exhaust: the wreckage tall-seekers leave behind. The pairing covers the sūrah's whole cast of the lost: Pharaoh desired dominance and produced corruption; Korah, whose desire was the treasury, produced both — contempt above, deprivation below. The commentators' sharper reading completes the gate's design: the Garden is attained by no longer dominating or corrupting — the refusals are not entry fees paid once but desires unlearned, a whole inner economy re-ordered so that height and hoard lose their sovereignty. The verse thus describes the Hereafter's population by their appetites, not their resumes: heaven is zoned for the ungrasping.

**The Outcome Belongs to the Righteous**

The closing clause states the standing law the sūrah has now demonstrated three times over: the outcome belongs to the reverent — the same verdict Moses pronounced to his people by the sea's shore: the ultimate outcome belongs ˹only˺ to the righteous (7:128), and the same refrain the eleventh chapter closes its stories with. The word outcome carries both senses the Arabic holds: the end of the matter and the abode at the end — whichever reading runs, the ownership is fixed. The clause's proof in this chapter is documentary: Pharaoh took the sea, Korah took the ground, and the mother of Moses — holding nothing but a promise — took the outcome. The law's comfort for the readers of every age is its record: outcomes are not distributed by the evening news; they are held in trust by the reverent, and the trust pays on a schedule the world's markets do not post.

**An Estate Ordained, Not Contested**

The verse's verb deserves its final reading: We ordain — assign, appoint — the Home for its people. The commentators' emphasis lands on the absence of competition: the Abode is not seized by the strongest, as Egypt was, nor inherited by accident, as fortunes are; it is assigned by the Owner to a described population. The description's two refusals function, in this light, as the estate's zoning code: anyone may qualify, and the qualification is internal — a heart that has stopped seeking the height and stopped serving the rot. The verse's audience in Makkah — poor, unranked, unbought — heard the assignment read out as their deed: the mighty of the city could not outbid them for this holding, because the holding is priced in desires, and theirs were already right. Every verse of the sūrah's wealth-critique converges here: property's last transfer puts the Abode in the hands of the ungrasping, on the Owner's signature.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Thesis**

Read the verse as the chapter's summary and nothing is left out: verse 5 promised succession to the oppressed — the verse names the succession's final term, the Abode. Verse 4 filed Pharaoh for tyranny — the verse bars the gate to his suite. Verse 58 showed the towns spoiled by ease — the verse names the spoiling's opposite as the qualification. Verse 60 appraised the world as fleeting — the verse opens the lasting ledger. The commentators' summary of the whole chapter could stand on this one verse: the Qur'an's political economy, its history, and its eschatology are one teaching — that outcomes belong to the reverent is not a piety appended to the stories; it is the mechanism the stories were displaying all along. The verse is the sūrah's thesis stated as deed: the Home exists, it is assigned, and the assignment is running now.

**Neither-Neither: The Gate's Double Negative**

The Home's qualification is stated as two refusals rather than one command, and the grammar is the doctrine: heaven's gate is defined by what its people stopped wanting. The commentators' reading of the pairing gives each refusal its field — dominance is the desire exercised over persons, corruption the desire exercised upon the world — so the two together cover the whole surface of a life's appetite for control. The double negative also explains the gate's universality: anyone can stop wanting; the rich and the poor hold equal equipment for this qualification, and the poor often hold an advantage, since the world has been testing their renunciation all along. The verse thereby quietly honors the sūrah's unsung: the shepherd's decade, the fugitive's flight, the mother's empty hands — lives that never grasped and never corrupted, qualified without ever appearing to compete. The Abode's gate has no queue for the impressive; it has a doorway for the ungrasping, and the verse has just posted its coordinates in the reader's own chest.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:84
> Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded with what is better. And whoever comes with an evil deed, then the evildoers will only be rewarded for what they used to do

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Two Handings-Over**

The Day's transaction is stated as an exchange: whoever comes with a good deed receives better than it; whoever comes with an evil deed is repaid only for what he earned. The verb is the same in both clauses — coming, bringing, the posture of a worker presenting his output — and the symmetry is the doctrine: everyone arrives with something; the difference is the exchange rate applied at the counter. The commentators paired the verse with the multiplication verse of the sixth chapter: Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold (6:160) — the better-than-it being quantified elsewhere, while here it is left vast on purpose. The evil deed's rate is stated with equal care: only for what they used to do — the word only doing the work of justice's ceiling: no compounding, no interest on wrong, repayment capped at the principal. The verse's two rates are the Qur'an's mercy and justice in a single ledger: good is under-paid forever, wrong is never over-charged.

**"Better Than It": The Unquantified Multiplier**

The good deed's return is left undefined here, and the commentators heard the freedom in the wording: better than it — tenfold in one verse, seven hundredfold for the zakāt-seeded grain in another, without limit for the patient in a third — because the return's measure belongs to the Giver's generosity, not the accountant's table. The open multiplier teaches the believer the hope the ledger is designed to produce: no deed arrives too small for the better-than clause, since the clause's floor is the deed itself and its ceiling is unstated. The verse's placement after the Abode verse completes the transaction's address: the better repayment is delivered at the Home just described — which re-prices every unseen kindness a life contains. The sūrah's whole cast illustrates the clause: the daughter's morning advice, the sister's watching, the runner's warning — each a small deed whose better-than is still being paid out in the record the Book itself keeps.

**Capped at What They Did**

The evil deed's clause states justice's discipline in its grammar: those who do evil deeds are repaid only for what they used to do. The commentators noted the two protections packed into the word: the doer is paid for his own acts — no inheriting another's burden, the Qur'an's standing rule that no soul carries the load of another — and the repayment does not exceed the acts — justice without the prosecutor's inflation. The verse thereby forecloses both fears at once: the innocent will not be billed for a relative's file, and the guilty will not be sentenced by rumor. The clause's audience includes every wronged reader who has watched calumny price another's life: the Day's counter runs on earned amounts only, and the counter is incorruptible. The sūrah's murdered sons of Egypt, whose account opened the chapter, fall under the same clause — the only repayment that will ever be issued for them is the one they are owed, and the ledger has kept it itemized for millennia.

**Coming With: The Deed as Luggage**

The verse's image of arriving with a deed teaches the life's real logistics: deeds are portable and nothing else is. The commentators' wider doctrine stands behind the image: wealth, rank, and health check in at the grave's door; the record travels. The verb's grammar also fixes the timing — what is brought is what was done; there is no manufacturing at the counter, which is why the sūrah's last-moment portraits carry their established weights: Pharaoh's deathbed cry arrived at the counter without the deeds it claimed, and the exchange refused it. The teaching's practical form is the believer's daily freight-loading: the day's deeds are being packed for a journey that admits no additional luggage at the terminal — and the verse's two clauses are the manifest of what clears. Nothing clarifies a schedule like knowing what the journey admits; the verse states it in two lines and lets the packing begin.

**The Counter Where the Sūrah's Cast Lines Up**

Read the verse as the sūrah's closing ledger and the whole cast files past the counter: the mother brings a night's obedience and receives the better-than — a prophet returned to her arms and a rank the tradition has never finished honoring; the runner brings a warning and receives the better-than, his name lost to history and his deed secure in revelation; Pharaoh brings his tyranny and is repaid only for what he did; Korah brings his treasury and discovers that currency is not a deed — the counter takes no legal tender. The commentators' summary is the verse's use: the rates are published in advance so that no one arrives surprised — good is multiplied beyond telling, wrong is paid to the coin, and the only strategic question the verse leaves its reader is what to be carrying when the counter opens.

**The Two Rates as Daily Counsel**

The verse's exchange rates were never meant to stay eschatological; the tradition reads them as working counsel for the present tense. The good deed's better-than clause powers the believer's smallest kindnesses: the cup of water, the returned greeting, the concealed offense — each is deposited at a counter that multiplies beyond inspection, which is why the Qur'an discourages tallying one's own goodness and encourages consistency in it. The evil deed's cap powers the believer's honesty about sin: no wrong compounds in the dark beyond its size, and the door of repentance — the verse 67 exception — can clear the principal entirely. The two rates together dissolve the twin despair-engines: perfectionism (the good is already better than it) and fatalism (the wrong is paid, not inherited, and can be surrendered before it is paid at all). A life conducted between these two clauses is a life of steady deposits and prompt clearing — and the verse's placement at the chapter's close makes the counsel final: the counter is real, the rates are published, and the account is open until it is not.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:85
> Most certainly, the One Who has ordained the Quran for you will ˹ultimately˺ bring you back home ˹to Mecca˺. Say, “My Lord knows best who has come with ˹true˺ guidance and who is clearly astray.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise of Return**

The verse addresses a grieving exile with a guarantee: the One who ordained the Qur'an for you will bring you back to the place of return. The commentators transmitted the tradition's setting: the verse, some sources say, was revealed at Juḥfah — the waypoint between Makkah and Madinah — as the Prophet made the hijrah, sorrowing to leave the city of his birth, and the verse informed him that he would return to it. The promise's structure is the sūrah's whole theology compressed into one clause: the same Hand that assigned the Book guarantees the homecoming — revelation and return issued by one Ordainer. The verse has been read on three levels: return to Makkah in victory, return to God at death, return at the Resurrection — and the layers do not compete; they nest, because every homecoming in a believer's biography is the small case of the last one.

**The Place of Return**

The phrase the commentators weighed — the place of return — carries the geography and the theology in one term: Makkah, the city the Prophet was leaving; and the return that outlasts geography, to God Himself. The tradition's reading of the phrase at both scales teaches the believer how to hold his own waypoints: every exile's road home is a rehearsal whose venue keeps changing. The Promise's fulfillment is history's footnote now — Makkah opened without battle, its idols cleared by the hands that had been stoned in its streets — and the fulfillment's manner matched the verse's issuer: the city was returned to him, not captured from it, because the Ordainer who assigned the Book does not deliver His assignments half-done. The verse thus stands in the Qur'an's small shelf of documented guarantees, where a promise made in flight was paid in a decade's time, to the day's precision, in front of the same city gates.

**Say: My Lord Knows Best**

The command attached to the promise is Moses' answer from verse 37, assigned again: say — My Lord knows best who has come with guidance and who is in manifest error. The commentators noted the commission's role: the Prophet, consoled with return, is not handed a verdict against his persecutors — he is handed the referral. The reply's restraint is the mission's discipline to the end: the promise guarantees the road, not the sentencing; the case's judgment stays with the Lord who knows the breasts (28:69). The pairing of promise and referral teaches the shape of all waiting: the believer may hold certainty about God's outcome and silence about men's verdicts in the same breath, because the first is the Sustainer's field and the second is not ours to farm. The Prophet's enemies were not named, cursed, or priced in the verse — only the return was pledged, and the knowing was delegated.

**Ordained: The Book as the Guarantee's Backing**

The promise's guarantor is specified by His work: the One who ordained the Qur'an for you — the verb carrying the verse-assigning of the commands and prohibitions, the Qur'an's delivery across the mission's years. The commentators heard the argument inside the wording: the Being who managed the Book's descent — verse by verse, year by year, through boycott and war and siege, always on time — will have no difficulty managing one man's road home. The logic comforts every reader between promises: the proof of the guarantee's reliability is the standing performance of its issuer in the reader's own recitation. Each completed revelation, each preserved letter across fourteen centuries, is the collateral on the verse's remaining pledge — and the believer who recites the Book is, in effect, auditing the guarantor's books before trusting Him with the road.

**The Traveler's Verse**

The tradition's use of this verse completed its meaning: the commentators noted it is recited as a prayer of protection for those setting out on journeys — the believer taking the road wraps himself in the verse of the first and greatest road-taker, whose exile was answered by return. The use is theology turned into liturgy: every departure repeats the hijrah's structure — home left, direction held, outcome with the Ordainer — and the verse equips the traveler with the Prophet's own certainty. The teaching's wide reach includes every believer inside any displacement: the migrant, the refugee, the man whose city has become unsafe for his faith — the verse names their position and its promise. The Lord who ordained the Book for the orphan leaving Makkah on a camel has never lost a servant on a road He assigned; the verse is the standing receipt of that record, recited over every bag ever packed in hope.

**The Consolation's Timing**

The verse's placement in the Prophet's biography is its own lesson: the promise of return was spoken on the road out, not the road back — at the moment of maximum loss, with the city receding. The commentators' reading of the tradition draws the pattern of divine consolation: God's reassurances characteristically arrive inside the hardship they address, not after it, because faith's work is done in transit. The same pattern fills the sūrah: the mother received her promise at the riverbank with the basket still afloat; the fugitive received Midian before he knew he was walking there; the Prophet received Makkah while Makkah was still expelling him. The teaching for every exile in every cause is exact: the promise is issued at the point of departure, when nothing yet supports it but the Sender's record — and the record, this chapter has documented, is perfect. Whoever is walking away from something loved on God's instruction is walking inside a verse like this one, and the road's gloom is not evidence against the return; it is the return's appointed weather.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:86
> You never expected this Book to be revealed to you, but ˹it came˺ only ˹as˺ a mercy from your Lord. So never side with the disbelievers ˹in their disbelief˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Unpredicted Book**

The verse states the Prophet's own surprise: you never hoped that the Book would be delivered to you — and the commentators read it as the decisive answer to the borrowing charges: a man does not spend forty unlettered years concealing a project he then unveils at forty, nor does an unexpected thing require collaborators. The Prophet's biography confirms the clause: no pursuit of scripture, no training under a master, no announced ambition — the first word came to him, tradition records, like the binding of a press, in a cave, to a man whose reported first response was terror and the words that no poet or priest had prepared: I cannot be a poet. The verse certifies the surprise as doctrine: the Book's arrival was mercy descending on an unprepared recipient — and its unpreparation is its authentication, because the last thing a forger's biography contains is the absence of motive, method, and apprenticeship.

**Mercy as the Book's Category**

The verse assigns the Book its classification: it was a mercy from your Lord — the same noun the mission wore at the mountain (28:46) and the epithet the Qur'an claims for itself across its pages. The commentators' note deserves its weight: the Book is not first a law code, a history, or an anthology — it is mercy, in the act of descending; its commands are mercy's grammar, its prohibitions mercy's fencing, its stories mercy's mirrors. The classification also answers the Book's prosecutors in every age: the document that ruins your schemes is not your enemy; it is the warning that arrived while the ruin was still repairable. The mercy's address — from your Lord — personalizes it: the same Lord who routed a basket, funded a hiding, and answered a shepherd's prayer sent the Book as His signature care, delivered to the man who needed it and through him to everyone who would ever need it.

**The Reason Inside the Mercy**

The verse's structure invites the question: why state the Prophet's surprise at all? The commentators' reading catches the pastoral design: the reminder steadies the recipient — the Book came to you unearned, so its weight upon you is not your burden to justify; and it warns his opponents — the mercy's recipient was chosen, not self-appointed, so the resentment against him is resentment of the Sender's choice. The clause also guards the Prophet's humility at the summit of his honors: the man who would lead the prayer of the Garden's population is told, in his own Book, that he did not commission it. The verse thus trains every bearer of every trust in the same reflex: what arrived without your planning arrived by assignment, and assignment is serviced with gratitude, never owned with pride.

**"Never Support the Disbelievers"**

The closing command forbids the one partnership the mission could never survive: be not a supporter of the disbelievers — and the word is the sūrah's own: ẓahīr, the exact noun of Moses' vow in verse 17 — never a supporter of the guilty. The echo is the commentary: the Prophet stands in Moses' position, the vow retold at the summit of a different mission, because the temptation it guards against is structural. The commentators glossed the sense: do not lend the denial your aid, your approval, your alliance — support, in the Qur'an's ledger, is not merely conspiracy but assistance's broadest range: the flattering silence, the shared platform, the softened wording. The command's placement after the mercy clause teaches the vow's ground: the Book came as mercy to you; you cannot subcontract its delivery to those it came to rescue. Gratitude, as Moses learned and the Prophet is reminded, is paid in allegiance.

**The Verse's Portrait of the Chosen**

Gather the verse's clauses and a portrait emerges that the tradition has loved: the chosen messenger is unprepared, surprised, recipient rather than author — and thereby unimpeachable. The commentators' wider application reaches every servant carrying anything of God's trust: the qualifications you did not plan are the assignment's signature, and the gratitude they demand is the work's only safe posture. The verse's last command keeps the portrait honest under pressure: the unprepared recipient, once equipped, will spend his life declining the alliances the unprepared never faced. The sūrah's final verses are assembling the Prophet's charge — return promised, allegiance guarded, call commanded — and this verse supplies its engine: the Book was mercy, unlooked for, undeserved, undeniable; the man who holds it has one duty left, and the next verse states it.

**The Support the Command Actually Forbids**

The commentators' precision about the forbidden support deserves its own reading, because the command's edge is broader than betrayal. To be a supporter, in the verse's range, includes the whole gradient of assistance: financing the denial, yes — but also the public platform, the softened testimony, the silence that lets the denial pass unmarked, the company kept for trade's sake when the company's price is the truth's. The Prophet's own biography polices the gradient's far end: he did not merely refuse to fight for the denial — he refused to flatter it, negotiate its terms, or bless its idols with a syllable, and the city's final offers all failed against that perimeter. The verse's teaching for every believer inside a dominant denial-culture is the perimeter's maintenance at one's own scale: the truth's non-negotiability in one's transactions, one's silences, one's signatures. Support, in the Qur'an's ledger, is measured in everything an alliance costs the truth — and the verse draws the ledger's bottom line before any alliance is signed.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:87
> Do not let them turn you away from the revelations of Allah after they have been sent down to you. Rather, invite ˹all˺ to ˹the Way of˺ your Lord, and never be one of the polytheists

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command That Outlines a Whole Ministry**

The verse hands the Prophet his remaining assignment in three strokes: do not be turned away from the signs; call to your Lord; never be of the polytheists. The commentators glossed the first stroke's content: the obstruction to be refused includes the lies, denials, and efforts to harm or shake him from the mission — the full repertoire of a hostile city. The verse's realism is its strength: it assumes the turning pressure exists — the boycott, the mockery, the offered bargains — and commands the standing refusal. The Prophet's biography is the command's case study: offered wealth, kingship, and the city's crown in exchange for silence, he answered with the reply that outlived the offerers; obstructed in market and assembly, he kept reciting; driven out, he kept the call alive from the next city. No life has ever demonstrated the verse's three strokes at fuller length, and the demonstration is the verse's own product.

**The Tradition's Two Audiences**

The commentators preserved a widening of the verse's address: some said it was revealed when the Quraysh tried to induce the Prophet to praise or magnify their gods — the compromise that would have dissolved the message into their shrine — while others held the real audience was the believers as a whole. The widening matters: whatever the first occasion, the command's grammar outgrew it, because the pressure it names is permanent. Every generation of believers inherits the same three-part charge — hold the delivered signs against the culture's pull, keep calling, keep the creed clean — because every generation hosts its own bargaining committee, its own soft demand that the recitation be slightly less recited. The verse's transferability is the Qur'an's design: instructions addressed to the Prophet at maximum pressure become the community's standing job description at every pressure after.

**"Call to Your Lord"**

The middle stroke is the mission's positive content, and its phrasing deserves the weight: call to your Lord — not call about your Lord, not argue for your Lord, but summon, address, extend the invitation whose whole logic is hospitality. The commentators placed the command among the Qur'an's standing commissions of the daʿwah: invite with wisdom and beautiful preaching, argue in the better way — the call's register fixed as invitation, not siege. The word's direction also disciplines the caller: the invitation travels to your Lord — the caller does not own the destination, only the asking. The verse thus defines evangelism in this Book as the opposite of conquest: a hand extended toward a door the Caller has named, held open for as long as the refusing lasts. The Prophet's practice set the standard: he called the same city for thirteen years, without armies, until the city's response exiled him — and the calling resumed from the next ridge, because the command had no pause button.

**Never of the Polytheists**

The third stroke guards the caller's own creed: never be of the polytheists. The commentators read it as the mission's perimeter — the caller must remain distinguishable from the called, or the invitation dissolves. The placement matters: the warning follows the pressure verses because association is pressure's preferred exit — the slow dissolution of a messenger into his audience's practices, one accommodation at a time, until the difference is a vocabulary. The Qur'an's portrait of its prophets is the standing refusal: Noah distinct from his people's mockery, Abraham distinct from his father's forge, Moses distinct from the court that raised him — and the Prophet commanded, at the verse's position, to hold the same line inside his own kinship. The verse's teaching for every caller since is the perimeter's maintenance: the invitation's power lives in the inviter's difference, and the difference is guarded by creed, not costume.

**The Verse Before the Closing Creed**

The verse's placement in the sūrah's last movement shows its function: the Prophet has been given the promise of return (85), the ownership check on the Book (86), and now the ministry's permanent orders (87) — before the chapter closes on the purest creed verse (88). The sequence is a commissioning in miniature: logistics, legitimacy, duty, and then the doctrine that fuels the whole. The commentators' summary carries the arrangement's logic: a messenger secured in his return, clear about his Book's authorship, and ordered into the call, requires one final sentence to hold it all — there is no god but Him — and the next verse supplies it. The reader standing here, at the chapter's edge, holds the same sequence as a personal commissioning: his own return is promised, his trust is assigned, his call is standing, and the creed is about to be recited one last time — in the sūrah's final verse, at full volume.

**The Call's Persistence as the Verse's Signature**

The verse's middle command — call to your Lord — is written as an unlimited commission, and the Prophet's execution of it set the standard the community has inherited: the call continued through boycott, assassination plots, the stoning at the city whose people rejected him, and the years in which converts could be counted in single digits. The commentators' larger doctrine stands behind the persistence: the messenger's duty is exhaustless invitation, because the invitation's power does not depend on its reception — it depends on its delivery. The command's structure also forbids the caller's two premature exits: quitting when the audience hardens, and fighting when the audience refuses — the first abandons the delivery, the second replaces it. What remains when both exits are closed is the patient, open-handed calling the verse orders — and the sūrah's whole cast testifies to its yield: the crowd at the crater converted by an event; the People of the Book converted by a recitation; the square's wiser cohort standing converted before the miracle came. The call, delivered long enough, collects everyone the message was meant to reach.

---


## Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ 28:88
> And do not invoke any other god with Allah. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. Everything is bound to perish except He Himself. All authority belongs to Him. And to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Last Command**

The chapter closes on the command from which all its stories derived: do not call upon another god with Allah. The command's placement is the summary's logic: eighty-seven verses have displayed what association costs — Egypt's drowned court, Korah's crater, the roll call of absent gods — and the closing verse states the avoidance in one clause. The commentators read the sūrah's whole arc as this command's proof: every ruin in the chapter is the biography of an associate-god's collapse — Pharaoh claimed the throne-room, Korah credited the treasury, the fabricated idols failed their callers — and the verse withdraws the reader's membership from every such failing before it is filed. The last command is thus the first lesson wearing its evidence: one God, called upon alone, held to the chapter's last breath.

**Everything Perishes Except His Face**

The verse's center holds the chapter's heaviest clause: everything perishes except His Face — and the commentators' readings circled its exact sense with care. Some read the Face as God Himself, His Self, the Reality; others as what is turned toward — every object of aspiration other than God coming to nothing; others as the qualifying exception — all things perish except what is done for His countenance, His religion, the deeds by which He is approached. The commentator tradition cited the Prophet's judgment on the poet Labīd — the truest word spoken by an Arab in poetry: Behold! apart from Allah everything is vain (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2255a) — the pre-Islamic verse that arrived, unaided, at the Qur'an's doctrine. The commentators also carried al-Rāzī's garment image: the tattered robe is called perished though its fibers remain — creation's perishing is its removal from service, not its annihilation, as the Qur'an states elsewhere: the earth will be changed into a different earth and the heavens as well (14:48). Every reading converges on the clause's practical force: the perishable cannot be worshipped as if it held, because holding is precisely what it does not do.

**The Creed Verses: First and Last**

The sūrah's closing creed mirrors its mid-point creed of verse 70 — there: His is the praise in the first and the last, His the judgment, to Him the return; here: no god but He, all things perish except His Face, judgment His, to Him the return. The commentators' structural eye catches the design: the chapter is bracketed by confession, the stories held between two statements of the only permanent fact. And the closing verse adds what verse 70 did not carry: the perishing clause — the fact that upgrades the creed from sovereignty to survival. Verse 70 said who rules; verse 88 says what remains. Together they are the complete answer to the sūrah's cast: Pharaoh and Korah believed in rule without survival — power that would keep; the believer is taught survival without rival — the One who remains when everything else has been recalled. The reader exits the chapter through this door: having watched every rival's estate close, he recites the only opening balance that persists.

**Judgment His, Return to Him**

The closing clauses repeat the standing terms of governance: judgment belongs to Him, and to Him you will be returned — the same pair that sealed verse 70, now sealing the chapter. The commentators noted the pairing's completeness: authority in the present, assembly at the end — sovereignty running through every interim and resuming fully at the terminus. The repetition inside one sūrah is deliberate pedagogy: the two facts a listener must carry out of every recitation are that the judgment is not his and the return is not optional. The chapter's cast demonstrates both: Pharoah judged himself a god and was judged; Korah ruled his fortune and was recalled; the mother entrusted her child and was repaid in person. The verse hands its reader the same ledger with the terms legible: rule nothing, contest nothing, and arrive — because the arriving is certain and the Judge is the One the whole chapter has been introducing.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Sealed Door**

Read as the chapter's last line, the verse gathers the sūrah's whole flight path into a single descending curve: the letters at the opening, the basket on the river, the palace and the well, the mountain and the court, the sea, the craters, the promises — all of it conducted by the One the verse finally names without narrative: there is no god but Him. The commentators' method of reading final verses as seals finds here its cleanest instance: the story's last word is the story's first cause, stated in the present tense, with the reader standing inside its jurisdiction. Everything in the chapter that was trusted — the river, the palace, the flocks, the treasure — has been shown perishing; everything that remained was the Face's doing; and the verse sends the reader home with the summary the home itself will one day confirm: He remains, He judges, He receives. The sūrah that opened with three severed letters closes with the One who cannot be severed — and between those two facts, a reader has been given a world.

**The Chapter's Farewell in One Verse**

Read the closing verse as the sūrah's hand on the reader's shoulder, and each clause is a farewell gift in order: the command against association — the one error every story warned about; the creed of oneness — the fact every story demonstrated; the perishing clause — the sentence every rival's estate just served; the judgment clause — the court every scene anticipated; and the return — the road every prophet in the chapter walked, from Joseph's brothers to Moses' flight to the Prophet's own promised homecoming. The commentators' practice of ending recitation on this verse gives the exit its liturgy: the listener leaves the chapter carrying five clauses that outlast the chapter, each one load-bearing for the week ahead — what not to call upon, who alone is real, what will not hold, who decides, and where the road ends. A chapter that began with severed letters ends with the unsevered Face; the reader who has traveled its distance can feel why the Book saved this verse for last: it is not the chapter's conclusion — it is the chapter's confirmation, signed by the only Signatory the verses left standing.

---
